Comparing cluster algebras on braid varieties
Roger Casals, Pavel Galashin, Mikhail Gorsky, Linhui Shen, Melissa Sherman-Bennett, José Simental

Abstract
. Braid varieties parametrize linear configurations of flags with transversality conditions dictated by positive braids. They include and generalize reduced double Bruhat cells, positroid varieties, open Bott-Samelson varieties, and Richardson varieties, among others. Recently, two cluster algebra structures were independently constructed in the coordinate rings of braid varieties: one using weaves and the other using Deodhar geometry. The main result of the article is that these two cluster algebras coincide. More generally, our comparative study matches the different concepts and results from each approach to the other, both on the combinatorial and algebraic geometric aspects.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 13F60, 14M15. Secondary: 05E99.

1.
Introduction

The coordinate rings of braid varieties have recently been proven to be cluster algebras. Two independent constructions appeared: using weaves and using Deodhar geometry. The object of this article is to compare these two cluster algebras. The main contribution of the manuscript is that, appropriately understood, these two cluster algebras coincide.

En route, the article provides a detailed comparative study of each of the two constructions, explaining how to develop the necessary concepts and results on each side through the lens of the other. On the geometric side, this includes building explicit isomorphisms between braid varieties and double braid varieties, between weave tori and Deodhar tori, comparing the weave 2-form and the Deodhar 2-form, and the weave and Deodhar cluster variables. On the combinatorial side, this includes matching the combinatorics of double braid words with the combinatorics of double strings and double inductive weaves, and the orders of vanishing of certain chamber minors on Deodhar hypersurfaces with the tropical Lusztig rules on weaves.

1.1.
Scientific context

Recently, the two articles
[
CGG+25
]
and
[
GLSB23
]
have shown that the coordinate rings of braid varieties are cluster algebras for any simple Lie group type
\mathsf{G}
. Braid varieties were introduced in
[
CGGS24
]
, and note that
[
GLSB23
]
was preceded by
[
GLSBS22
]
, which focused on the case
\mathsf{G}=\operatorname{SL}
and 3D plabic graphs. In brief, braid varieties are irreducible smooth affine varieties that parametrize linear configurations of flags with transversality conditions dictated by a positive braid. They generalize known classes of varieties appearing in Lie theory and algebraic combinatorics, such as positroid and Richardson varieties, and appear prominently in low-dimensional contact and symplectic topology, cf. e.g.
[
CG22
,
CG24
,
Cas25
]
. Succinctly, the two approaches of
[
CGG+25
]
and
[
GLSB23
]
can be described as follows:

(1)

The main result of
[
CGG+25
]
is the construction of a cluster algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
, where
X(\beta)
is the braid variety associated to a positive braid word
\beta
. The key technique in
[
CGG+25
]
is weave calculus, as introduced in
[
CZ22
]
, and see also
[
CGGS24
]
. At core, a weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
is a combinatorial object, given by a diagram in the plane, which encodes a way to relate two braids words
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
via braid moves and the moves
\sigma_{i}^{2}\to\sigma_{i}
, where
\sigma_{i}
is an Artin generator for the braid group. The cluster algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
built in
[
CGG+25
]
is such that a weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
from a braid
\beta
to its Demazure product
\delta(\beta)
defines a cluster seed on
\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
. Specifically, such a weave defines a weave torus
T_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset X(\beta)
, a weave quiver
Q_{\mathfrak{w}}
, and a corresponding set of cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}}\in\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
.

(2)

The main result of
[
GLSB23
]
is the construction of a cluster algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
, where
R(\bbbeta)
is the double braid variety associated to a double braid word
\bbbeta
. The key technique in
[
GLSB23
]
is using a generalization of the Deodhar decomposition of Richardson varieties and certain rational expressions of generalized minors. The cluster algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
built in
[
GLSB23
]
is such that the double braid word
\bbbeta
itself defines a cluster seed on
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
. Specifically, it defines a Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subset R(\bbbeta)
, a Deodhar quiver
Q_{\bbbeta}
, and a corresponding set of cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}\in\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
.

Each technique has its strengths and shortcomings. By developing a detailed comparison between these two approaches, as we do in this manuscript, the advantages of each technique explicitly translate to the other. For instance, the propagation algorithm for weaves, which naturally follows the tropical Lusztig rules, can now be also implemented as a propagation algorithm for the Deodhar cluster variables. We have summarized some of the key ingredients and results of our comparative study in Tables
1
and
2
below.

1.2.
Main result

The combinatorial and algebraic geometric objects appearing in
[
CGG+25
]
and
[
GLSB23
]
, the two works we are comparing, are qualitatively different. To wit, we summarize the key aspects of each work as follows. In
[
CGG+25
]
, braid varieties
X(\beta)
are associated to a braid word
\beta
. Given a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
, three objects are constructed:

(1)

the weave torus
T_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset X(\beta)
, via the flag transversality conditions imposed by
\mathfrak{w}
,

(2)

the weave cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}}\in\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
, measuring transversality according to
\mathfrak{w}
,

(3)

the weave quiver
Q^{\rm W}
, equivalently encoded in the weave 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}\in\Omega^{2}X(\beta)
.

The cluster algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
constructed in
[
CGG+25
]
is such that the triple
(T_{\mathfrak{w}},{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}},\Omega^{\operatorname{W}})
associated to a given Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
is a cluster seed for
\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
. Non-equivalent Demazure weaves yield different cluster seeds. We refer to this cluster algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
, built in
[
CGG+25
]
, as the
weave cluster algebra
. In essence, the weave cluster algebra uses the combinatorics of weaves as a way to govern transversality conditions of tuples of flags, and each cluster seed is itself named in terms of a weave, via the tropical combinatorics of its Lusztig cycles.

In
[
GLSB23
]
double braid varieties
R(\bbbeta)
are associated to double braid words
\bbbeta
. From a given double braid word
\bbbeta
, three objects are constructed:

(1)

the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subset R(\bbbeta)
, with cocharacter lattice, via certain chamber minors,

(2)

the Deodhar cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}\in\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
, via the vanishing of certain characters,

(3)

the Deodhar quiver
Q^{\rm D}
, equivalently encoded in the Deodhar 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}\in\Omega^{2}R(\bbbeta)
.

The cluster algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
constructed in
[
GLSB23
]
is such that the triple
(T_{\bbbeta},{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}},\Omega^{\operatorname{D}})
associated to a given double braid word
\bbbeta
is a cluster seed for
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
. We refer to this cluster algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
, built in
[
GLSB23
]
, as the
Deodhar cluster algebra
. In a nutshell, the Deodhar cluster algebra uses a Deodhar-type decomposition of the double braid variety, whose strata are dictated by the flag relations as imposed by
\bbbeta
. Each cluster seed is itself named in terms of certain unique characters determined by their vanishing along codimension-1 strata: intuitively, for each codimension-1 stratum, a cluster variable is named as the unique character on the Deodhar torus solely vanishing along that hypersurface.

The core of this article is a detailed comparison of the weave and Deodhar cluster algebras. It occurs in two different layers, the combinatoral comparison and the geometric comparison:

(i)

Combinatorially, we explain how to transition from a double braid word
\bbbeta
to a certain braid word
\beta=\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
, establish a dictionary between double braid words
\bbbeta
, double strings
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
and double inductive weaves
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
, and relate the tropical Lusztig propagation rules for weaves to a type of propagation of Deodhar cluster variables in chamber minors.

(ii)

Geometrically, we establish isomorphisms between double braid varieties and braid varieties, and show that they map weave tori to Deodhar tori, weave cluster variables to Deodhar cluster variables and the weave 2-form to the Deodhar 2-form. See
Table

2
.

The qualitatively different nature of these two constructions, one based on the combinatorics of weaves and the other on properties of chamber minors and character lattices, leads to a comparison with substance. For instance, we prove a new formula expressing the Cartan element
h^{+}_{c}
in
[
GLSB23
]
in terms of the
u
-variables for an edge labeling of a weave. While both
h^{+}_{c}
and the
u
-variables are crucial in defining the respective cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
and
{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}}
, such formula is of interest on its own. Another instance, the tropical Lusztig propagation rules, translated through our comparison, lead to a new propagating algorithm to compute the Deodhar cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
, a desired procedure that was missing in
[
GLSB23
]
.

In summary, the main result of this article is that the weave and Deodhard cluster algebras are isomorphic. There are also new contributions in the techniques and results we develop to establish such isomorphism, which are provided throughout the article, cf. e.g.
Table

2
. For reference, we state the main contribution, again emphasizing that the ingredients developed for its proof are by themselves new and of interest:

Main Theorem

(
Comparison of weave and Deodhar cluster algebras
)
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word. Then, there exists an isomorphism of algebraic varieties

\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\xrightarrow{\sim}X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})

such that the following equalities hold:

(1)

Equality of tori:
\varphi(T_{\bbbeta})=T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta))}
,

(2)

Equality of cluster variables:
\varphi^{\ast}{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}}={\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
,

(3)

Equality of 2-forms:
\varphi^{\ast}\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}=\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}
.

This implies that the weave cluster algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)}]
is isomorphic to the Deodhar cluster algebra structure on
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
, via the isomorphism
\varphi^{\ast}
.

In the statement above,
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
denotes a specific braid word associated to the given double braid word
\bbbeta
, and
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta))
denotes the double inductive weave of the double string associated to the double braid word
\bbbeta
.
Table

2
summarizes where these objects and constructions between them are established in the article.

Beyond the
Main Theorem
above, the manuscript contains a number of additional details and comparisons of independent value. To wit,
Section

6.1
provides the combinatorial relation between the 3D plabic graphs from
[
GLSBS22
]
and weaves for Lie Type
A
, e.g.
\mathsf{G}=\operatorname{SL}
, and
Section

6.2
develops the relation between monotone multicurves, which are associated to 3D plabic graphs, and the tropical rules for Lusztig cycle propagation in weaves. In particular,
Section

6.2.2
contains an interesting connection between the behavior of certain cocharacters under the propagation rules and string operators. Appendices B and C contain two more comparisons: between the double braid moves for double braid words from
[
GLSB23
]
and the double string moves for double inductive weaves from
[
CGG+25
]
, cf.
Table

5
, and between constructions of cluster algebra structures in the coordinate rings of open Richardson varieties, cf.
9.3
.

2.
Comparison of algebraic varieties

This section focuses on comparing the algebraic varieties whose coordinate rings are shown to be cluster algebras in
[
CGG+25
,
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
:

(1)

In
[
CGG+25
]
, one considers braid varieties
X(\beta)
, associated to a braid word
\beta
;

(2)

In
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
one considers double braid varieties
R(\bbbeta)
, associated to a double braid word
\bbbeta
.

These varieties
X(\beta)
and
R(\bbbeta)
are introduced in
Section

2.2
, after describing their combinatorial inputs
\beta
and
\bbbeta
in
Section

2.1
, and their comparison is explained in
Section

2.3
.

2.1.
Braid words and double braid words

In
[
CGG+25
]
, braid words
\beta
use the alphabet
I
, whereas
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
employ braid words
\bbbeta
in the alphabet
\pm I
. The latter type of words will be referred as double braid words and the former simply as braid words, to mark the difference. The first transitional step in comparing these approaches is the comparison of such braid words. Starting with
\bbbeta
, as used in
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
, the corresponding braid word
\beta
, as used in
[
CGG+25
]
, is described as follows:

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\bbbeta=i_{1}\cdots i_{n+m}
be a double braid word in the alphabet
\pm I
and consider

J_{-}=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{n}\}\subseteq[n+m],\quad J_{+}=\{b_{1}<b_{2}<\cdots<b_{m}\}\subseteq[n+m],

the sets of indices of the negative and positive letters of
\bbbeta
, respectively. By definition, the braid word
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
associated to
\bbbeta
is

(2.1)

\bbbeta^{(-|+)}:=(-i_{a_{1}}^{\ast})\cdots(-i_{a_{n}}^{\ast})i_{b_{m}}\cdots i_{b_{1}}.

By construction,
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
is a braid word in the alphabet
I
.
\Box

In
Definition

2.1
, we denoted by
-^{\ast}:I\to I
the map defined by the property that conjugating by
w_{\circ}
sends
s_{i}
to
s_{i^{*}}
, and set
(-i)^{\ast}:=-i^{\ast}
. A braid word
\beta=i_{1}\cdots i_{l}
defines a word
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{l}}
in the generators of
W
. The
Demazure product

\delta(\beta)
of
\beta
is the maximal element
w\in W
with respect to the Bruhat order such that
s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{l}}
contains a reduced expression of
w
as a (not necessarily consecutive) subexpression. The Demazure product can be equivalently defined inductively by the following rule:

(2.2)

\delta(i):=s_{i},\ \delta(\beta\cdot i):=\begin{cases}\delta(\beta)s_{i}&\text{if}\ \ell(\delta(\beta)s_{i})=\ell(\delta(\beta))+1\\
\delta(\beta)&\text{if}\ \ell(\delta(\beta)s_{i})=\ell(\delta(\beta))-1.\\
\end{cases}

We will also write
s_{i}\ast s_{j}
for the Demazure product
\delta(i\cdot j)
. We define the Demazure product of a double braid word as follows.

Definition 2.2
.

For
i\in\pm I
, let

s_{i}^{+}:=\begin{cases}s_{i}&\text{if }i\in I\\
\mathrm{id}&\text{if }i\in-I\end{cases}\qquad s_{i}^{-}:=\begin{cases}\mathrm{id}&\text{if }i\in I\\
s_{|i|}&\text{if }i\in-I\end{cases}.

By definition, the Demazure product of a double braid word
\bbbeta=i_{1}\cdots i_{l}
is

\delta(\bbbeta):=s_{i_{1}^{*}}^{-}*\cdots*s_{i_{l}^{*}}^{-}*s_{i_{l}}^{+}*\cdots*s_{i_{1}}^{+}

where
*
denotes the usual Demazure product.
∎

Note that
\delta(\bbbeta)=\delta(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
, where the left hand side of the equality is
Definition

2.2
and the right hand side is the standard Demazure product from
Equation

2.2
.
Figure

1
illustrates diagrams for (double) braid words in the case of Type A, i.e.
\mathsf{G}=\operatorname{SL}
, where the number of strands is the rank of
\mathsf{G}
plus one. In such braid diagrams, the strands are numbered increasingly starting from 1 at the highest strand, both for the strands at the bottom and at the top blocks. Note that a
w_{\circ}
which is not part of
\bbbeta,\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
is inserted in this diagram. The presence of
w_{\circ}
is the reason for the appearance of the involution
*
in the lower indices
s_{i^{*}_{a_{j}}}
,
j\in[1,n]
, and it is needed in order to introduce the different variants of braid varieties.

Figure 1
.
In Type A,
\mathsf{G}=\operatorname{SL}
, the braid words in
Definition

2.1
can be interpreted as geometric braids that close up to Legendrian links, whose fronts are depicted here. (Left) The front braid diagram associated to
\bbbeta
. (Right) The front associated to
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
. The transition from left to right is given by passing, using Reidemeister moves, the two top boxes in the left diagram to the bottom through the
right
side, as indicated by the red arrow. This geometric transport explains why the word
s_{i_{b_{1}}}\ldots s_{i_{b_{m}}}
at the top of the left diagram becomes
s_{i_{b_{m}}}\ldots s_{i_{b_{1}}}
at the bottom of the right diagram. Here
w_{0}
indicates a braid word for
w_{\circ}
, the appearance on the right should be understood as also reflecting (reading right-to-left) the choice of braid word for
w_{\circ}
on the left.

Example 2.3
.

Let
\mathsf{G}=\operatorname{SL}_{3}
, so that
I=\{1,2\}
, and
\bbbeta=(-2,1,2,1,-1,1,2)
. Then
J_{-}=\{1,5\}
,
J_{+}=\{2,3,4,6,7\}
and
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}=(2^{*},1^{*},2,1,1,2,1)=(1,2,2,1,1,2,1)
. The associated braid diagram is depicted in
Figure

2
. This is the running example in this manuscript.
\Box

Figure 2
.
The braid diagram associated to the braid word
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
in
Example

2.3
. Here crossings are all understood as positive Artin generators, i.e. overcrossings. Smoothly, the resulting link is a 3-component link.

2.2.
Braid varieties and double braid varieties

Let us introduce braid varieties and double braid varieties in Definitions
2.4
and
2.6
below. The necessary preliminaries on flags, weighted flags, and their relative positions are summarized in Appendix A. The braid varieties studied in
[
CGG+25
]
are defined as follows:

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\beta=i_{1}\cdots i_{l}
be a braid word with the Demazure product
\delta(\beta)=w_{\circ}\in W
. The
braid variety

X(\beta)
associated to
\beta
is

X(\beta):=\{(\mathsf{B}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{l})\in(\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B})^{l+1}\mid\mathsf{B}_{0}=\mathsf{B},\mathsf{B}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{s_{i_{k+1}}}}\mathsf{B}_{k+1},\mathsf{B}_{l}=w_{\circ}\mathsf{B}\},

That is,
X(\beta)
parametrizes tuples of flags
\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}=(\mathsf{B}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{l})
satisfying the relative positions

(2.3)

\mathsf{B}_{+}=\mathsf{B}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{1}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{l}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{l}=w_{\circ}\mathsf{B}_{+},

dictated by
\beta
and the boundary constraints
\mathsf{B}_{+}=\mathsf{B}_{0}
and
\mathsf{B}_{l}=w_{\circ}\mathsf{B}_{+}
.
\Box

It follows from
[
CGG+25
, Corollary 3.7]
that
X(\beta)
is a smooth irreducible affine variety of dimension
\ell(\beta)-\ell(w_{\circ})
. The isomorphism type of
X(\beta)
as an affine variety only depends on the braid
[\beta]
: it is independent of the particular braid word
\beta
for
[\beta]
. Precisely, if
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
are related by a braid move, there exists an explicit isomorphism, which depends on the braid move, between
X(\beta)
and
X(\beta^{\prime})
, cf.
[
CGG+25
, Section 3]
.

Remark 2.5
.

In
[
CGG+25
]
, braid varieties are defined more generally without the assumption that
\delta(\beta)=w_{\circ}
. However, by
[
CGG+25
, Lemma 3.4 (1)]
, we can consider the case
\delta(\beta)=w_{\circ}
without loss of generality.∎

There is an equivalent description of
X(\beta)
in terms of weighted flags, cf.
[
CGG+25
, Lemma 3.13]
. This description is employed in Section
4
and also brings
X(\beta)
closer to double braid varieties, which are defined in terms of weighted flags. Specifically, consider the algebraic variety
X_{\mathsf{U}}(\beta)
defined by

(2.4)

X_{\mathsf{U}}(\beta):=\{(\mathsf{U}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{U}_{l})\in(\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U})^{l+1}\mid\mathsf{U}_{0}=\mathsf{U}_{+},\mathsf{U}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{1}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{U}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{l}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{U}_{l},\pi(\mathsf{U}_{l})=w_{\circ}B_{+}\},

where
\mathsf{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{U}^{\prime}
denotes strong relative position
w
between weighted flags, cf.
Section

7
. Then, the braid variety
X(\beta)
is isomorphic to
X_{\mathsf{U}}(\beta)
:

(2.5)

X(\beta)\cong X_{\mathsf{U}}(\beta).

Thus, under this isomorphism, the braid variety
X(\beta)
also parametrizes tuples of
weighted
flags
\mathsf{U}_{\bullet}=(\mathsf{U}_{0},\mathsf{U}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{U}_{l})
satisfying:

(2.6)

\mathsf{U}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{1}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{U}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{l}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{U}_{l}

and the boundary conditions
\mathsf{U}_{0}=\mathsf{U}_{+}
and
\pi(\mathsf{U}_{l})=w_{\circ}B_{+}
. By
[
CGG+25
, Lemma 3.13]
, an explicit isomorphism witnessing
Equation

2.5
is given by projecting each such tuple
\mathsf{U}_{\bullet}
of weighted flags to the tuple
\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}
of underlying flags in
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}
. In short,
Equation

2.5
states that we can lift the first flag of a point in
X(\beta)
to be the weighted flag
\mathsf{U}_{+}
and that this lift propagates to the right in a unique manner, reading
\beta
left-to-right. That is, once
\mathsf{B}_{+}
is lifted to
\mathsf{U}_{+}
we can continue lifting each flag
\mathsf{B}_{i}
to a weighted flag
\mathsf{U}_{i}
while preserving the relative positions.

The double braid variety studied in
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
is defined as follows.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\bbbeta=i_{1}\cdots i_{l}
be a double braid word with Demazure product
\delta(\bbbeta)=w_{\circ}\in W
. The
double braid variety

R(\bbbeta)
associated to
\bbbeta
is the variety of pairs of weighted flags
\{((X_{0},Y_{0}),\ldots,(X_{l},Y_{l}))\}\subset(\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U}\times\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U})^{l}
, satisfying the following relative position conditions

(2.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{l}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{1}}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{2}}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{l}}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{1}^{\ast}}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{2}^{\ast}}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{l}^{\ast}}^{-}}

modulo the simultaneous action of
\mathsf{G}
on the left.
\Box

It follows from
[
GLSB23
, Section 4]
that double braid moves applied to
\bbbeta
preserve the isomorphism type of the double braid variety
R(\bbbeta)
, see loc. cit. for the definition of these double braid moves, see also
Remark

2.10
. That is, if
\bbbeta
and
\bbbeta^{\prime}
are related by a double braid move, then there exists an explicit isomorphism, depending on the braid move, between
R(\bbbeta)
and
R(\bbbeta^{\prime})
. In particular, there is an isomorphism
R(\bbbeta)\cong R(\bbbeta^{\prime})
if
\bbbeta
and
\bbbeta^{\prime}
are related by commuting a letter in
I
with a letter in
-I
. Therefore, moving all the letters in
-I
to the left and all the letters in
I
to the right, we have that every double braid variety
R(\bbbeta)
is naturally isomorphic to a double braid variety of the form
R(\bbbeta^{\prime})
where
\bbbeta^{\prime}=(-i_{1})\cdots(-i_{m})j_{1}\cdots j_{n}
, with
l=m+n
and
i_{1},\dots,i_{m},j_{1},\dots,j_{n}\in I
. See
Figure

1
for a depiction of this.

Remark 2.7
.

The Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subseteq R(\bbbeta)
is an open torus chart that has a central role in construction of the cluster structures in
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
. It is defined in
[
GLSB23
, Definition 2.5]
, cf. also
Section

3.4
below. Such torus
T_{\bbbeta}
depends crucially on the double braid word
\bbbeta
in the following sense: if
\bbbeta
and
\bbbeta^{\prime}
are related by a double braid move and
\psi:R(\bbbeta)\longrightarrow R(\bbbeta^{\prime})
is the isomorphism associated to such double braid move, then
\psi(T_{\bbbeta})
is typically not the same as
T_{\bbbeta^{\prime}}
.
\Box

Note that the symbols
s^{\pm}_{i}
in
Definition

2.2
are allowed to be the identity
\mathrm{id}\in W(\mathsf{G})
in some cases. More precisely, in (
2.7
) we have
Y_{j-1}=Y_{j}
, resp.
X_{j-1}=X_{j}
, if
i_{j}\in I
, resp.
i_{j}\in-I
. In contrast, in
[
CGG+25
]
no consecutive flags in a point of
X(\beta)
are allowed to be equal. This is a technical difference between
Definition

2.4
and
Definition

2.6
.

2.3.
An isomorphism between
X(\beta)
and
R(\bbbeta)

By
Definition

2.1
, each double braid word
\bbbeta
defines a braid word
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
. In this subsection, we show that the varieties
R(\bbbeta)
and
X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
are isomorphic. Intuitively, this isomorphism is obtained by reading
Equation

2.7
counter-clockwise and starting at
Y_{0}
, while ignoring any arrows that are the identity. Specifically, the isomorphism of varieties is given as follows:

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
\bbbeta=i_{1}\cdots i_{n+m}
be a double braid word with
J_{-}=\{a_{1}<\cdots<a_{n}\}
and
J_{+}=\{b_{1}<\cdots<b_{m}\}
. Given a
\mathsf{G}
-orbit
\mathsf{G}.(X_{\bullet}^{\prime},Y_{\bullet}^{\prime})\in R(\bbbeta)
, let
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})
be the unique representative such that
Y_{0}=\mathsf{U}_{+}
and
\pi(X_{0})=w_{\circ}\mathsf{B}_{+}
. Consider the map

(2.8)

\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\longrightarrow X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)}),\quad\mathsf{G}.(X_{\bullet}^{\prime},Y_{\bullet}^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\longmapsto}}(\mathsf{B}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{n+m})\in X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)}),

where the flags
\mathsf{B}_{i}
,
i\in[0,n+m]
are defined by

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{B}_{0}=\pi(Y_{0})=\mathsf{B}_{+},\mathsf{B}_{1}=\pi(Y_{a_{1}}),\dots,\mathsf{B}_{n}=\pi(Y_{a_{n}}),\\
\mathsf{B}_{n+1}=\pi(X_{b_{m}-1}),\mathsf{B}_{n+2}=\pi(X_{b_{m-1}-1}),\dots,\mathsf{B}_{n+m}=\pi(X_{0})=w_{\circ}\mathsf{B}_{+}.\end{array}

Then the map
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\longrightarrow X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
is an isomorphism of algebraic varieties.

Proof.

Consider the map

\varphi^{\prime}:R(\bbbeta)\to X_{\mathsf{U}}(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})\qquad\varphi:\mathsf{G}.(X_{\bullet}^{\prime},Y_{\bullet}^{\prime})\mapsto(Y_{0},Y_{a_{1}},\ldots,Y_{a_{n}},X_{b_{m}-1},X_{b_{m-1}-1},\ldots,X_{0}),

where
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})
is the unique representative of
\mathsf{G}.(X_{\bullet}^{\prime},Y_{\bullet}^{\prime})
with
Y_{0}=\mathsf{U}_{+}
and
\pi(X_{0})=w_{\circ}\mathsf{B}_{+}
, as in the statement.
The morphism
\varphi
is
\varphi^{\prime}
composed with the projection
\pi:\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U}\longrightarrow\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}
applied to each flag. Since
[
CGG+25
, Lemma 3.13]
implies that such projection
\pi
induces an isomorphism between
X_{\mathsf{U}}(\beta)
and
X(\beta)
, as in
Equation

2.5
above, it suffices to show that the morphism
\varphi^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.

Note that
\varphi^{\prime}
is well-defined, as the existence of the special representative
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})
of
\mathsf{G}.(X_{\bullet}^{\prime},Y_{\bullet}^{\prime})
follows from the condition that
Y_{0}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{\circ}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}X_{0}^{\prime}
, and its uniqueness follows from the fact that
\mathsf{G}
acts freely on such pairs
(Y_{0}^{\prime},X_{0}^{\prime})
. Once this representative is fixed, well-definedness of
\varphi^{\prime}
follows from the definition of
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
. Indeed, the
linear sequence of relative positions in the definition of
X_{\mathsf{U}}(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
coincides with the sequence of relative positions in
Equation

2.7
describing
R(\bbbeta)
, where we read counterclockwise starting at
Y_{0}
and ignore instances of
s^{\pm}_{i}=\mbox{id}
. The inverse map
(\varphi^{\prime})^{-1}
is constructed by repeating weighted flags for every instance of
s^{\pm}_{i}=\mbox{id}
and then taking the
\mathsf{G}
-orbit: it follows that
\varphi^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.
∎

The isomorphism
\varphi
in
Proposition

2.8
is essentially given by reflecting (
2.7
) across the
x=y
line and reading (the projections of) the flags clockwise starting at
Y_{0}=U_{+}
, contracting all arrows labeled with the identity in (
2.7
).
