Remark 2.10
.

The isomorphism
\varphi
is compatible with certain types of double braid moves:

(i)

First, assume that
\bbbeta=\bbbeta_{1}ij\bbbeta_{2}
and
\bbbeta^{\prime}=\bbbeta_{1}ji\bbbeta_{2}
where
i
and
j
have different signs. This is move (B1) in
[
GLSB23
, Section 4]
. Then there is a natural isomorphism
\phi_{\bbbeta,\bbbeta^{\prime}}:R(\bbbeta)\to R(\bbbeta^{\prime})
. If
i
is the
k
-th letter of
\bbbeta
, this isomorphism is given by
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})\mapsto(X^{\prime}_{\bullet},Y^{\prime}_{\bullet})
where

(X^{\prime}_{p},Y^{\prime}_{p})=\begin{cases}(X_{p},Y_{p})&\text{if}\;p\neq k,k+1\\
(X_{k+1},Y_{k+1})&\text{if}\;p=k\\
(X_{k},Y_{k})&\text{if}\;p=k+1.\end{cases}

Note that
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}=(\bbbeta^{\prime})^{(-|+)}
. Then the following diagram commutes

(2.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\bbbeta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\bbbeta^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\bbbeta,\bbbeta^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\bbbeta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\bbbeta^{\prime}}}

(ii)

Assume that
\bbbeta=\bbbeta_{1}i
and
\bbbeta^{\prime}=\bbbeta_{1}(-i)^{\ast}
. This is move (B4) in
[
GLSB23
, Section 4]
. Then there is a natural isomorphism
\phi_{\bbbeta,\bbbeta^{\prime}}:R(\bbbeta)\to R(\bbbeta^{\prime})
, described in
[
GLSB23
, Section 4.6]
. As in (i) above, we have that
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}=(\bbbeta^{\prime})^{(-|+)}
and a diagram similar to (
2.10
) commutes.∎

2.4.
The commutative algebras

A central goal in both
[
CGG+25
]
and
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
is the construction of cluster algebras. Specifically, the two commutative algebras that are shown to admit a cluster structure are the rings of regular functions
\mathbb{Z}[X(\beta)]
and
\mathbb{Z}[R(\bbbeta)]
.

By construction,
X(\beta)
is an affine variety over
\mathbb{Z}
and the weave methods are defined and applied over
\mathbb{Z}
. Such integrality in the case of
R(\bbbeta)
can also be deduced from
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
, even if less explicitly stated there. For simplicity, we focus on the base change to the ground field
k=\mathbb{C}
and work with the commutative algebras
\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
and
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
.

(i)
Braid varieties
. In the case of
X(\beta)
,
Definition

2.4
gives a presentation of equations and inequalities cutting out
X(\beta)
as a quasi-affine scheme in the projective variety
(\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B})^{l+1}
. In fact,
[
CGG+25
, Proposition 3.6]
implies that the space of sequences of flags
\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}=(\mathsf{B}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{l})
satisfying the relative position conditions
\mathsf{B}_{+}=\mathsf{B}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{1}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{l}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{l}
is isomorphic to the affine space
\mathbb{C}^{l}
, and thus the braid variety
X(\beta)
can be described as the closed subvariety of
\mathbb{C}^{l}
cut out by the condition
\mathsf{B}_{l}=w_{\circ}\mathsf{B}_{+}
. With this approach, the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
can be presented rather explicitly as a quotient of
\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}]
by using braid matrices, as explained in
[
CGG+25
, Corollary 3.7]
and as we now recall.

Fix a pinning
(H,\mathsf{B}_{+},\mathsf{B}_{-},x_{i},y_{i};i\in I)
of
\mathsf{G}
, cf.
Section

7.1
. For
i\in I
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
we set

B_{i}(z):=\phi_{i}\begin{pmatrix}z&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}=x_{i}(z)\dot{s}_{i}.

Then
[
CGG+25
, Corollary 3.7]
implies that a set of equations for
X(\beta)
in affine space is:

(2.11)

X(\beta)\cong\{(z_{1},\dots,z_{l})\in\mathbb{C}^{l}\mid w_{\circ}B_{i_{1}}(z_{1})\cdots B_{i_{l}}(z_{l})\in\mathsf{B}\},

where
w_{\circ}
is understood as a lift of the permutation
w_{\circ}
.

Example 2.11
.

Consider the braid word
\beta=1221121
, as in
Example

2.3
, and the following elements
B_{1}(z),B_{2}(z)
of
\mathsf{G}=\operatorname{SL}_{3}
:

B_{1}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}z&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad B_{2}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&z&-1\\
0&1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Denote
B_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\beta}}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{7}):=B_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}(z_{1})B_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}}(z_{2})B_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}}(z_{3})B_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}(z_{4})B_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}(z_{5})B_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}}(z_{6})B_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}}(z_{7})
, where we have highlighted in
blue
the dependence on the braid word
\beta
. These are the braid matrices introduced in
[
CGG+25
, Section 3.4]
to coordinatize
X(\beta)
. Then
X(\beta)
is isomorphic to the closed subvariety of
\mathbb{C}^{7}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{7}]
cut out by the condition

(2.12)

w_{\circ}B_{\beta}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{7})\in\mathsf{B}_{+},

where
w_{\circ}
is understood as a permutation matrix lifting
w_{\circ}\in S_{n}
. The three diagonal conditions in
Equation

2.12
are non-equalities, cutting out an open subset, whereas the vanishing of the entries
(2,1),(3,1)
and
(3,2)
of
w_{\circ}B_{\beta}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{7})
impose three independent conditions. Altogether, these lead to a dimension count of
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}X(\beta)=7-3=4
, which indeed coincides with
\ell(\beta)-\ell(\delta(\beta))=7-3=4
.
\Box

(ii)
Double braid varieties
. In the case of
R(\bbbeta)
, the quotient by the
\mathsf{G}
-action requires a word. Consider the variety
\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
given by the subset of points in
(\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U}_{+})^{[0,l]}\times(\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U}_{+})^{[0,l]}
satisfying
Equation

2.7
, so that
R(\bbbeta)
is the quotient of
\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
by the diagonal action of
\mathsf{G}
. Since this
\mathsf{G}
-action is free and
\mathsf{G}
is reductive, Geometric Invariant Theory, cf. e.g.
[
New09
]
, implies that

(2.13)

\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]\cong\Gamma(\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta),\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)})^{\mathsf{G}},

i.e. the ring of regular functions on
R(\bbbeta)
is isomorphic to the ring of
\mathsf{G}
-invariant global sections of the structure sheaf of
\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
. For an alternative description, let
\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)\subseteq\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
be the closed subvariety defined by the condition
Y_{0}=\mathsf{U}_{+}
.

Then the group
\mathsf{U}_{+}
, which is the stabilizer of
Y_{0}=\mathsf{U}_{+}
, acts freely on
\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
and there is a natural bijection between the
\mathsf{G}
-orbits in
\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
and the
\mathsf{U}_{+}
-orbits in
\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
, so that
R(\bbbeta)\cong\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)/\mathsf{G}\cong\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)/\mathsf{U}_{+}
. It follows that the pull-back
i^{*}:\Gamma(\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta),\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)})\to\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)]
, induced by the inclusion
i:\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)\to\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
, descends to an isomorphism
[i^{*}]:\Gamma(\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta),\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)})^{\mathsf{G}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)]^{\mathsf{U}_{+}}
. Therefore, the commutative algebra
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
is isomorphic to

(2.14)

\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)]^{\mathsf{U}_{+}}.

2.5.
Parametrizations

Let us provide explicit coordinates on the varieties
R(\bbbeta)
and
X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
, as well as on the auxiliary variety
\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
appearing in
Section

2.4
.

2.5.1.
Parametrizing
R(\bbbeta)

Let
\bbbeta=i_{1}\cdots i_{n+m}
be a double braid word. We parameterize
R(\bbbeta)
as follows. Using the
\mathsf{G}
-action, we assume that
Y_{0}=\mathsf{U}_{+}
, i.e. we work on the space
\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\beta)
defined in
Section

2.4
above. We set
g^{\prime}_{0}:=\mathrm{id}\in\mathsf{G}
and define a sequence of elements
g^{\prime}_{1},\dots,g^{\prime}_{n+m}\in\mathsf{G}
and the associated weighted flags
Y_{1}=g_{1}^{\prime}\mathsf{U}_{+},\dots,Y_{n+m}=g_{n+m}^{\prime}\mathsf{U}_{+}
inductively by

g^{\prime}_{c}:=\begin{cases}g^{\prime}_{c-1},&\text{if $i_{c}\in I$,}\\
g^{\prime}_{c-1}B_{|i_{c}|^{\ast}}(z_{c}),&\text{if $i_{c}\in-I$,}\end{cases}\quad\text{for $c=1,2,\dots,{n+m}$.}

Note that the weighted flags
Y_{0},\dots,Y_{n+m}
uniquely determine the scalars
z_{c},c\in J_{-}
and vice versa. Similarly, we define a sequence of elements
g_{m+n},\dots,g_{0}\in\mathsf{G}
and the associated weighted flags
X_{m+n}=g_{n+m}\mathsf{U}_{+},\dots,X_{0}=g_{0}\mathsf{U}_{+}
by

g_{m+n}=g^{\prime}_{m+n}\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{c-1}=\begin{cases}g_{c}B_{i_{c}}(z_{c}),&\text{if}\;i_{c}\in I,\\
g_{c},&\text{if}\;i_{c}\in-I.\end{cases}

The weighted flags
X_{m+n},\dots,X_{0}
uniquely determine the scalars
z_{c}
,
c\in J_{+}
, and vice versa. Thus, if we let
\mathcal{Z}(\bbbeta)
be the space defined as
\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
but without the condition that
Y_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{\circ}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}X_{0}
, we obtain that
\mathcal{Z}(\bbbeta)
is an affine variety with coordinate algebra

(2.15)

\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{Z}(\bbbeta)]\cong\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{n+m}],

that is,
z_{1},\dots,z_{n+m}
give coordinates on the space
\mathcal{Z}(\bbbeta)
. The condition
Y_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{\circ}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}X_{0}
gives an open condition on
\mathcal{Z}(\bbbeta)
, so we obtain that

\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)]\;\text{is a localization of}\;\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{Z}(\bbbeta)]\cong\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{n+m}].

Finally,
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)]^{\mathsf{U}_{+}}
, so
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
is a subalgebra of a localization of
\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{n+m}]
.

2.5.2.
Parametrizing
X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})

A parametrization of
X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
has been obtained in
Equation

2.11
, but we recall it for the sake of comparing to the parametrization of
R(\bbbeta)
obtained in
Section

2.5.1
. Let us write
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}=j_{1}\cdots j_{m+n}
. We define elements
f_{0}=\mathrm{id}\in\mathsf{G}
,
f_{1},\dots,f_{n+m}
and the associated flags
\mathsf{B}_{0}=\mathsf{B}
,
\mathsf{B}_{1}=f_{1}\mathsf{B},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{n+m}=f_{n+m}\mathsf{B}
as follows:

f_{c}=f_{c-1}B_{j_{c}}(z^{\prime}_{c}).

The variety of collections of flags satisfying the relative position conditions

\mathsf{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{j_{1}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{j_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{j_{n+m}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{n+m}

is then isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}[z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,z^{\prime}_{n+m}]
, and the condition
\mathsf{B}_{n+m}=w_{\circ}\mathsf{B}
defining
X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
is a closed condition. Thus, we obtain a natural surjection

\mathbb{C}[z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,z^{\prime}_{n+m}]\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{C}[X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})].

2.5.3.
Parametrizing the isomorphism
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\longrightarrow X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})

By
Proposition

2.8
, we have an isomorphism
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\xrightarrow{\cong}X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
. We now present the induced isomorphism
\varphi^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}[X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
in terms of the coordinates obtained above.

Recall that we set
J_{-}=\{a_{1}<\cdots<a_{n}\}
for the set of indices
j
such that
i_{j}\in-I
, and similarly
J_{+}=\{b_{1}<\cdots<b_{m}\}
. We define a bijection
\phi:[{n+m}]\longrightarrow[{n+m}]
as follows:

\phi(d)=a_{d}\quad\mbox{for}\quad d\in[1,n],\qquad\phi(n+d^{\prime})=b_{m+d^{\prime}-1}\quad\mbox{for}\quad d^{\prime}\in[1,m].

We can use the bijection
\phi
to present the isomorphism
\varphi^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}[(X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
using the coordinates
z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,z^{\prime}_{n+m}
and
z_{1},\dots,z_{n+m}
, as follows:

Proposition 2.12
.

Consider the isomorphism

\varphi^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}[z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,z^{\prime}_{n+m}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{n+m}],\quad\varphi^{\ast}(z^{\prime}_{c})=z_{\phi^{-1}(c)}.

Then,
\varphi^{\ast}
induces an homonymous isomorphism
\varphi^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}[X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
that is dual to the isomorphism
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\longrightarrow X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
. In precise terms, the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,z^{\prime}_{n+m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{n+m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)]^{\mathsf{U}_{+}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

∎

Proposition

2.12
follows directly from the construction of the isomorphism
\varphi
. Note that a tuple
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})\in R(\bbbeta)
satisfying (
2.7
) is mapped under
\varphi
to the tuple of weighted flags
F_{\bullet}\in X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
given by

(2.16)

F_{d}=B_{i^{\prime}_{1}}(z^{\prime}_{1})B_{i^{\prime}_{2}}(z^{\prime}_{2})\cdots B_{i^{\prime}_{d}}(z^{\prime}_{d})U_{+}\quad\mbox{ for each }d\in[0,{n+m}].

Example 2.13
.

We illustrate the isomorphism in
Proposition

2.12
in our running example, continuing
Example

2.9
.
We have

\displaystyle Y_{0}=F_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{U}_{+};

\displaystyle Y_{1}=Y_{2}=Y_{3}=Y_{4}=F_{1}

\displaystyle=B_{1}(z_{1})\mathsf{U}_{+};

\displaystyle Y_{5}=Y_{6}=Y_{7}=X_{7}=F_{2}

\displaystyle=B_{1}(z_{1})B_{2}(z_{5})\mathsf{U}_{+};

\displaystyle X_{6}=F_{3}

\displaystyle=B_{1}(z_{1})B_{2}(z_{5})B_{2}(z_{7})\mathsf{U}_{+};

\displaystyle X_{5}=X_{4}=F_{4}

\displaystyle=B_{1}(z_{1})B_{2}(z_{5})B_{2}(z_{7})B_{1}(z_{6})\mathsf{U}_{+};

\displaystyle X_{3}=F_{5}

\displaystyle=B_{1}(z_{1})B_{2}(z_{5})B_{2}(z_{7})B_{1}(z_{6})B_{1}(z_{4})\mathsf{U}_{+};

\displaystyle X_{2}=F_{6}

\displaystyle=B_{1}(z_{1})B_{2}(z_{5})B_{2}(z_{7})B_{1}(z_{6})B_{1}(z_{4})B_{2}(z_{3})\mathsf{U}_{+};

\displaystyle X_{1}=X_{0}=F_{7}

\displaystyle=B_{1}(z_{1})B_{2}(z_{5})B_{2}(z_{7})B_{1}(z_{6})B_{1}(z_{4})B_{2}(z_{3})B_{1}(z_{2})\mathsf{U}_{+}.

Thus, the isomorphism from
Proposition

2.12
is given by
\varphi^{\ast}(z^{\prime}_{1})=z_{1},\varphi^{\ast}(z^{\prime}_{2})=z_{5}
,
\varphi^{\ast}(z^{\prime}_{3})=z_{7}
,
\varphi^{\ast}(z^{\prime}_{4})=z_{6}
,
\varphi^{\ast}(z^{\prime}_{5})=z_{4}
,
\varphi^{\ast}(z^{\prime}_{6})=z_{3}
and
\varphi^{\ast}(z^{\prime}_{7})=z_{2}
.∎

3.
Comparison of tori

Section

2
introduced
X(\beta)
and
R(\bbbeta)
, from
[
CGG+25
]
and
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
respectively, and
Proposition

2.8
established the isomorphism
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\xrightarrow{\sim}X(\beta)
when
\beta=\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
, cf.
Equation

2.8
. The goal of the present section is to compare the open torus charts in
X(\beta)
and
R(\bbbeta)
, respectively described in
[
CGG+25
]
and
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
, that are part of the cluster seeds of the respective cluster structures on
\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
and
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
.

There is an interesting conceptual difference in the way such cluster tori in
X(\beta)
and
R(\bbbeta)
are built. It is helpful to understand this crucial point when implementing the comparison:

(1)

In
[
CGG+25
]
, given the braid word
\beta
, one chooses an additional piece of combinatorial data to name a cluster torus: a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
, cf.
[
CZ22
, Section 2]
and
[
CGG+25
, Section 4]
. There are many such Demazure weaves for
\beta
, even up to weave equivalence. Once a specific Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
is chosen, it names an open torus chart
T_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset X(\beta)
. Non-equivalent weaves
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
give different tori
T_{\mathfrak{w}},T_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\subset X(\beta)
in the
same
braid variety
X(\beta)
.

(2)

In
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
, given the double braid word
\bbbeta
there is
no
additional choice of combinatorial data to name a torus. Instead, a unique torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subset R(\bbbeta)
is named from the double braid word
\bbbeta
: such torus is referred to as the Deodhar torus, cf.
[
GLSBS22
, Section 7.1]
and
[
GLSB23
, Section 2.3]
. It is possible to describe other tori in
R(\bbbeta)
by changing the double braid word from
\bbbeta
to
\bbbeta^{\prime}
, via a double braid move, and using the induced isomorphism
m:R(\bbbeta)\to R(\bbbeta^{\prime})
to pull

T_{\bbbeta^{\prime}}\subset R(\bbbeta^{\prime})
back to
m^{*}(T_{\bbbeta^{\prime}})\subset R(\bbbeta)
. In many cases,
T_{\bbbeta},m^{*}(T_{\bbbeta^{\prime}})\subset R(\bbbeta)
are two different tori in the
same
double braid variety
R(\bbbeta)
.

The main contribution of this section, summarized in
Proposition

3.17
, is the description of a Demazure weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta):\bbbeta^{(-|+)}\to w_{\circ}
such that the image
\varphi(T_{\bbbeta})
of the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subset R(\bbbeta)
under the isomorphism
\varphi
coincides with the torus
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)}\subset X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
.

3.1.
Demazure weaves

Weaves were introduced in
[
CZ22
]
and further studied in
[
CGGS24
, Section 4]
and
[
CGG+25
, Section 4]
. They are the combinatorial object that drives the construction of cluster structures on braid varieties in
[
CGG+25
]
. Indeed, given a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
, one names the cluster torus
T_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset X(\beta)
, the quiver
Q_{\mathfrak{w}}
and the cluster variables
x^{\operatorname{W}}=\{A_{i}(\mathfrak{w})\}
all in terms of the combinatorics of
\mathfrak{w}
. We now briefly summarize their definition and then discuss double inductive weaves, leading to the construction of the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)
, whose associated torus matches the corresponding Deodhar torus.

Definition 3.1

(Graph of braid words)
.

The directed graph
\Gamma=\Gamma(\mathsf{G})
is the directed graph whose vertex set consists of all the braid words in the positive Artin generators of the braid group
\operatorname{Br}(\mathsf{G})
, words being of arbitrary finite length. The set of arrows of
\Gamma
is described as follows, where
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
denote two vertices of
\Gamma
:

(a)

There is an arrow
\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
if

\beta=\dots\underbrace{(iji\dots)}_{m_{ij}\;\text{letters}}\dots\,\to\beta^{\prime}=\dots\underbrace{(jij\dots)}_{m_{ij}\;\text{letters}}\dots,

where
i,j
are adjacent vertices of the Dynkin diagram of
\mathsf{G}
.

(b)

There is an arrow
\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
if

\beta=\dots ik\dots\,\to\beta^{\prime}=\dots ki\dots,

where
i,k
are not adjacent in the Dynkin diagram of
\mathsf{G}
.

(c)

There is an arrow
\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
if

\beta=\dots ii\dots\,\to\beta^{\prime}=\dots i\dots,

where
i
is an arbitrary vertex of the Dynkin diagram of
\mathsf{G}
.
\Box

In
Definition

3.1
the notation
\beta=\dots(\upsilon)\dots
denotes that
\beta
is a braid word which contains
\upsilon
as a (consecutive) braid subword. Note that in cases (a),(b) of
Definition

3.1
,
\Gamma
has arrows
\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}\to\beta
, while in (c),
\Gamma
has an arrow only in one direction
\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
.

Definition 3.2

(Demazure weave)
.

Let
\beta\in\Gamma
be a braid word. By definition, a
Demazure weave

\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
is a directed path on the graph
\Gamma
from
\beta
to a reduced word for its Demazure product
\delta(\beta)
.
\Box

The Demazure product gives a bijection between the connected components of
\Gamma
and the elements of the Weyl group
W(\mathsf{G})
. As explained earlier, we focus on the case
\delta(\beta)=w_{\circ}
in this article, so we can consider vertices
\beta
in the connected component
\Gamma_{\circ}\subset\Gamma
consisting of elements with Demazure product
w_{\circ}
, and thus weaves
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to w_{\circ}
.

Demazure weaves can be drawn diagrammatically in the plane, as follows. First, the braid word
\beta\in I^{l}
is drawn as a sequence of
l
vertical strings colored by the elements of
I
: these are drawn so that they spell
\beta
left-to-right. Second, each arrow in the path is represented by the following intertwinings of the vertical strings, going from top to bottom:

(a)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{ij}=3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i}}
(a)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{ij}=4}
(a)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{ij}=6}
(b)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k}}
(c)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i}}

Each of these five pictures describes a move
\beta\to\beta^{\prime}
. The top horizontal slice in any of these five pictures above corresponds to the braid word
\beta
, which is spelled left-to-right by the vertical strings above, and the bottom horizontal slice corresponds to the braid word
\beta^{\prime}
, which is spelled left-to-right by the vertical strings below. From this diagrammatic viewpoint, a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
can be also described as a planar graph whose edges are colored by the elements of
I
, and with vertices of different types:
(2m_{ij})
-valent vertices as in (a), tetravalent vertices as in (b), trivalent vertices as in (c), and univalent vertices that are located at the top and bottom of the weave, spelling
\beta
at the top and a reduced word for
\delta(\beta)
at the bottom.

Figure 3
.
A Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:1221121\to 121
drawn in an upward pointing triangle
\vartriangle
. In this figure, blue is color 1 and red is color 2. This weave
\mathfrak{w}
is the double inductive weave associated to the double string
(2R,1R,1^{*}L,1R,2R,1R,2^{*}L)
and double braid word
(-2,1,2,1,-1,1,2)
from Example
3.14
. To recover the double string from the weave, scan top to bottom and record
iR
(or
i^{*}L
) when you see a weave line of color
i
on the right (or left) side of
\vartriangle
. To recover the double braid word, scan bottom to top and record
i
(or
-i^{*}
) when you see a weave line of color
i
on the right (or left) side of
\vartriangle
.

From now onward, a weave
\mathfrak{w}
will refer to such a planar edge-colored graph. An edge of this colored planar graph is said to be an
edge
or
weave line
, and
top edge
(resp.
bottom edge
) is edge adjacent to a univalent vertex on the top (resp. bottom) of
\mathfrak{w}
. We denote by
E(\mathfrak{w})
the set of edges of a weave
\mathfrak{w}
. We often draw
\mathfrak{w}
inside an upward pointing triangle
\vartriangle
, as in
Figure

3
, with one side parallel to the
x
-axis. The
bottom boundary
of this triangle is the horizontal base side, and the
top boundary
is the union of the two other sides, adjacent to the topmost vertex of the triangle. We place the univalent vertices spelling
\beta
on the top boundary of
\vartriangle
and those spelling
\delta(\beta)
on the bottom boundary. Figure
3
depicts a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to w_{\circ}
for the braid word
\beta=1221121
, where the word for its Demazure product is
\delta(\beta)=w_{\circ}=121
.

Remark 3.3

(Weave equivalences)
.

There are certain local moves between weaves which are said to be equivalences. Though we do not give the precise definition here, we refer to
[
CZ22
, Section 4.1]
,
[
CGGS24
, Section 4.2]
and
[
CGG+25
, Section 4.2 and Section 6.3]
for the equivalence moves and necessary definitions. The discussions in this article related to weaves are all well-defined for an equivalence class of weaves, and not just a weave itself.
\Box

3.2.
Weave tori

We now explain how each Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
specifies an open algebraic torus
T_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset X(\beta)
. We will use the following concept.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\beta
be a braid word and
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to w_{\circ}
a Demazure weave, drawn in a triangle
\mathfrak{w}\subset\vartriangle
. A
flag labeling
of
\mathfrak{w}
is an assignment of a flag
\mathsf{B}_{C}
to each connected component
C\subset\vartriangle\setminus\mathfrak{w}
of the complement of
\mathfrak{w}
such that:

(1)

If
C
and
D
are separated by an edge of color
i
, then
\mathsf{B}_{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{D}
.

(2)

\mathsf{B}_{C_{+}}=\mathsf{B}_{+}
and
\mathsf{B}_{C_{-}}=w_{\circ}\mathsf{B}_{+}
,

where
C_{+}
(resp.
C_{-}
) is the only connected component of
\vartriangle\setminus\mathfrak{w}
containing the left (resp. right) vertex of the bottom boundary of the triangle
\vartriangle
.
\Box

By construction, given a flag labeling of
\mathfrak{w}
, the tuple consisting of flags at the top regions (those that intersect the top boundary of
\vartriangle
) determines a point of the braid variety
X(\beta)
. This tuple of flags is said to be the
top tuple
of the flag labeling. By
[
CGG+25
, Lemma 3.1]
, the top tuple of a flag labeling uniquely determines all other flags in that flag labeling. That is, giving the top tuple of a flag labeling is the same as specifying a point in
X(\beta)
, and such flags in the top tuple uniquely propagate down to a flag labeling for the weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
. Given a weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
, not all points of
X(\beta)
are the top tuple of a flag labeling for
\mathfrak{w}
. In fact, such locus of points in
X(\beta)
is the required weave torus:

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
be a Demazure weave. By definition, the
weave torus

T_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset X(\beta)
associated to
\mathfrak{w}
is

T_{\mathfrak{w}}:=\{(\mathsf{B}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{l})\in X(\beta)\mbox{ s.t.~$(\mathsf{B}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{l})$ is the top tuple of a flag labeling of }\mathfrak{w}\},

i.e. the locus of points in
X(\beta)
that appear as top tuples for some flag labeling of
\mathfrak{w}
.
\Box

It is proven in
[
CGG+25
, Lemma 4.1]
that
T_{\mathfrak{w}}
is indeed isomorphic to an algebraic torus and it is open in
X(\beta)
, thus the nomenclature in
Definition

3.5
. As an instance of
Remark

3.3
, the results of
[
CGGS24
, Sections 5&6]
imply that the weave tori
T_{\mathfrak{w}},T_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}\subset X(\beta)
associated to two equivalent weaves
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}:\beta\to\delta(\beta)
must coincide
T_{\mathfrak{w}}=T_{\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}}
as subvarieties of
X(\beta)
.

Example 3.6
.

Consider the braid word
\beta=1221121
, as in
Example

2.3
, and the Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\to w_{\circ}
depicted in
Figure

3
. By using the
z
-coordinates in
Example

2.11
, we expressed
X(\beta)\subset\mathbb{C}^{7}
as a closed subvariety of
\mathbb{C}^{7}
. In these coordinates, the open torus
T_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset X(\beta)
corresponding to
\mathfrak{w}
is

T_{\mathfrak{w}}=\{(z_{1},\ldots,z_{7})\in X(\beta):z_{3}\neq 0,z_{5}\neq 0,z_{3}z_{5}z_{7}-z_{3}z_{6}-1\neq 0,z_{3}z_{6}-z_{5}z_{4}+1\neq 0\}.

The general method to obtain such equations is explained in
[
CGG+25
, Section 5]
. For instance, given the top tuple
(B_{0},\ldots,B_{7})\in X(\beta)
of any flag labeling of
\mathfrak{w}
, which is equivalent to giving
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{7})\in T_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset X(\beta)
, the weave
\mathfrak{w}
is such that any flag labeling must satisfy:

(1)

B_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}}}{{\to}}B_{3}
, which translates to the condition
z_{3}\neq 0
,

(2)

B_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{1}}}{{\to}}B_{5}
, which translates to the condition
z_{5}\neq 0
.

There are two more transversality conditions, expressed by
z_{3}z_{5}z_{7}-z_{3}z_{6}-1\neq 0
and
z_{3}z_{6}-z_{5}z_{4}+1\neq 0
, which translate to
B_{0}
and
B_{7}
being
s_{1}
-transverse to
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
respectively, where
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}
are certain flags obtained from the top tuple
(B_{0},\ldots,B_{7})
. Specifically,
F_{1},F_{2}
are the two flags assigned to the two connected components of
\vartriangle\setminus\mathfrak{w}
which are not adjacent to the top boundary of
\vartriangle
, cf.
Figure

4
below.
\Box

3.3.
Double inductive weaves

Given a double braid word
\bbbeta
, the Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\bbbeta^{(-|+)}\longrightarrow w_{\circ}
whose weave torus
T_{\mathfrak{w}}
coincides with
\varphi(T_{\bbbeta}\subset)R(\bbbeta)
is a rather particular type of Demazure weave, called a double inductive weave. These were introduced in
[
CGG+25
, Section 6.4]
. We recall double inductive weaves here and establish
Lemma

3.9
, which is the key result to compare the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}
in
R(\bbbeta)
to a weave torus in
X(\beta)
.

3.3.1.
Double strings

Double inductive weaves
\mathfrak{w}:\beta\longrightarrow w_{\circ}
are a type of Demazure weaves determined by the following piece of combinatorial data, cf.
[
CGG+25
, Section 6.4]
.

Definition 3.7

(Double strings)
.

A
double string

\ddot{\mathbf{s}}=(i_{1}A_{1},\dots,i_{l}A_{l})
is a tuple of entries of the form
iA
, where
i\in I
and
A\in\{L,R\}
. A double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
determines a sequence of braid words
\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{0},\dots,\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{l}
by the rule

\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{0}=e\quad\text{and}\quad\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{{k}}=\begin{cases}\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{{k}-1}i_{k}&\text{if }A_{k}=R\\
i_{k}\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{{k}-1}&\text{if }A_{k}=L.\end{cases}

The notation
L,R
in
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
stands for Left and Right. By definition,
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
is said to be a double string for
\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{l}
. To ease notation, the Demazure product
\delta(\beta_{k}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}})
is denoted by
w_{k}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
.
\Box

3.3.2.
Double inductive weaves

We draw double inductive weaves
\mathfrak{w}
in a triangle
\vartriangle
embedded in the
(x,y)
-plane, as in
Figure

3
. By definition, a point
(x,y)
is said to have
depth

d:=-y
and the
slice
of a weave
\mathfrak{w}\subset\vartriangle
at depth
d
refers to the list of weave lines encountered at depth
d
, read left-to-right. For reference, the top vertex of the triangle is always drawn at depth
-\varepsilon
and the (horizontal) base of the triangle is at depth
d=l+\varepsilon
,
l
being the length of
\beta
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
small enough.

Definition 3.8

(Double inductive weaves)
.

Let
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}=(i_{1}A_{1},\dots,i_{l}A_{l})
be a double string for a braid word
\beta
. By definition, the double inductive weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
associated to
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
as follows. First, each entry
i_{k}A_{k}
of
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
corresponds to a weave line of color
i_{k}
which starts at depth
d={k}-0.4
on the
A_{k}
side of the triangle and goes down. Second,
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
is then constructed scanning top-to-bottom, according to the following:

(1)

The slice at
d={k}
gives a reduced expression for the Demazure product
w_{k}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
.

(2)

If
w_{{k}-1}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}\neq w_{{k}}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
, then there are no vertices between the slices at
d={k}-1
and
d={k}
and the weave lines continue vertically through this strip.

(3)

If
w_{{k}-1}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=w_{{k}}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
, then there are only vertices of degree larger than 3 between the slices at
d={k}-1
and
d={k}-0.5
. In this case, the slice at
d={k}-0.5
must be a reduced expression for
w_{{k}-1}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
which ends in
i_{{k}}
if
A_{{k}}=R
, and which begins in
i_{{k}}
if
A_{{k}}=L
. Between the slices
d={k}-0.4
and
d={k}
, there is exactly one vertex
\mathtt{v}
, which is trivalent and involves the new weave line colored
i_{k}
that starts at
d={k}-0.4
.

Finally, for
{k}\in[1,l]
, we define
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k}
to be the truncation of the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
up to depth
{k}
, i.e.
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k}
consists of the strips from depth
d=0-\varepsilon
to depth
{k}
.
\Box

Note that
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{l}=\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
and that
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k}
is a double inductive weave for the braid word
\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{{k}}
according to
[
CGG+25
, Section 6.4]
. As introduced in
Definition

3.8
, there is an ambiguity in the definition of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
, as different choices of vertices between
d=k-1
and
d=k-0.5
result in technically different weaves. They are nevertheless equivalent, cf.
Remark

3.3
, and we denote all such equivalent weaves by
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
. See
Figure

4
for an instance of a double inductive weave as in
Definition

3.8
, with the relevant slices depicted as dashed horizontal lines: it is the double inductive weave associated to the double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}=(2R,1R,2L,1R,2R,1R,1L)
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(7)}=\mathsf{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(7)}=\mathsf{B}_{7}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(6)}=\mathsf{B}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(6)}=\mathsf{B}_{7}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(5)}=\mathsf{B}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(5)}=\mathsf{B}_{6}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(4)}=\mathsf{B}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(4)}=\mathsf{B}_{5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(3)}=\mathsf{B}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(3)}=\mathsf{B}_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(2)}=\mathsf{B}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(2)}=\mathsf{B}_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(1)}=\mathsf{B}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(1)}=\mathsf{B}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(0)}=\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(0)}=\mathsf{B}_{2}}

Figure 4
.
The weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
for the double string
(2R,1R,1^{*}L,1R,2R,1R,2^{*}L)
and double braid word
(-2,1,2,1,-1,1,2)
, together with the labeling of the regions of
\vartriangle\setminus\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
by the flags
\mathsf{B}_{i}
. The flags
\mathsf{B}_{(c)},\mathsf{B}^{\prime}_{(c)}
label the regions neighboring the left and right sides, respectively, of the triangle
\vartriangle
. Compare with the diagram (
2.9
) in
Example

2.9
.

3.3.3.
Weave tori for double inductive weaves

Let us provide another description of the weave torus
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}}\subset X(\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{l})
associated to a double inductive weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}\subset\vartriangle
for a double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
of length
l
.

For each
c\in[0,l]
, let
L_{c}
(resp.
R_{c}
) be the leftmost (resp. rightmost) connected component of
\vartriangle\setminus\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
intersected by a horizontal line of depth
d=c
. Note that
L_{0}=R_{0}
is the unique component touching the apex of the triangle,
L_{l}
contains the bottom left vertex and
R_{l}
contains the bottom right vertex. In addition, for each
c\in[0,l-1]
,
L_{c}=L_{c+1}
(resp.
R_{c}=R_{c+1}
) if and only if in the double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
we have
A_{c+1}=R
(resp.
A_{c+1}=L
).

For each point
\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}=(\mathsf{B}_{0},\mathsf{B}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{l})\in X(\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{l})
, place the flags in the top regions of
\vartriangle\setminus\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
, starting with
\mathsf{B}_{0}
in the region
L_{l}
and moving parallel to the side edges of the triangle. These uniquely determine a flag labeling, cf.
Section

3.2
. For each
{k}\in[0,l]
, let
\mathsf{B}_{({k})}^{L}
, resp.
\mathsf{B}_{({k})}^{R}
, be the flag labeling the region
L_{k}
, resp.
R_{k}
. See
Figure

4
for an example with all such labels.

Points
\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}\in X(\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{l})
that belong to the weave torus
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}}\subset X(\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{l})
are then characterized by the following property:

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
a double string and
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
the corresponding double inductive weave.
Then

\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}\in T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}}\Longleftrightarrow

\mathsf{B}_{({k})}^{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{{k}}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}^{R}_{({k})}
,
\forall k\in[0,l]
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Let us show that
(\mathsf{B}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{l})\in T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}}
implies
\mathsf{B}_{({k})}^{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{{k}}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{({k})}^{R}
for all
k
. Indeed, fix a flag labeling of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
with top regions labeled by
(\mathsf{B}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{l})
and restrict this labeling to
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k}
. The left region of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k}
is labeled by
\mathsf{B}_{({k})}^{L}
and the right region is labeled by
\mathsf{B}^{R}_{({k})}
. Since the bottom edges of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k}
spell a reduced word for
\delta(\beta_{k}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}})=w_{k}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
, which is a permutation, by
Lemma

7.1
(2) we must have
\mathsf{B}_{({k})}^{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{{k}}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}^{R}_{({k})}
.

(\Leftarrow)
Supppose that
\mathsf{B}_{({k})}^{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{{k}}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}^{R}_{({k})}
for all
k\in[0,l]
. We show inductively that the sequence
(\mathsf{B}^{L}_{({k})},\dots,\mathsf{B}^{R}_{({k})})
appear as the top tuple of a flag labeling for the partial double inductive weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{{k}}
obtained after
{k}
steps, relaxing the condition on the leftmost and rightmost flags if necessary. Once this is proven, this implies that
(\mathsf{B}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{l})\in T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}}
, as required. The base case is
k=1
, and since the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}
is simply a line, the statement is readily true. For the inductive step, suppose that
(\mathsf{B}^{L}_{({k})},\dots,\mathsf{B}^{R}_{({k})})
is the top tuple of a flag labeling
\mathcal{L}
of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k}
. For concreteness, assume that
A_{k+1}=L
: the proof in the case
A_{k+1}=R
is similar. Then
\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{k+1}=i_{a_{k}-1}\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{k}
, and we need to show that

(\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(k+1)},\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(k)},\dots,\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(k)}=\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(k+1)})

is the top tuple of a flag labeling of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k+1}
. By construction,
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k+1}
is obtained from
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k}
by adding first a string on the left and then, possibly, a strip of vertices with degree at least 4, followed by a strip with a trivalent vertex. We label
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k+1}
by extending
\mathcal{L}
to a flag labeling of the strip of degree
\geq 4
vertices (if it exists) and then placing
\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(k+1)}
in the far left region. By
Lemma

7.1
(4), this fails to be a valid flag labeling of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k+1}
exactly when
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k+1}
and
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{k}
differ by a trivalent vertex (equivalently, when

w_{k+1}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=w_{k}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
) and
\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(k+1)}
is equal to the flag
\mathsf{B}
to the right of the trivalent vertex. In that case the distance between
\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(k+1)}=\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(k+1)}=\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(k)}
is
s_{i_{a_{k}}}w_{k+1}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
, which is strictly less than
w_{k+1}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
: this is a contradiction and thus the resulting flag labeling must have been valid.
∎

Example 3.10
.

Note that the weave
\mathfrak{w}
from
Example

3.6
is double inductive. Let us verify that the description of the torus
T_{\mathfrak{w}}
given by
Lemma

3.9
coincides with the one obtained in
Example

3.6
. Let us recall that in
Example

3.6
we obtained that
T_{\mathfrak{w}}
is given by the conditions:

(3.1)

\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{3},\qquad\mathsf{B}_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{5},\qquad\mathsf{B}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F_{1},\qquad F_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{7},

where
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are flags obtained from the top tuple
(\mathsf{B}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{7})
, see
Figure

4
. Reading this figure top-to-bottom, we also obtain that the description of
T_{\mathfrak{w}}
in
Lemma

3.9
is:

(3.2)

\mathsf{B}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{3},\;\mathsf{B}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{4},\;\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{4},\;\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{5},\;\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{\circ}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{6},\;\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{\circ}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{7},\;\mathsf{B}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{\circ}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{7}.

By the definition of the braid variety
X(\beta)
and
Lemma

7.1
(2), the first two conditions in (
3.2
) are valid for any element of
X(\beta)
, so they can be omitted. By
Lemma

7.1
(2), we have that
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{4}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{B}_{1}\neq\mathsf{B}_{3}
, and by
Lemma

7.1
(4) this is in turn equivalent to
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{3}
, i.e. the conditions
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{3}
and
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{4}
are equivalent. Similarly, assuming
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{3}
, the condition
\mathsf{B}_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{5}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{5}
.

By
Lemma

7.1
(2) and the definition of
X(\beta)
, the condition
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{2}s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{5}
implies
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{\circ}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{6}
, so the latter can be omitted from (
3.2
). Similarly to the previous paragraph, assuming
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{\circ}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{6}
, the condition
\mathsf{B}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F_{1}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{\circ}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{7}
, and assuming the latter we have that
F_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{7}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{B}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{\circ}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{7}
. So (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) indeed define the same subvariety of
X(\beta)
.
\Box

3.4.
Deodhar tori

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word,
[
GLSBS22
, Definition 7.1]
and
[
GLSB23
, Definition 2.5]
introduce an open torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subseteq R(\bbbeta)
, referred to as the
Deodhar torus
. It depends on the following data that can be readily read from the double braid word
\bbbeta
.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
\bbbeta=i_{1}\cdots i_{n+m}
be a double braid word. Define a sequence of elements
w_{m+n},\dots,w_{0}\in W
by

(3.3)

w_{m+n}=\mathrm{id}\;\text{and}\;w_{c-1}=s_{i_{c}^{\ast}}^{-}*w_{c}*s_{i_{c}}^{+}.

where
*
denotes the Demazure product.

Note that
w_{0}=\delta(\bbbeta)
. Since we are assuming that
\delta(\bbbeta)=w_{\circ}
, the following definition makes sense.

Definition 3.12

(Deodhar torus)
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word. By definition, the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subset R(\bbbeta)
is the subvariety of tuples
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})
of pairs of flags, modulo the diagonal action of
\mathsf{G}
, satisfying the transversality conditions

(3.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{m+n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{m+n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{m+n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{m+n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{1}}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{2}}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{3}}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{m+n-1}}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{m+n}}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{1}^{\ast}}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{2}^{\ast}}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{3}^{\ast}}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{m+n-1}^{\ast}}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{i_{m+n}^{\ast}}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{m+n-1}}

\Box

It is proven in
[
GLSB23
, Corollary 2.8]
that
T_{\bbbeta}
is an algebraic torus of dimension
m+n-\ell(w_{\circ})
and an open subset of
R(\bbbeta)
. Intuitively speaking, the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}
is given by the condition that for every index
c\in[0,m+n-1]
, the weighted flags
Y_{c}
and
X_{c}
are as far away from each other as possible, starting with
Y_{m+n}=X_{m+n}
.

3.5.
The Deodhar torus as a weave torus

This section shows that the isomorphism
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\longrightarrow X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
from
Proposition

2.8
maps the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subseteq R(\bbbeta)
isomorphically onto a torus
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}}
defined by a specific double inductive weave
{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}}:\bbbeta^{(-|+)}\to w_{\circ}
. We start by describing this weave.

3.5.1.
Double strings from double braid words

The weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
such that
\varphi(T_{\bbbeta})=T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}}
is associated to a double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
. The relation between double strings
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
, as introduced in
Definition

3.7
, and double braid words
\bbbeta
is as follows: given a double braid word
\bbbeta
, we can associate a double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)
by reading
\bbbeta
right-to-left and replacing each positive letter
i
with
iR
and each negative letter
j
with
|j|^{*}L
. More formally:

Definition 3.13
.

Let
\bbbeta=i_{1}\cdots i_{n+m}
be a double braid word, with negative and positive index sets
J_{-},J_{+}
, and for each
c\in[0,m+n]
consider
{\overline{c}}:=m+n-c
. By definition, the double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)=(j_{1}A_{1},\dots,j_{n+m}A_{n+m})
associated to
\bbbeta
is given by

(3.5)

j_{c+1}A_{c+1}:=\begin{cases}i_{{\overline{c}}}R&\text{if}\;{\overline{c}}\in J_{+},\\
|i_{{\overline{c}}}|^{\ast}L&\text{if}\;{\overline{c}}\in J_{-}.\end{cases}

where
c\in[0,n+m-1]
.
\Box

By
Definition

2.1
, the braid word associated to
\bbbeta
is
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
: the double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)
in
Definition

3.13
is indeed a double string for
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
.

Example 3.14
.

Consider the double braid word
\bbbeta=(-2,1,2,1,-1,1,2)
as in
Example

2.3
. The associated double string via
Definition

3.13
is
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)=(2R,1R,1^{*}L,1R,2R,1R,2^{*}L)
, which is indeed a double string for the braid word
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}=2^{\ast}1^{\ast}21121=1221121
.
\Box

The association of a double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)
to each double braid word
\bbbeta
in
Definition

3.13
yields a bijective correspondence:

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\beta
be a positive braid word,
\mathbb{W}(\beta)
the set of double braid words
\bbbeta
such that
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}=\beta
, and
\mathbb{S}(\beta)
the set of double strings for
\beta
. Then

\mathbb{W}(\beta)\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}(\beta),\quad\bbbeta\mapsto\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)

is a bijection.

Proof.

For a double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}=(j_{1}A_{1},\dots,j_{l}A_{l})
, consider the double braid word
\bbbeta(\ddot{\mathbf{s}})=i_{1}\cdots i_{l}
given by

(3.6)

i_{c}:=\begin{cases}j_{{\overline{c}}+1}&\text{if}\;A_{{\overline{c}}+1}=R,\\
-j_{{\overline{c}}+1}^{\ast}&\text{if}\;A_{{\overline{c}}+1}=L,\end{cases}

for
c\in[1,l]
. If
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
is a double string for
\beta
then
(\bbbeta(\ddot{\mathbf{s}}))^{(-|+)}=\beta
and the assignment
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}\mapsto\bbbeta(\ddot{\mathbf{s}})
defines the inverse
\mathbb{S}(\beta)\longrightarrow\mathbb{W}(\beta)
of the map
\bbbeta\mapsto\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)
.
∎

Recall that each double braid word
\bbbeta
defines a sequence of elements
w_{m+n},\dots,w_{0}\in W
, cf.
Definition

3.11
. Likewise, a double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
of length
m+n
defines a sequence of elements
w_{0}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}},\dots,w_{m+n}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
as in
Definition

3.7
. The next lemma shows that the bijection in
Lemma

3.15
is compatible with these sequences.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\bbbeta=i_{1}\cdots i_{m+n}
be a double braid word and
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}=\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)
its associated double string. Then for any
c\in[0,n+m]
,

w_{c}=w_{{\overline{c}}}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}},

where
{\overline{c}}=m+n-c
.

Proof.

We verify that
w_{{\overline{c}}}=w_{c}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
for
c=0,\dots,m+n
, in increasing order. By
Equation

3.3
and
Definition

3.7
,
w_{m+n}=\mathrm{id}=w_{0}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
. Assume now that
w_{{\overline{c}}}=w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{c}
. By definition,
w_{\overline{c+1}}=w_{{\overline{c}}-1}=s_{i_{{\overline{c}}}^{\ast}}^{-}*w_{{\overline{c}}}*s_{i_{{\overline{c}}}}^{+}
. To determine
w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{c+1}
, note that by (
3.5
),

\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{c+1}=\begin{cases}\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{c}i_{{\overline{c}}}&\text{ if }{\overline{c}}\in J_{+}\\
|i_{{\overline{c}}}|^{*}\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{c}&\text{ if }{\overline{c}}\in J_{-}.\\
\end{cases}

Using the inductive definition of Demazure products, we have that
w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{c+1}=s_{i_{{\overline{c}}}^{\ast}}^{-}*w_{{\overline{c}}}*s_{i_{{\overline{c}}}}^{+}
as desired.
∎

3.5.2.
Tori comparison

Given a double braid word
\bbbeta
, denote by
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)
the double inductive weave associated to the double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)
in
Definition

3.13
.

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word and
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\longrightarrow X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
the isomorphism from
Equation

2.8
. Then

\varphi(T_{\bbbeta})=T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)}.

Proof.

Consider a point
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})\in T_{\bbbeta}
and, using the
\mathsf{G}
-action, we assume that
Y_{0}=\mathsf{U}_{+}
and
\pi(X_{0})=w_{\circ}\mathsf{B}_{+}
. Let
\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}\in X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
denote
\varphi(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})
. Use the notation of Lemma
3.9
to define
\mathsf{B}_{(c)}^{L},\mathsf{B}_{(c)}^{R}
for
c\in[0,n+m]
. We claim that for every
c\in[0,n+m]
we have the flag equalities

\pi(Y_{c})=\mathsf{B}_{({\overline{c}})}^{L},\qquad\pi(X_{c})=\mathsf{B}_{({\overline{c}})}^{R},

where we again use the notation
{\overline{c}}:=m+n-c
. If the claim is established, then the characterization of
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)}
in Lemma
3.9
together with
Lemma

3.16
imply the required result. We prove the claim by induction on
c
. The base case is
c=0
: by definition
\pi(Y_{0})=\mathsf{B}_{0}=\mathsf{B}_{(m+n)}^{L}
and
\pi(X_{0})=\mathsf{B}_{m+n}=\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(m+n)}
, thus the claim holds. For the inductive step, let us suppose the claim is true for
c
, and proceed to prove it for
c+1
, as follows.

Let us focus on the case
i_{c+1}\in I
, as the case
i_{c+1}\in-I
is similar. If
i_{c+1}\in I
, then
Y_{c}=Y_{c+1}
and in the double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
we have
A_{{\overline{c}}}=R
. This implies that
L_{{\overline{c}}}=L_{{\overline{c}}-1}
and therefore
\pi(Y_{c+1})=\pi(Y_{c})=\mathsf{B}_{({\overline{c}})}^{L}=\mathsf{B}_{({\overline{c}}-1)}^{L}
. This shows the first flag equality. For the second one, note that
X_{c}\neq X_{c+1}
: it then follows from
Equation

2.8
, and the fact that
R_{{\overline{c}}}\neq R_{{\overline{c}}-1}
, that
\pi(X_{c+1})=\mathsf{B}^{R}_{(c-1)}
. This concludes the claim.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

The proof of
Proposition

3.17
gives an alternative description of the isomorphism
\varphi
, where points in the braid variety
X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
are understood as top tuples, and points in
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)}
as top tuples for flag labelings of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
. Indeed, let
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})
be the unique representative of a point in
R(\bbbeta)
such that
Y_{0}=U_{+}
and
\pi(X_{0})=w_{\circ}\mathsf{B}_{+}
, and label region
L_{c}
with
\pi(Y_{{\overline{c}}})
and region
R_{c}
with
\pi(X_{{\overline{c}}})
. That is, set
\mathsf{B}^{L}_{(c)}:=\pi(Y_{{\overline{c}}})
and
\mathsf{B}_{(c)}^{R}:=\pi(X_{{\overline{c}}})
. To obtain the corresponding element
\varphi((X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet}))=\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}\in X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
read the labels of the regions, from leftmost to rightmost along the two non-horizontal sides of
\vartriangle
, and that yields the required tuple of flags
\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}=(\mathsf{B}_{0},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{m+n})
.
\Box

In
[
CGG+25
, Section 4.3]
right and left inductive weaves were considered, as special cases of double inductive weaves. Specifically, if
\beta=i_{1}\dots i_{r}
is a braid word, then its
right
inductive weave is the double inductive weave corresponding to the double string
(i_{1}R,i_{2}R,\dots,i_{r}R)
, while its
left
inductive weave is the double inductive weave corresponding to the double string
(i_{r}L,i_{r-1}L,\dots,i_{1}L)
, cf.
[
CGG+25
, Section 4.3]
for details. The corresponding weave tori are referred to as the right and left inductive tori.
Proposition

3.17
implies the following fact:

Corollary 3.19
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word and
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\longrightarrow X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
the isomorphism from
Equation

2.8
. The following holds:

(1)

Suppose
\bbbeta
is a double braid word using only letters from
I
,
\bbbeta=j_{1}\cdots j_{m}
. Then,

\varphi(T_{\bbbeta})\subset X(j_{m}\dots j_{1})

is the right inductive torus of
\beta=j_{m}\dots j_{1}
.

(2)

Suppose
\bbbeta
is a double braid word using only letters from
-I
,
\bbbeta=(-i_{1})\cdots(-i_{n})
. Then,

\varphi(T_{\bbbeta})\subset X(i_{1}^{\ast}\cdots i_{n}^{\ast})

is the left inductive torus of
\beta=i_{1}^{\ast}\cdots i_{n}^{\ast}
.∎
