. The construction and comparison of cluster variables will use these ingredients, so we give a summarizing account at this point to aid the reader in the next sections.

4.
Comparison of cluster variables

The goal of this section is to show that the cluster variables in
[
CGG+25
]
and
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
coincide. Specifically,
Proposition

3.17
implies that the isomorphism

\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\longrightarrow X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})

from
Equation

2.8
satisfies
\varphi(T_{\bbbeta})=T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)}
, where
T_{\bbbeta}
is the Deodhar torus of the double braid word
\bbbeta
and
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)}
is the weave torus for the double inductive weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
associated to the double string in
Definition

3.13
, cf.
Table

3
. For the cluster variables in such tori:

(1)

In
[
CGG+25
, Sect. 5.2&6]
, cluster variables
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\}
for the weave torus
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)}\subset X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
are constructed such that
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\in\mathbb{C}[X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})]
. These cluster variables are indexed by the trivalent vertices of the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)
, which are in bijection with certain letters of
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
.

(2)

In
[
GLSBS22
, Section 2.4]
and
[
GLSB23
, Section 2.7]
, cluster variables
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}\}
for the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subset R(\bbbeta)
are constructed such that
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}\in\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
. These cluster variables are indexed by Deodhar hypersurfaces, which are in bijection with certain letters of
\bbbeta
.

Both tori
T_{\bbbeta}
and
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)}
are given by the non-vanishing of
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\}
and
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}\}
, respectively, i.e. there are isomorphisms

(4.1)

T_{\bbbeta}\cong\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[(x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}})^{\pm 1}]),\quad T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)}\cong\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e})^{\pm 1}])

where the indices
e,{\overline{e}}
are understood to range through all cluster variables in the corresponding sets.
Equation

4.1
and
Proposition

3.17
imply that the pullback isomorphism

\varphi^{*}:\mathbb{C}[X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]

must be such that the pullback
\varphi^{*}(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e})
of a cluster variable
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}
is a Laurent monomial on the cluster variables
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}\}
. The goal of this section is to show that the Laurent monomial
\varphi^{*}(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e})
consists solely of a cluster variable
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}
. That is, for all indices
e
, the goal is to show that there exists a unique
{\overline{e}}
such that

(4.2)

\varphi^{*}(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e})=x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}.

and, moreover, the assignment
e\mapsto{\overline{e}}
gives a bijection betweeen the corresponding indexing sets. In short,
Equation

4.2
states that the cluster variables of
[
CGG+25
]
and
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
coincide under the isomorphism
\varphi^{*}
. This is achieved in two steps:

(1)

First, we show that
\varphi^{*}(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e})
is proportional to
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}
, i.e. we prove the equality in
Equation

4.2
up to non-zero constant
\lambda_{e}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

(2)

Second, we show that the constant of proportionality
\lambda_{e}
is exactly
1
, i.e. we establish the equality in
Equation

4.2
.

Conceptually, the first step requires only part of the technical results of
[
CGG+25
]
and
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
, whereas the second step is proven using a significant amount of the technical details from both these approaches. In what follows, Subsections
4.1
and
4.2
start discussing
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\}
and
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}\}
, respectively, and
Section

4.3
achieves Step (1), in
Proposition

4.16
. Subsections
4.5
,
4.6
and
4.7
further establish some needed results on
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\}
and
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}\}
, and
Section

4.8
establishes Step (2), in
4.27
.

4.1.
The cluster variables
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\}

In full generality, a description of the cluster variables
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\}
for an arbitrary Demazure weave is provided in
[
CGG+25
, Section 5.2]
. Since the comparison to
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}\}
only requires the use of the double inductive weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
, we now provide a more tailored description of
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\}
, cf.
[
CGG+25
, Section 5.3]
.

The combinatorial input data is a double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
for the braid word
\beta
. We maintain the notation that
\beta=\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
has length
n+m
and
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
is the double inductive weave associated to
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
via
Definition

3.8
. By
Definition

3.7
,
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
yields a sequence of braid words
\beta_{c}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
whose Demazure products are denoted by
w_{c}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=\delta(\beta_{c}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}})
. Diagrammatically, the cluster variables
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\}
are indexed by trivalent vertices of the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
. This can be expressed as follows:

Definition 4.1
.

An index
e\in\{1,\dots,m+n\}
is said to be
vertex crossing
of
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
if
w_{e}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=w_{e+1}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
. Equivalently, if there is a (necessarily unique) trivalent vertex between depths
e
and
e+1
on the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
. The set of such vertex crossings associated to
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
is denoted by

J^{\text{We}}_{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,m+n\}.

The trivalent vertex corresponding to
e
is denoted
\mathtt{v}_{e}
and its cluster variable
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}
.
\Box

The cluster variables
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\}
, indexed by
J^{\text{We}}_{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
, can be computed by using an edge labeling of the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
, decorating weave lines with elements of
\mathsf{G}
. This decoration uses the data of the chosen pinning for
\mathsf{G}
, specifically the coroot
\chi_{i}
associated to
i\in I
and the variant
B_{i}(z)=x_{i}(z)\dot{s}_{i}
of the exponential Chevalley generator
x_{i}(z)
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
. In precise terms:

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
be a double inductive weave. By definition, the
edge labeling
of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
is the labeling of the edges of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
by elements of the form

g_{\mathtt{e}}=B_{i}(f_{\mathtt{e}})\chi_{i}(u_{\mathtt{e}})\in\mathsf{G},

where
i
is the color of the edge
\mathtt{e}
and
f_{\mathtt{e}},u_{\mathtt{e}}\in\mathbb{C}(X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
are rational functions on
X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
obtained as follows:

(i)

The
j
th top edge
\mathtt{e}_{j}
of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
is labeled by

g_{\mathtt{e}_{j}}:=B_{i^{\prime}_{j}}(z^{\prime}_{j})\chi_{i^{\prime}_{j}}(1),\mbox{ so that }f_{\mathtt{e}_{j}}=z^{\prime}_{j}\mbox{ and }u_{\mathtt{e}_{j}}=1,

where we have written
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}=i^{\prime}_{1}\cdots i^{\prime}_{n+m}
and the functions
z_{j}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}[X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})]
are as defined in
Section

2.5
. In particular,
u_{\mathtt{e}}=1
for all top edges
\mathtt{e}
of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
.

(ii)

The functions
f_{\mathtt{e}},u_{\mathtt{e}}
for an arbitrary weave edge
\mathtt{e}
of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
are obtained by propagating down the functions
f_{\mathtt{e}},u_{\mathtt{e}}
at the top edges by using the rules from
[
CGG+25
, Definition 5.8]
.
\Box

At this stage,
Definition

4.2
admittedly has little content, as it refers to rules from
[
CGG+25
]
which we have not specified.
1
1

1

[
CGG+25
]
has raked weaves: we do not need such refinement for the comparison, so we omit this data.
The rules are such that the edge labeling of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
is unique. For now, it suffices to know that an edge labeling satisfies the following:

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
be a double inductive weave and
\mathcal{L}
a flag labeling. Then,
\mathcal{L}
lifts to a unique labeling
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
by weighted flags so that:

(1)

The top labels of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
are the weighted flags
F_{i}
given by Equation (
2.16
).

(2)

If two regions
F,F^{\prime}
of
\vartriangle\setminus\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
are separated by a weave
i
-edge
\mathtt{e}
and
F
is to the left of
\mathtt{e}
, then
F^{\prime}=g_{\mathtt{e}}F
.

Lemma

4.3
is a consequence of
[
CGG+25
, Lemma 5.9]
. Let us denote the set of vertex crossings
J^{\text{W}}_{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)}
by
J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
. The cluster variables
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\}
are then defined as follows:

Definition 4.4

(Weave cluster variables)
.

Let
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
be a double inductive weave,
e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
the index of a trivalent vertex
\mathtt{v}_{e}
of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
, and
\mathtt{e}
be the southern weave edge of
\mathtt{v}_{e}
. By definition, the cluster variable
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}
associated to
e
is

x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}:=u_{\mathtt{e}},

where
u_{\mathtt{e}}
is specified by the edge labeling of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
.
\Box

It is implied by
[
CGG+25
, Theorem 5.19.(3)]
that the rational function
u_{\mathtt{e}}\in\mathbb{C}(X(\beta))
is a regular function on
X(\beta)
, i.e. we actually have
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\in\mathbb{C}[X(\beta)]
, where
\beta=\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
. The set of cluster variables
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\}
in
Definition

4.4
is the set of cluster variables for the weave torus
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}}\subset X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
.

The following lemma is used in the proof of
Proposition

4.16
, in the upcoming comparison of cluster variables:

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
be a double inductive weave and
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
.
The locus

\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\neq 0\mid e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}},e<c\}\cap\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=0\}\subset X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})

coincides with the set of points
\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}\in X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
such that

(4.3)

\begin{cases}\mathsf{B}_{(e)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{e}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}^{\prime}_{(e)}&\text{ if }\;e<c,\\
\mathsf{B}_{(c)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}^{\prime}_{(c)}&\text{ for some }\;w<w_{c}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}.\end{cases}

Proof.

At core, this is implied by Equation (25) in the proof of
[
CGG+25
, Theorem 5.12]
, as follows. Let
e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
and let
e_{\mathtt{ne}}
be the north-east edge incident to the trivalent vertex
\mathtt{v}_{e}
. By this Equation (25), there exists a Laurent monomial
m
in the cluster variables
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e^{\prime}}
, with
e^{\prime}<e
, such that we have the equality

(4.4)

x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}=f_{e_{\mathtt{ne}}}\cdot m.

The proof of the lemma is deduced from
Equation

4.4
as follows. Consider a point
\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}\in X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
with
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}(\mathsf{B}_{\bullet})\neq 0
for
e<c
and
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=0
: we want to deduce the conditions in
Equation

4.3
. First we need to show that
\mathsf{B}_{(e)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{e}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}^{\prime}_{(e)}
for
e<c
. Note that
Equation

4.4
implies that
f_{e_{\mathtt{ne}}}(\mathsf{B}_{\bullet})\neq 0
for
e<c
, and that
B_{i}(z)B_{i}(z^{\prime})\in\mathsf{B}_{+}
if and only if
z^{\prime}=0
. This implies that, labeling the top regions of
\vartriangle\setminus\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
with the flags
\mathsf{B}_{\bullet}
and propagating them through the other regions, the flags separated by the southern edge of the trivalent vertex
\mathtt{v}_{e}
will be distinct. The first required relative position condition in
Equation

4.3
then follows from
Lemma

7.1
(2). Let us now verify the second condition of
Equation

4.3
. Since
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=0
and
m
is a monomial in cluster variables
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}
for
e<c
,
Equation

4.4
implies that
f_{c_{\mathtt{ne}}}=0
. This implies that the flags separated by the southern edge of
\mathtt{v}_{c}
are the same, and the distance between
\mathsf{B}_{(c)}
and
\mathsf{B}^{\prime}_{(c)}
is strictly less than
w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{c}
, as required. This shows that the locus

\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\neq 0\mid e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}},e<c\}\cap\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=0\}

is contained in the locus of points satisfying
Equation

4.3
. The reverse inclusion follows similarly.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

Definition

4.4
and
Lemma

4.5
are valid for double inductive weaves but, as stated, do not hold for a general weave. For a description of cluster variables in a general weave see
[
CGG+25
, Section 5.2]
.
\Box

4.2.
The cluster variables
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}\}

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word and
T_{\bbbeta}\subset R(\bbbeta)
its Deodhar torus. The cluster variables
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}\}
are defined in terms of certain hypersurfaces in
R(\bbbeta)
, which are part of the complement
R(\bbbeta)\setminus T_{\bbbeta}
of the Deodhar torus. For the indexing set, we need to introduce solid and hollow crossings, cf.
[
GLSB23
, Section 2.3]
. Recall the elements
w_{0},\dots,w_{{n+m}}
from
Definition

3.11
.

Definition 4.7

(Solid and hollow crossings)
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word. An index
e\in\{1,\dots,m+n\}
is said to be

(i)

a
solid crossing
if
w_{e-1}=w_{e}
, i.e. if the Demazure product stays the same through
e
.

(ii)

a
hollow crossing
if
w_{e-1}>w_{e}
.

The set of solid crossings of
\bbbeta
is denoted by
J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
.
\Box

The cluster variables
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}\}
are indexed by
J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
, i.e. there is a cluster variable per solid crossing of
\bbbeta
. Now, given a solid crossing
e
we define a sequence of elements
v^{(e)}_{m+n},\dots v^{(e)}_{0}
recursively by
v^{(e)}_{m+n}:=\mathrm{id}
and

(4.5)

v^{(e)}_{c-1}:=\begin{cases}s_{i_{c}^{\ast}}^{-}*v^{(e)}_{c}*s_{i_{c}}^{+}&\text{if }c\neq e,\\
s_{i_{c}^{\ast}}^{-}v^{(e)}_{c}s_{i_{c}}^{+}&\text{if }c=e.\end{cases}

In words, the sequence of permutations
v^{(e)}_{m+n},\dots,v^{(e)}_{e}
agrees with the sequence of Demazure products
w_{m+n},\dots,w_{e}
and then at index
e-1
one makes a “mistake” and takes the usual product (instead of the Demazure product), so that
v^{(e)}_{e-1}<v^{(e)}_{e}
. Then one proceeds by taking Demazure products to compute
v^{(e)}_{e-2},\dots,v^{(e)}_{0}
. This sequence of permutations names a sequence of relative positions that defines the following hypersurfaces of
R(\bbbeta)
:

Definition 4.8

(Mutable Deodhar hypersurfaces)
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word and
e
be a solid crossing such that
v^{(e)}_{0}=w_{\circ}
. By definition, the
mutable Deodhar hypersurface

V_{e}\subseteq R(\bbbeta)
is the Zariski closure of the locus

\{(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})\in R(\bbbeta):Y_{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v^{(e)}_{c}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}X_{c}\quad\text{for all }c\in[0,m+n]\}\subset R(\bbbeta).

\Box

The mutable cluster variables
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}\}
on
T_{\bbbeta}
will be characters of
T_{\bbbeta}
which vanish on exactly one mutable Deodhar hypersurface, and extend themselves to regular functions on
R(\bbbeta)
. In order to determine these characters exactly, rather than just up to units of
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
, we need to consider additional hypersurfaces, the frozen Deodhar hypersurfaces, which sit inside of a space different than
R(\bbbeta)
, as follows:

(1)

Let
\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)\subset(G/U_{+})^{[0,m+n]}\times(G/U_{+})^{[0,m+n]}
be the subset of points satisfying the relative position conditions from
Equation

2.7
, so that the quotient of
\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
by the diagonal (free)
\mathsf{G}
-action is the double braid variety
R(\bbbeta)
. This is the variety discussed in
Section

2.4
.(ii).

(2)

Let
\tilde{T}_{\bbbeta}\subset\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
be the subset satisfying the relative position conditions from
Equation

3.4
, which is the preimage of
T_{\bbbeta}
under the
\mathsf{G}
-quotient map
\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)\to R(\bbbeta)
.

(3)

Let
\mathcal{Y}(\bbbeta)
denote the partial compactification of
\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
obtained by dropping the condition
X_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{\circ}}}{{\Longleftarrow}}Y_{0}
.

Note that
\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
has already been considered in
Section

2.4
. The spaces
\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
and
\mathcal{Y}(\bbbeta)
suffice to name all necessary Deodhar hypersurfaces:

Definition 4.9

(Deodhar hypersurfaces)
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word. For a solid index
e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
, the
Deodhar hypersurface

\tilde{V}_{e}\subset\mathcal{Y}(\bbbeta)
is the Zariski closure of the locus

\{(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})\in\mathcal{Y}(\bbbeta):Y_{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v^{(e)}_{c}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}X_{c}\quad\text{for all }c\in[0,m+n]\}.

The index
e
is said to be
mutable
if
v^{(e)}_{0}=w_{\circ}
, or equivalently if
\tilde{V}_{e}\subset\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
. The index
e
is said to be
frozen
if
v^{(e)}_{0}\neq w_{\circ}
, or equivalently if
\tilde{V}_{e}\cap\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)=\emptyset
.
\Box

If
e
is mutable then the
\mathsf{G}
-action on
\tilde{V}_{e}
is free and
\tilde{V}_{e}/\mathsf{G}=V_{e}
is the mutable Deodhar hypersurface defined in
Definition

4.8
. By
[
GLSB23
, Proposition 2.19]
, the Deodhar hypersurfaces are irreducible, codimension 1, and their union is
\mathcal{Y}(\bbbeta)\setminus\tilde{T}_{\bbbeta}
.

Remark 4.10
.

By
Equation

2.13
, we can identify
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]\cong\Gamma(\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta),\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)})^{\mathsf{G}}
, i.e. any regular function in
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
defines a
\mathsf{G}
-invariant regular function on
\mathcal{Y}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)
, which in turn defines a
\mathsf{G}
-invariant rational function on
\mathcal{Y}(\bbbeta)
.
\Box

In
[
GLSB23
, Section 2.5]
, the authors construct a collection of functions
\Delta_{c}\in\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
for
c\in[m+n]
, referred to as the
chamber minors
, cf.
Definition

4.20
below, and show in
[
GLSB23
, Prop. 2.12.(2)]
that the collection
\{\Delta_{e}\}_{e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}
of chamber minors indexed by the solid crossings provides an isomorphism

(4.6)

\{\Delta_{e}\}_{e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}:T_{\bbbeta}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{|J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}|}.

That is, the restrictions of the chamber minors indexed by
J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
can be used as coordinates on the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}
. Via the isomorphism (
4.6
), the character lattice
\mathrm{Hom}(T_{\bbbeta},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
of the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}
has a basis given by the restriction of
\{\Delta_{e}\}_{e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}
. See
Section

4.6
for more on chamber minors. By
Remark

4.10
, a character
\chi\in\mathrm{Hom}(T_{\bbbeta},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
can be interpreted as a (
\mathsf{G}
-invariant) rational function on
\mathcal{Y}(\bbbeta)
, and the isomorphism (
4.6
) provides a specific identification for this. By definition, a character
\chi\in\mathrm{Hom}(T_{\bbbeta},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
is said to vanish along a hypersurface in
\mathcal{Y}(\bbbeta)
if the corresponding Laurent monomial in the solid chamber minors
\{\Delta_{e}\}_{e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}
, seen as a rational function on
\mathcal{Y}(\bbbeta)
, vanishes along the hypersurface. Such vanishing is crucial in the definition of the cluster variables in
[
GLSB23
]
, as it allows to name unique characters based on their vanishing along the Deodhar hypersurfaces from
Definition

4.9
:

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word and
e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
the index of a solid crossing. Then there exists a unique character

x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}\in\mathrm{Hom}(T_{\bbbeta},\mathbb{C}^{\times})

vanishing to order 1 on the Deodhar hypersurface
\tilde{V}_{e}
and not vanishing on
\tilde{V}_{c}
if
c\neq e
.

The collection of characters
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}\}_{e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}
from
Proposition

4.11
form the cluster of the seed with cluster torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subset R(\bbbeta)
constructed in
[
GLSB23
]
. For reference,
Proposition

4.11
is
[
GLSB23
, Proposition-Definition 1.3]
.

4.3.
The cluster variables
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}
and
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}
are proportional

The goal of this subsection is to establish that the cluster variables from
[
CGG+25
]
and
[
GLSB23
,
GLSBS22
]
are proportional. Specifically, that
\varphi^{*}(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e})
is equal to a non-zero scalar multiple of
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}
, so that
Equation

4.2
holds up to constants.

4.3.1.
Bijection between indexing sets

The cluster variables
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}
from
[
CGG+25
]
are indexed by the vertex crossings in
J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
, cf.
Definition

4.1
. The cluster variables
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}
from
[
GLSB23
,
GLSBS22
]
are indexed by solid crossings
J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
, cf.
Proposition

4.11
.
Lemma

3.16
readily implies following bijection between
J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
and
J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
:

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word with
n+m
letters and
{\overline{e}}:=m+n-e
. Then

inv:J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}\longrightarrow J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}},\quad e\longmapsto inv(e):={\overline{e}},

is a bijection of sets.

Example 4.13
.

Consider
\bbbeta=(-2,1,2,1,-1,1,2)
as in
Example

2.3
, so that
n+m=7
. From the viewpoint of
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
, the sequence of Demazure products reads
w_{7}=e,w_{6}=s_{2}
,
w_{5}=s_{2}s_{1}=w_{4}=w_{3}
,
w_{2}=s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}=w_{1}=w_{0}
and the (indices for the) solid crossings are
J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}=\{5,4,2,1\}
. From the perspective of
[
CGG+25
]
, the associated double string is
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)=(2R,1R,1^{*}L,1R,2R,1R,2^{*}L)
and thus we have
w_{0}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=e
,
w_{1}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=s_{2}
,
w_{2}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=s_{2}s_{1}=w_{3}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=w_{4}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
,
w_{5}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}=w_{6}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=w_{7}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
. In particular, the indices for the trivalent vertices of the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
associated to
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)
are given by
J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}=\{2,3,5,6\}=\{\overline{5},\overline{4},\overline{2},\overline{1}\}
, as stated in
Lemma

4.12
.
\Box

4.3.2.
Proportionality between cluster variables

Let us use the following notation:

Definition 4.14
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word and
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)
its double inductive weave.

(1)

{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}}:=\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}\}
,
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
, denotes the set of cluster variables in
\mathbb{C}[X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})]
for the weave torus
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)}
, as constructed in
[
CGG+25
]
.

(2)

{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}:=\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}\}
,
e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
, denotes the set of cluster variables in
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
for the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}
, as constructed in
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
.
\Box

To establish proportionality, we use that the variables
{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}}
and
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
can be expressed as polynomials in the
z
-variables introduced in
Section

2.4
.(i) and
Section

2.5
, for
X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
and
R(\bbbeta)
respectively. This property is in fact inductive, in that the polynomial expressions do not change as we add crossings in certain directions:

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word.

(1)

For every
e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
, there exists a polynomial expression

x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}=x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}(z_{1},\dots,z_{m+n})

which is invariant under extending the double braid word
\bbbeta
on the left.

(2)

For every
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
, there exists a polynomial expression

x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}(z_{1},\dots,z_{m+n})

which is invariant under extending the double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
on the right (and thus extending the braid word
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
on either side).

Proof.

For Part (1),
[
GLSB23
, Proposition 2.12]
implies that every character of
T_{\bbbeta}
may be expressed as a Laurent monomial in the chamber minors
\{\Delta_{c}\}_{c\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}
. Each chamber minor
\Delta_{c+1}
is a certain
(\mathsf{U}_{+}\times\mathsf{U}_{+})
-invariant generalized minor of
Z_{c}:=Y_{c}^{-1}X_{c}\in\mathsf{U}_{+}\backslash\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U}_{+}
. Assuming
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})\in R(\bbbeta)
is parametrized as in
Section

2.5
,
Z_{c}
depends only on the parameters
z_{c+1},z_{c+2},\dots,z_{m+n}
. Indeed, this follows by using the
\mathsf{G}
-action to multiply every representative flag by
g_{m+n}^{-1}
on the left, does not affect
Z_{c}
. The generalized minors of
Z_{c}
, and in particular the chamber minor
\Delta_{c+1}
, are polynomials in
z_{c+1},\dots,z_{m+n}
and do not depend on the letters
i_{1},\dots,i_{c}
of
\bbbeta
.

Now, the cluster variable
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}
is a character of
T_{\bbbeta}
. By the upper-triangularity property in
[
GLSB23
, Proposition 2.20]
,
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}
can be written as a Laurent monomial in the chamber minors
\{\Delta_{c}\}_{c\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}\cap[e,n+m]}
. In particular, the exponents of each chamber minor are given by the entries in row
e
of an upper unitriangular matrix. By
[
GLSB23
, Lemma 2.25]
, the entries in row
e
do not depend on the letters
i_{1},\dots,i_{e}
of
\bbbeta
and thus the expression for
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}
in terms of chamber minors only depends on the suffix
i_{e+1}\dots i_{n+m}
of
\bbbeta
. The expression for these chamber minors in terms of
z_{e},\dots,z_{m+n}
in turn only depends on the suffix
i_{e+1}\cdots i_{n+m}
. This gives an a priori rational expression for
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}
in terms of
z_{e},\dots,z_{m+n}
which only depends on the suffix
i_{e+1}\cdots i_{n+m}
. By
[
GLSB23
, Corollary 2.24]
,
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}
lifts to a regular function on
\mathcal{Y}(\bbbeta)
and thus this expression for
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}
must be a polynomial in the
z
-variables, see
Equation

2.15
.

Part (2) follows directly from the construction of cluster variables
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
, cf.
[
CGG+25
, Section 7.2]
, since extending the double string does not affect the top part of the double inductive weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
.
∎

Let us establish the proportionality of the cluster variables
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}
and
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{{\overline{e}}}
:

Proposition 4.16

(Proportionality of cluster variables)
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word and
e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
the index of a solid crossing. Then
\exists\lambda_{e}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that

(4.7)

x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}=\lambda_{e}\cdot x^{\operatorname{W}}_{{\overline{e}}}.

Proof.

We use the isomorphism
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\xrightarrow{\sim}X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
from
Equation

2.8
to identify the two algebraic varieties
R(\bbbeta)
and
X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
, and denote by
\varphi^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}[X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})]\to\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
the corresponding pullback isomorphism between their rings of functions. To ease notation, we denote the pullbacks
\varphi^{\ast}(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c})
of the weave cluster variables to
R(\bbbeta)
by
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
. For clarity, we separate the proof in the following steps:

Step 1.
By construction there is an inclusion
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]\subseteq\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)]
, and the latter algebra
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(\bbbeta)]
is isomorphic to a localization of the polynomial algebra
\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{n+m}]
. By
[
GLS13
, Theorem 1.3]
, the units in
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
are precisely the monomials in frozen variables of
\{x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{c}\}
. Therefore, we can directly work with the inclusion

(4.8)

\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]\subseteq\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{m+n}][(x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{c})^{-1}\mid x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{c}\;\text{is frozen}].

Step 2.
Both
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
and
{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}}
are cluster variables for a seed in a cluster structure on the double braid variety
R(\bbbeta)
. By
[
GLSB23
, Definition 2.23]
and
[
CGG+25
, Theorem 5.12]
, together with Lemma
3.17
, the associated cluster tori determined by these two clusters (in a priori different cluster structures) are both the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}
. Since cluster variables form a free generating set of units in the algebra
\mathbb{C}[T_{\bbbeta}]
, for every
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
there exists a Laurent monomial
m_{c}({\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}})
in the
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
-cluster variables such that

(4.9)

x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=m_{c}({\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}).

By Step 1, this equality can be understood as holding in the polynomial algebra

\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{m+n}][(x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{c})^{-1}\mid x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{c}\;\text{is frozen}].

Step 3.
Let
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
be such that
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
is a mutable variable. By
[
GLS13
, Theorem 3.1]
, the cluster variable
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
is irreducible in
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
. Thus, in this case the monomial
m_{c}
must be of the form

(4.10)

x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\phi(c)}m^{\prime}_{c}({\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}),

where
m^{\prime}_{c}({\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}})
is a monomial involving only frozen variables in
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
and
\phi
is a bijection of the indexing sets. Note that if
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
is such that
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
is a frozen variable, then the Laurent monomial
m_{c}({\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}})
must be a monomial in the frozen variables by
[
GLS13
, Theorem 2.2]
.

Step 4.
We now argue that
m^{\prime}_{c}=\lambda_{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
for
m^{\prime}_{c}
as in
Equation

4.10
. For this, we use
Lemma

4.15
as follows. Assume there is a frozen variable appearing in
m^{\prime}_{c}
. Clear denominators in (
4.10
) so that we have an equality

(4.11)

m_{c,1}({\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}})x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=m_{c,2}({\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}})x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\phi(c)}

that is now valid in the polynomial algebra
\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{m+n}]
. Extend the double braid word on the left so that all frozen variables appearing in (
4.11
) become mutable; this can be done, for example, by appending a reduced expression for
w_{\circ}
on the left of
\bbbeta
. By
Lemma

4.15
, the expression (
4.11
) is still valid for the longer double braid word
\widetilde{\bbbeta}
. The factoriality of
\mathbb{C}[R(\widetilde{\bbbeta})]
, cf.
[
CGG+25
, Lemma 5.29]
, and the irreducibility of cluster variables imply that
m_{c,1}({\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}})
is proportional to
m_{c,2}({\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}})
, and so are
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
and
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\phi(c)}
.
2
2

2

Note that the cluster variable
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
cannot be a factor of
m_{c,2}(x^{{\operatorname{D}}})
since
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
is not frozen in
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
. Similarly,
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\varphi(c)}
cannot be a factor of
m_{c,1}({\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}})
.
Thus,
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=\lambda_{c}x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\phi(c)}
for some constant
\lambda_{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

Step 5.
Let us now verify that
\phi(c)={\overline{c}}
, i.e. that the indexing bijection
\phi
is indeed given by the inversion
c\mapsto{\overline{c}}
. Consider the locus
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\neq 0|e<c\}\cap\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=0\}
. By
Lemma

4.5
, this locus coincides with the set of elements
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})\in R(\bbbeta)
satisfying

Y_{{\overline{e}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w_{{\overline{e}}}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}X_{{\overline{e}}}\;\text{for}\;e<c\;\text{but}\;Y_{{\overline{c}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\Longrightarrow}}X_{{\overline{c}}}\;\text{with}\;w<w_{{\overline{c}}}.

We note that the only possibility for
w
above is
s_{i_{{\overline{c}}}^{*}}^{-}w_{{\overline{c}}}s_{i_{{\overline{c}}}}^{+}
, by the properties of relative position. It thus follows that the locus
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\neq 0|e<c\}\cap\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=0\}
contains the locally closed subset from
Definition

4.8
, whose closure is the Deodhar hypersurface
V_{{\overline{c}}}
, and thus the vanishing locus of
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
contains the Deodhar hypersurface
V_{{\overline{c}}}
. By (
4.10
), the vanishing locus of
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
is exactly the same as the vanishing locus of
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\phi(c)}
. By
Proposition

4.11
, of all the cluster variables in
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
only
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{c}}}
vanishes on
V_{{\overline{c}}}
. Therefore
\phi(c)={\overline{c}}
, as required.

Step 6.
We have shown that
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=\lambda_{c}\cdot x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{c}}}
with
\lambda_{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
for
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
such that
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
is mutable. If
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
is frozen, extend the double braid word (and thus the double inductive weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
) until
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
becomes mutable, and apply the arguments above. This finishes the proof.
∎

4.4.
Towards equality of cluster variables

The current goal is to show that
\lambda_{e}=1
for every
e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
, where
\lambda_{e}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
are the constants of proportionality in
Proposition

4.16
. That is, we want to show that the Deodhar and weave cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
and
{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}}
coincide identically, not just up to constants. For this, we need to further study the combinatorics of weave and Deodhar cluster variables and their relation.

For the remainder of this section, we let
\bbbeta=(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n+m})=i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{n+m}\in(\pm I)^{n+m}
be a double braid word,

J_{-}=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{n}\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad J_{+}=\{b_{1}<b_{2}<\cdots<b_{m}\}

be the subsets of
[{n+m}]
recording positions of negative and positive letters in
\bbbeta
, and

\bbbeta^{(-|+)}=i^{\prime}_{1}i^{\prime}_{2}\cdots i^{\prime}_{n+m}\in I^{n+m}

be the (ordinary) braid word corresponding to
\bbbeta
, cf.
Proposition

2.8
. Let

\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)=(j_{1}A_{1},j_{2}A_{2},\dots,j_{n+m}A_{n+m})

be the associated double string and let
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)
be the double inductive weave, cf.
Section

3.3.2
.

4.5.
Combinatorics of weave cluster variables

From
Definition

4.2
we have the edge labeling of the double inductive weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
and the rational functions
u_{\mathtt{e}}
involved in the edge label
g_{\mathtt{e}}
. By
Definition

4.4
, for the southern edge
\mathtt{e}
of a trivalent vertex, the function
u_{\mathtt{e}}
is equal to
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
, for a certain index
c
. By
[
CGG+25
, Theorem 5.12 (i)]
, for all edges
\mathtt{e}
,
u_{\mathtt{e}}
is a product of cluster variables. In this section, we review the combinatorics of this product formula for
u_{\mathtt{e}}
and how the cluster variables spread along the weave edges top-to-bottom. This scanning procedure is essential in our comparison of the cluster variables
x^{\operatorname{W}}
to the cluster variables
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}
.

Recall that if
\mathfrak{w}
is a weave, then
E(\mathfrak{w})
denotes the set of edges of the graph
\mathfrak{w}
. The following notion is a key aspect of
[
CGG+25
]
:

Definition 4.17

(Lusztig cycles)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a weave. A function
C:E(\mathfrak{w})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is said to be
Lusztig cycle
if it satisfies the
tropical Lusztig rules
. Specifically, at
6
,
4
and
3
-valent vertices the tropical Lusztig rules are given by the following local models:

(4.12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\min(a_{1},a_{2})}

where the weights
a_{i}
and
b_{j}
in the 6-valent case satisfy the equalities:

(1)

b_{1}=a_{2}+a_{3}-\min(a_{1},a_{3})
.

(2)

b_{2}=\min(a_{1},a_{3})
.

(3)

b_{3}=a_{2}+a_{1}-\min(a_{1},a_{3})
.

More generally, at
8
- and
12
-valent vertices the rules are obtained by tropicalizing the formulas in
[
BZ97
, Theorem 3.1]
, see e.g.
[
CGG+25
, (36), (37)]
for the tropical Lusztig rules at an
8
-valent vertex.
\Box

Let us fix a double inductive weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
for
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
. For each cluster variable
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
, we now introduce a function
\nu_{c}:E(\ddot{\mathfrak{w}})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
which tracks the exponent of
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
in
u_{\mathtt{e}}
. Recall from
Definition

4.1
that if
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
, the unique trivalent vertex between depths
c
and
c+1

is denoted
\mathtt{v}_{c}
. The function
\nu_{c}
is defined as follows:

Definition 4.18

(Vertex cycles)
.

Let
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
be a double inductive weave for
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
,
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
and
\mathtt{e}
the southern edge of the vertex
\mathtt{v}_{c}
in
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
. By definition, the
vertex cycle

\nu_{c}
is the function
\nu_{c}:E(\ddot{\mathfrak{w}})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
uniquely characterized by

(1)

\nu_{c}(\mathtt{e})=1
,

(2)

\nu_{c}
vanishes on all other edges which intersect the region above depth
c+1
,

(3)

\nu_{c}
propagates down from
\mathtt{v}_{c}
by the tropical Lusztig rules (cf. Definition
4.17
) for edges that lie entirely below depth
c+1
.
\Box

The following result asserts that the collection of vertex cycles, as introduced in
Definition

4.18
, exactly record the factorization of
u_{\mathtt{e}}
into cluster variables. Note that this fact is true for general weaves, though we state it here only for double inductive weaves:

Theorem 4.19

(
[
CGG+25
, Theorem 5.12 (i)]
)
.

Let
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
be a double inductive weave for
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
. In the edge labeling of
Definition

4.2
, for any
\mathtt{e}\in E(\ddot{\mathfrak{w}})
, we have

u_{\mathtt{e}}=\prod_{c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}}(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c})^{\nu_{c}(\mathtt{e})}.

4.6.
Deodhar cluster variables and grid minors

In this section we discuss the torus element
h^{+}_{c}
of
[
GLSB23
]
, and further comment on the chamber minors, which briefly appeared in
Section

4.2
when defining the Deodhar cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
. Each element
h^{+}_{c}
belongs to the Cartan torus, and the chamber minors are certain characters on the torus that can be evaluated on
h^{+}_{c}
. Since the chamber minors are Laurent monomials in the cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}=(x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e})_{e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}
,

h^{+}_{c}
can be understood as a certain cocharacter evaluated on
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
. Intuitively, in analogy with weaves, the Deodhar cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
spread through the chamber minors analogous to the way the weave cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}}
spread through the weave; this analogy is made more precise in
Section

4.7
, see also
Section

6.2
. The chamber minors and
h^{+}_{c}
are rigorously introduced as follows.

Fix a point
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})\in R(\bbbeta)
in the double braid variety. For
c\in[0,n+m]
, we define the coset
Z_{c}:=Y_{c}^{-1}X_{c}\in U_{+}\backslash\mathsf{G}/U_{+}
and, to ease notation, we use
Z_{c}
to also denote a representative of such double coset in
G
. Following
[
GLSB23
, Equation (2.6)]
, for
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})\in T_{\bbbeta}
, we define
h^{+}_{c}\in H
to be the unique torus element such that

(4.13)

Z_{c}\in U_{+}\dot{w}_{c}h^{+}_{c}U_{+}.

We now define certain functions of
h^{+}_{c}
, which extend to regular functions on
R(\bbbeta)
, in
Definition

4.20
. Set
u_{c}:=w_{\circ}w_{c}
, where
w_{c}
is as in
Definition

3.11
, use
\omega_{i}
to denote the fundamental weights. If
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell}}\in W
, we define the group element

\overline{\overline{w}}:=\dot{s}_{i_{1}}^{-1}\cdots\dot{s}_{i_{\ell}}^{-1}\in\mathsf{G}.

Definition 4.20

(Grid and chamber minors)
.

Let
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})\in T_{\bbbeta}
,
c\in[0,m+n]
, and
i\in I
. By definition, the
grid minors
of the point
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})
are

\Delta_{c,i}:=\omega_{i}(h^{+}_{c})\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta_{c,-i}:=\omega_{i}(\overline{\overline{u_{c}}}h^{+}_{c}\overline{\overline{u_{c}}}^{-1}).

For
c\in[m+n]
, the
chamber minor
is defined to be the grid minor
\Delta_{c}:=\Delta_{c-1,i_{c}}
, and it is said to be
solid
whenever
c
is solid.
\Box

The grid minors in
Definition

4.20
are defined on the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subset R(\bbbeta)
and
[
GLSB23
, Lemma 2.17]
implies that they extend to regular functions on
R(\bbbeta)
and
\mathsf{G}
-invariant regular functions on
\mathcal{Y}(\bbbeta)
. In particular, they can be written as polynomials in the parameters
z_{i}
from
Section

2.5
.
3
3

3

The regularity of the grid minors follows from the fact that they are
generalized minors
of
\mathsf{G}
evaluated at the element
Z_{c}
, cf.
[
GLSB23
, Section 2.5]
.

The important features of the grid and chamber minors, which follow from
[
GLSB23
, Prop. 2.12 & Cor. 2.22]
, can be summarized as follows:

Lemma 4.21

(
[
GLSB23
]
)
.

The following holds:

(1)

The grid minors are characters on the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}
.

(2)

The collection
\{\Delta_{c}\}_{c\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}
of solid chamber minors is a basis of characters of
T_{\bbbeta}
.

(3)

The collection of cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
is related to the chamber minors
\{\Delta_{c}\}_{c\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}
by an upper unitriangular transformation.

The most important fact is
Lemma

4.21
.(3), which allows for the explicit computation of the cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
via Laurent monomials on the chamber minors.
Lemma

4.21
, together with the definition of
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
and the regularity of grid minors and cluster variables on
R(\bbbeta)
, imply the following lemma:

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
c\in[0,m+n]
and
i\in\pm I
. Then the grid minor
\Delta_{c,i}
relates to
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
via the formula

\Delta_{c,i}=\prod_{e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}(x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e})^{\operatorname{ord}_{V_{e}}(c,i)}

where
\operatorname{ord}_{V_{e}}(c,i)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is the order of vanishing of
\Delta_{c,i}
on the Deodhar hypersurface
\tilde{V}_{e}
.

In a certain sense,
Lemma

4.22
can be understood as the analogue of
4.19
in the Deodhar approach.

4.7.
Deodhar torus element in terms of weave
u
-variables

Let us now compare the data introduced in
Section

4.5
, for weaves, and in
Section

4.6
, for Deodhar tori. The main contribution of this section is
Proposition

4.25
, which expresses the torus element
h^{+}_{c}
for a Deodhar torus, as defined by
Equation

4.13
, in terms of the
u
-variables associated to the corresponding weave.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{6}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{6}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{7}=X_{7}}

Figure 5
.
The labels on the regions
L_{c},R_{c}
,
c\in[0,7]
for our running example, cf. Examples
2.13
,
3.14
and
4.13
. The dashed lines are at depth
c
, counting from top to bottom, as
Y_{\overline{c}}
labels
L_{c}
and
X_{\overline{c}}
labels
R_{c}
.

For that, we fix the double inductive weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
associated to the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}
. We first determine how to compute
Z_{{\overline{c}}}
from the slice of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
at depth
c
, as follows. Applying the isomorphism
\varphi
from
Proposition

2.8
, the top regions of
\vartriangle\setminus\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
are labeled by the weighted flags
(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})
, in some order. Recall from
Remark

3.18
that
L_{c}
is the region labeled by
Y_{{\overline{c}}}
and
R_{c}
is the region labeled by
X_{{\overline{c}}}
, see e.g.
Figure

5
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}P_{3}^{\uparrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,1,1}P_{3}^{\downarrow}}

Figure 6
.
A double inductive weave corresponding to the running
Example

2.13
, shown together with the paths
P^{\uparrow}_{3},P^{\downarrow}_{3}
from
Definition

4.23
. In this case
c=3
and
{\overline{c}}=4
, so
P^{\uparrow}_{3},P^{\downarrow}_{3}
start in the region
L_{c}=L_{3}
, labeled by
Y_{\overline{c}}=Y_{4}
, and end in the region
R_{c}=R_{3}
, labeled by
X_{\overline{c}}=X_{4}
. Additional flag labels
Y_{\overline{c}},X_{\overline{c}}
are depicted in
Figure

5
.

Let us now construct, using the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
, an element
Z(P^{\uparrow}_{c})\in\mathsf{G}
which will match the coset (representative)
Z_{{\overline{c}}}
used in
Section

4.6
:

Definition 4.23
.

In the notation above,
P^{\uparrow}_{c}
is defined to be the highest possible path in the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
from the flag region
L_{c}
to the flag region
R_{c}
: it is obtained by moving left-to-right and passing through only the top regions of
\vartriangle\setminus\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
, as in
Figure

6
(green). By definition,
P^{\downarrow}_{c}
is the path in the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
from
L_{c}
to
R_{c}
that is a horizontal line at depth
c
, as in Figure
6
(teal). Suppose that the path
P^{\uparrow}_{c}
intersects weave edges
\mathtt{e}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{e}_{r}
, in this order. By definition, the element
Z(P^{\uparrow}_{c})
is

Z(P^{\uparrow}_{c}):=g_{\mathtt{e}_{1}}\cdots g_{\mathtt{e}_{r}},

where
g_{\mathtt{e}}
denotes the label of
\mathtt{e}
in the edge labeling of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
, cf.
Definition

4.2
. The element
Z(P^{\downarrow}_{c})
is defined analogously.
\Box

Lemma 4.24
.

For all
c\in[0,m]
, we have the equality

Z_{{\overline{c}}}=Z(P^{\uparrow}_{c}).

In addition,
Z_{{\overline{c}}}
agrees with
Z(P^{\downarrow}_{c})
up to right multiplication by
U_{+}
.

Proof.

To show that
Z_{{\overline{c}}}=Y_{{\overline{c}}}^{-1}X_{{\overline{c}}}
equals
Z(P^{\uparrow}_{c})
, use the parametrization of
X_{{\overline{c}}},Y_{{\overline{c}}}
from
Section

2.5
to express
Z_{{\overline{c}}}
as a product of elements
B_{i}(z_{j})
. By
Definition

4.2
, the edge labels of the top edges of the weave are exactly the elements
B_{i}(z_{j})
appearing in the parametrization, and thus the equality follows. The fact that
Z_{{\overline{c}}}
agrees with
Z(P^{\downarrow}_{c})
up to right multiplication by
U_{+}
follows from the equality
Z_{{\overline{c}}}=Z(P^{\uparrow}_{c})
and proof of Lemma 5.9 in
[
CGG+25
]
, specifically the second paragraph of it.
∎

Let
\mathbf{j}=s_{j_{1}}\cdots s_{j_{\ell}}
be a reduced expression for
w\in W
and consider the following sequence of coroots
\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{i}:\mathbb{C}^{\times}\longrightarrow H
, which are the
inversions
of
w
:

(4.14)

\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{\ell}:=\chi_{j_{\ell}},\quad\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{\ell-1}:=s_{j_{\ell}}\cdot\chi_{j_{\ell-1}},\quad\dots\quad,\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{1}:=s_{j_{\ell}}s_{j_{\ell-1}}\cdots s_{j_{2}}\cdot\chi_{j_{1}}.

Now we can use
Lemma

4.24
to express
h^{+}_{c}
in terms of the
u
-variables in the edge labeling of the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
:

Proposition 4.25
.

In the notation above, suppose that the weave path
P^{\downarrow}_{c}
intersects weave edges
\mathtt{e}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{e}_{l}
, the corresponding reduced expression for
w_{c}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=w_{{\overline{c}}}
is
\mathbf{j}=s_{j_{1}}\dots s_{j_{\ell}}
and let
\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{\ell},\dots,\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{1}
be the inversions of
w_{{\overline{c}}}
, as in (
4.14
). Then the following equality holds:

(4.15)

h^{+}_{{\overline{c}}}=\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{1}(u_{\mathtt{e}_{1}})\cdot\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{2}(u_{\mathtt{e}_{2}})\cdots\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{\ell}(u_{\mathtt{e}_{\ell}}).

Proof.

Consider the product

Z(P^{\downarrow}_{c})=g_{\mathtt{e}_{1}}\cdots g_{\mathtt{e}_{\ell}}=B_{j_{1}}(f_{\mathtt{e}_{1}})\chi_{j_{1}}(u_{\mathtt{e}_{1}})\cdots B_{j_{\ell}}(f_{\mathtt{e}_{\ell}})\chi_{j_{\ell}}(u_{\mathtt{e}_{\ell}})

and recall that
B_{j}(f)=x_{j}(f)\dot{s}_{j}
. Since the word
\mathbf{j}
is reduced, we can move all factors
x_{j}(f)
to the left, conjugating them by elements of the form
\chi_{j}(u)
and
\dot{s}_{j}
along the way, and they will give an element of
U_{+}
. We can simultaneously move all factors
\chi_{j}(u)
to the right and they will get conjugated by factors
\dot{s}_{j}
. Thus, we have

Z(P^{\downarrow}_{c})=g^{\prime}\cdot\dot{w_{\overline{c}}}\cdot\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{1}(u_{\mathtt{e}_{1}})\cdot\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{2}(u_{\mathtt{e}_{2}})\cdots\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{\ell}(u_{\mathtt{e}_{\ell}})

where
g^{\prime}\in U_{+}
. By
Lemma

4.24
,
Z(P^{\downarrow}_{c})
differs from
Z_{{\overline{c}}}
only by right multiplication by
U_{+}
. So, the uniqueness of
h^{+}_{{\overline{c}}}
in the factorization (
4.13
) implies the required equality (
4.15
).
∎

Example 4.26
.

Let us compute these elements for our running
Example

2.3
, cf. Examples
2.9
,
2.13
, and
4.13
. Applying the computation from
Example

2.13
to
c=3
, we obtain

X_{4}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}z_{1}z_{6}-z_{5}z_{7}+1&-z_{1}&z_{5}\\
z_{6}&-1&0\\
z_{7}&0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right]U_{+},\quad Y_{4}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}z_{1}&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right]U_{+},\quad Z_{4}=Z(P^{\uparrow}_{3})=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}z_{6}&-1&0\\
z_{5}z_{7}-1&0&-z_{5}\\
z_{7}&0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right].

By (
4.13
), we thus have the following expression for the Cartan element at
{\overline{c}}=4
:

(4.16)

h^{+}_{4}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}z_{7}&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{z_{7}}\end{smallmatrix}\right].

Let us independently compute the right hand side of
Equation

4.15
to verify the equality. The weave path
P^{\downarrow}_{3}
in
Figure

6
intersects a blue edge and a red edge, which gives
w_{\overline{c}}=s_{2}s_{1}
and
(j^{(c)}_{1},j^{(c)}_{2})=(2,1)
. By
[
CGG+25
, Def. 5.8 & Lemma 5.9]
, we have

Z(P^{\downarrow}_{3})=B_{2}\left(\frac{z_{5}z_{7}-1}{z_{7}}\right)\chi_{2}(z_{7})\cdot B_{1}(z_{6})\chi_{1}(1)=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}z_{6}&-1&0\\
z_{5}z_{7}-1&0&-\frac{1}{z_{7}}\\
z_{7}&0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right].

First, this equality illustrates directly that
Z(P^{\downarrow}_{3})
differs from
Z_{4}
by right multiplication by
U_{+}
, in agreement with
Lemma

4.24
. Second, we observe from (
4.16
) that
h^{+}_{4}=(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})(z_{7})
, whereas the right hand side of (
4.15
) reads

h^{+}_{c}=(s_{1}(\alpha_{2}))(u^{(c)}_{1})\cdot\alpha_{1}(u^{(c)}_{2})=(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})(u^{(c)}_{1})\cdot\alpha_{1}(u^{(c)}_{2})=(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})(z_{7}),

since
u^{(c)}_{1}=z_{7}
and
u^{(c)}_{2}=1
. This illustrates the equality (
4.15
) in
Proposition

4.25
.
\Box

4.8.
Coincidence of cluster variables

Let us now upgrade
Proposition

4.16
to an equality where the proportionality constants are identically one. The following is one of our main results:

Theorem 4.27

(Equality of cluster variables)
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word,
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
the ring of regular functions of its associated double braid variety and
{\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
the Deodhar cluster variables of the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}
. Consider the isomorphism
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\xrightarrow{\sim}X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
in
Equation

2.8
and the weave cluster variables
{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}}
associated to the double inductive weave of
\bbbeta
. Then, for every
e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
, we have the equality

\varphi^{\ast}(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e})=x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}.

Proof.

As before, we work in the algebra
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
and use
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}
to denote
\varphi^{\ast}(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e})
. By Lemma
4.16
, there exists a constant
\lambda_{e}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}=\lambda_{e}\cdot x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}
, and we need to show that
\lambda_{e}=1
. It is enough to show
\lambda_{e}=1
when we restrict to the open Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}\subset R(\bbbeta)
and thus, to ease notation, we denote by
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e},x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}
the restriction of these functions to
T_{\bbbeta}
.

The weave cluster variables
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}\mid e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}\}
give an explicit isomorphism

T_{\bbbeta}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{\left|J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}\right|},

thus endowing
T_{\bbbeta}
with a multiplicative structure in such a way that
\{x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}:e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}\}
form a basis of the character lattice. Let us denote the torus
T_{\bbbeta}
with this multiplicative structure by
T_{\bbbeta}^{\operatorname{W}}
. We will show that for every
c\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
,
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{c}
is a character of
T_{\bbbeta}^{\operatorname{W}}
. Note that this will imply the result, as we will have that the constant function
\lambda_{e}=x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}(x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}})^{-1}
is a character of
T_{\bbbeta}^{\operatorname{W}}
and thus
\lambda_{e}=1
for every
e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
.

By
Lemma

4.21
, if the grid minors
\Delta_{c,i}
are characters of
T_{\bbbeta}^{\operatorname{W}}
for every
c\in[1,m+n]
and
i\in I
, then
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{c}
is a character of
T_{\bbbeta}^{\operatorname{W}}
for every
c\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
. It thus suffices to show that the grid minors
\Delta_{c,i}
are characters of
T_{\bbbeta}^{\operatorname{W}}
for every
c\in[1,m+n]
and
i\in I
. By
4.19
, the functions
u_{\mathtt{e}}
are characters of
T_{\bbbeta}^{\operatorname{W}}
for every edge
\mathtt{e}
of the double inductive weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}=\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)
associated to the double braid word
\bbbeta
. By
Proposition

4.25
, the torus element
h^{+}_{c}
is a particular cocharacter applied to some
u_{\mathtt{e}}
-variables. By
Definition

4.20
, the grid minors are obtained by applying
\omega_{i}
to
h^{+}_{c}
or a particular conjugate of
h^{+}_{c}
. Since cocharacters are multiplicative, as is conjugation and applying
\omega_{i}
, the grid minors are characters of
T_{\bbbeta}^{\operatorname{W}}
, as desired.∎

4.27
concludes the proof of equality of cluster
variables
. In order to show coincidence of cluster
structures
we still need to show that the exchange matrix associated to the double braid word
\bbbeta
coincides with the exchange matrix associated to the corresponding double inductive weave. This will be achieved in
Section

5
.

4.9.
Lusztig data and cocharacter comparison

We conclude this section by introducing the concept of Lusztig datum, which helps crystallize the role of cocharacters in the context of weaves, and by studying how cocharacters used in
[
GLSB23
, Section 7]
and (implicitly) in
[
CGG+25
]
compare to each other.
Section

4.9.1
focuses on Lusztig data, and
Section

4.9.2
establishes the equality between the associated weave cocharacters and the Deodhar cocharacters. Note that neither of these two subsections are logically required to prove the
Main Theorem
, but might be of independent interest.

4.9.1.
From vertex cycles to Lusztig data

Given a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
, it can be conceptually useful to translate the information of a vertex cycle
\nu_{e}:E(\mathfrak{w})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
into a collection of
Lusztig data

[\mathbf{j},f]
and a list of coweights, as introduced in
Definition

4.28
below. Conceptually, coroots
\chi:\mathbb{C}^{\times}\longrightarrow T_{\mathfrak{w}}
can be evaluated at
u_{\mathtt{e}}
, which
4.19
expresses as cluster variables with exponents given by vertex cycles
\nu_{c}
. This relation between weaves cycles and coroots can be formalized as follows.

Definition 4.28

(Lusztig datum)
.

A
weighted expression
for
w\in W
is a pair
(\mathbf{j},f)
, where
\mathbf{j}
is a reduced expression for
w
and
f:[\ell(w)]\mapsto\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is a nonnegative integer weighting of the letters. By definition, two weighted expressions
(\mathbf{j},f)
and
(\mathbf{j}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
are
equivalent
if for any Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:\mathbf{j}\to\mathbf{j}^{\prime}
, the unique Lusztig cycle with values given by
f
at the top of
\mathfrak{w}
has values given by
f^{\prime}
at the bottom of
\mathfrak{w}
. By definition, a
Lusztig datum
for
w
is an equivalence class of weighted expressions for
w
, denoted by
[\mathbf{j},f]
.
\Box

By definition, the
coweight
of a Lusztig datum
[\mathbf{j},f]
for
w\in W
is the following linear combination of coroots:

(4.17)

\chi[\mathbf{j},f]:=\sum_{r=1}^{\ell(w)}f(r)\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{r}.

The coroots used in
Equation

4.17
are the inversions of
w
, as defined in
Equation

4.14
. Note that the coweight in (
4.17
) depends only on the equivalence class
[\mathbf{j},f]
, and not a given representative
(\mathbf{j},f)
, i.e. the coweight of
[\mathbf{j},f]
is well-defined.

Let
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
be a double string of length
\ell
and
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}=\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\ddot{\mathbf{s}})
the corresponding weave. The connection between Lusztig data in
Definition

4.28
and weaves
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
starts with the fact that the horizontal slice of the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
at depth
c
gives a reduced expression
\mathbf{j}^{(c)}
for the permutation
w_{c}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
. Then, given a fixed trivalent vertex
\mathtt{v}_{e}
of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
, we can evaluate the vertex cycle
\nu_{e}
on the edges in this slice, which gives a weighting
\nu^{(c)}_{e}
of the reduced expression
\mathbf{j}^{(c)}
. This pair gives a well-defined equivalence class
[\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e}]
which is a Lusztig datum for
w_{c}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
:

Definition 4.29

(Lusztig datum from weave slices)
.

In the notation above, we define the Lusztig datum for
w_{c}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
to be given by the equivalence class
[\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e}]
. Its coweight is denoted by
\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathbf{s}},c,e}:=\chi[\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e}]
and, if
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}=\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)
is the double string associated to the double braid word
\bbbeta
, we write
\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)},c,e}
for the coweight
\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathbf{s}},c,e}
.
\Box

Note that slicing
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
anywhere from depth
c
to depth
c+0.5
gives rise to the same Lusztig datum, though the reduced word may change. In particular, all of the equivalent weaves we denote by
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
give rise to the same collection of Lusztig data. For an example of a weave and all its Lusztig data, see Figure
7
and Table
4
.

. Note that there are no

or

.

4.9.2.
Comparing weave and Deodhar cocharacters

For each
c\in[0,m+n]
we have many grid minors, which are all monomials in the Deodhar cluster variables. In order to record how the cluster variable
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}
spread across all grid minors at
c
, it can be useful to introduce the following cocharacter:

Definition 4.30
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word,
c\in[0,m+n]
and
e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
. By definition, the cocharacter
\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,c,e}:\mathbb{C}^{*}\to H

is given by

\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,c,e}:=\sum_{i\in I}\operatorname{ord}_{V_{e}}(c,i)\chi_{i}

where
\operatorname{ord}_{V_{e}}(c,i)
is the order of vanishing of
\Delta_{c,i}
on
\tilde{V}_{e}
, as in
Lemma

4.22
.∎

Note that the Cartan element
h^{+}_{c}
from
Equation

4.13
satisfies the equality

(4.18)

h^{+}_{c}=\prod_{e\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,c,e}(x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{e}).

Remark 4.31
.

Similar to
Definition

4.30
, one may define a cocharacter
\gamma^{-}_{\bbbeta,c,e}:=\sum_{i\in I}\operatorname{ord}_{V_{e}}(c,-i)\chi_{i}
encoding the vanishing of
\Delta_{c,-i}
. This cocharacter records the “spread” of the cluster variables in the negative chamber minors, and satisfies an analogous formula to (
4.18
), using
\bar{\bar{u_{c}}}h^{+}_{c}\bar{\bar{u_{c}}}^{-1}
rather than
h^{+}_{c}
. It suffices to analyze
\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,c,e}
.∎

Definition

4.30
gives a geometric description of the cocharacters
\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,c,e}
, whereas
[
GLSB23
, Section 7]
gives an algorithm for computing
\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,c,e}
in general type using root-system combinatorics and a somewhat intricate induction. As an application of our main cluster comparison result, we will see in
4.33
that the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
corresponding to
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta)
gives a simpler way to compute these cocharacters.

Remark 4.32
.

In Lie Type
A
,
[
GLSBS22
, Section 3.4]
gives an algorithm for computing
\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,c,e}
using the combinatorics of
monotone multicurves
.
Section

6.2
establishes the comparison between these specific combinatorics and the weave framework.
\Box

The next corollary shows that the Deodhar cocharacter, recording the orders of vanishing of grid minors along Deodhar hypersurfaces, can be computed entirely combinatorially from weaves, Lusztig cycles, and the corresponding Lusztig datum.

Corollary 4.33
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word,
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
the corresponding braid word and
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}
the corresponding double inductive weave. Choose
e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
and
c\in[0,m+n]
. Then

\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,{\overline{c}},{\overline{e}}}=\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)},c,e}.

Proof.

We use the notation from the proof of
Proposition

4.25
. By (
4.15
) and (
4.18
), we have

\prod_{{\overline{e}}\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,{\overline{c}},{\overline{e}}}(x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\overline{e}})=h^{+}_{{\overline{c}}}=\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{1}(u_{\mathtt{e}_{1}})\cdot\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{2}(u_{\mathtt{e}_{2}})\cdots\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{\ell}(u_{\mathtt{e}_{\ell}}).

Recall from
4.19
that each
u_{\mathtt{e}_{i}}
is a product of cluster variables, and the exponents are given precisely by the values of the vertex cycles on
\mathtt{e}_{i}
. The contribution of
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}
to the right-hand side of the above equation is

\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{1}({(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e})}^{\nu_{e}(\mathtt{e}_{1})})\cdot\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{2}({(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e})}^{\nu_{e}(\mathtt{e}_{2})})\cdots\chi^{\mathbf{j}}_{\ell}({(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e})}^{\nu_{e}(\mathtt{e}_{\ell})})=\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)},c,e}(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}).

So in fact

\prod_{{\overline{e}}\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,{\overline{c}},{\overline{e}}}(x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\overline{e}})=h^{+}_{{\overline{c}}}=\prod_{e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}}\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)},c,e}(x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}).

By
4.27
,
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{{\overline{e}}}=x^{\operatorname{W}}_{e}
for every
e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
. Let us fix
e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
. Since cluster variables are algebraically independent, we can set
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\overline{f}}=x^{\operatorname{W}}_{f}=1
for
f\neq e
and the above equation then shows that
\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,{\overline{c}},{\overline{e}}}=\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)},c,e}
.
∎

Remark 4.34
.

4.33
could also be proved purely combinatorially by showing that the cocharacter
\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)},c,e}
satisfies all the conditions in
[
GLSB23
, Proposition 7.2]
.∎

5.
Comparison of exchange matrices

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word.
Section

2
established an isomorphism of algebraic varieties

\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\xrightarrow{\sim}X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)}).

Section

3
proved the equality of tori between the weave and Deodhar cluster algebra structures:
\varphi(T_{\bbbeta})=T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta))}
, cf.
Proposition

3.17
.
Section

4
showed the corresponding equality of cluster variables:
\varphi^{\ast}{\bm{x}}^{\operatorname{W}}={\bm{x}}^{{\operatorname{D}}}
, cf.
4.27
. In order to complete our proof of the
Main Theorem
, it remains to establish the equality of 2-forms:
\varphi^{\ast}\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}=\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}
, which is the content of the following theorem.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word,
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
the ring of regular functions of its associated double braid variety and
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}
the Deodhar 2-form on the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}
. Consider the isomorphism
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\xrightarrow{\sim}X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
in
Equation

2.8
and the weave 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
on the weave torus
T_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\ddot{\mathbf{s}}(\bbbeta))}
associated to the double inductive weave of
\bbbeta
. Then

\varphi^{\ast}\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}=\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}.

The remainder of this section is devoted to proving
5.1
.

5.1.
Setup and notation

In this section, we take the viewpoint that a cluster seed
\Sigma
consists of a collection
\mathbf{x}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
of cluster variables, a skew-symmetrizable matrix
(\varepsilon_{ij})
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
, together with symmetrizers
d_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. The matrix
(\varepsilon_{ij})
is often referred to as the exchange matrix, and in the skew-symmetric case it is equivalently encoded by a quiver. In our case, where the cluster algebra
A=\mathbb{C}[X]
is the coordinate ring of an affine variety
X
, the cluster variables
x_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[X]
are regular functions on
X
and the torus
T_{\Sigma}\subset X
associated to
\Sigma
is given by the non-vanishing of the cluster variables
\mathbf{x}
in
\Sigma
, so that we have the defining equality
\mathbb{C}[T_{\Sigma}]=\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm 1}]
.

The data of the cluster seed
\Sigma
defines a 2-form on the corresponding cluster torus
T_{\Sigma}
by

(5.1)

\Omega(\Sigma):=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}d_{i}\varepsilon_{ij}\dlog x_{i}\wedge\dlog x_{j},

where the symbol
\dlog(x)
is notation for
\dlog(x):=x^{-1}dx
. In other words, the cluster variables
\mathbf{x}
are the exponential Darboux coordinates for
\Omega(\Sigma)
on
T_{\Sigma}
, where the constant coefficients are given by the data of
(\varepsilon_{ij})
and
d_{i}
. In general, for a locally acyclic cluster algebra, this form
\Omega(\Sigma)
extends from the torus
T_{\Sigma}
to a (homonymous) regular
2
-form
\Omega(\Sigma)
on the entirety of the (spectrum of the) associated cluster algebra
A(\Sigma)
, see
[
Mul13
, Theorem 4.4]
and
[
FG09
, Lemma 6.10]
. The extended 2-form
\Omega(\Sigma)
does not depend on the chosen seed, and thus we denote it simply by
\Omega
. Note that the 2-form
\Omega
together with the cluster
\mathbf{x}
determines the matrix
(\varepsilon_{ij})
and thus, in the presence of
\mathbf{x}
, the data of the exchange matrix
(\varepsilon_{ij})
and the 2-form are interchangeable. Since both the weave and Deodhar cluster algebras are locally acyclic, by
[
CGG+25
, Theorem 7.13]
and
[
GLSB23
, Theorem 4.10]
respectively, these considerations apply to the coordinate rings of
X=X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
and
X=R(\bbbeta)
.

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word and
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
its associated braid word. We have shown in
4.27
the equality of cluster variables

x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}=x^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\overline{c}},

where we denote by
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}
the pullback of
x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}\in\mathbb{C}[X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})]
to
\mathbb{C}[R(\bbbeta)]
under the isomorphism
\varphi
, to ease notation. Let
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}\in\Omega^{2}(X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)}))
be the regular
2
-form on
X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
obtained from the weave cluster structure, as constructed and studied in
[
CGG+25
, Sections 8&9]
, and let
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}\in\Omega^{2}(R(\bbbeta))
be the regular
2
-form on
R(\bbbeta)
obtained from the Deodhar cluster structure, as constructed in
[
GLSB23
, Section 2.9]
. To ease notation, we still denote by
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
the pullback
\varphi^{*}\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
of the form
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
under the isomorphism
\varphi
, so that both
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
and
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}
are defined on
R(\bbbeta)
. In this notation, our goal is to establish the equality

(5.2)

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}=\Omega^{\operatorname{D}},

as regular 2-forms on
R(\bbbeta)
.

Remark 5.2
.

In order to show (
5.2
), it suffices to assume that
\bbbeta
is a double braid word only on positive letters. Indeed, if
\bbbeta
and
\bbbeta^{\prime}
are related by either of the moves (B1) or (B4) from
Remark

2.10
,
[
GLSB23
, Section 4]
implies that
\phi_{\bbbeta,\bbbeta^{\prime}}^{\ast}(\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{\bbbeta^{\prime}})=\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{\bbbeta}
, where
\phi_{\bbbeta,\bbbeta^{\prime}}
is the isomorphism from
Remark

2.10
.
4
4

4

This equality is called Property (F) in the proof of
[
GLSB23
, Theorem 4.2]
.
Using (B1) and (B4) moves, we can bring any double braid word to a double braid word with only positive letters. The commutativity of the diagram (
2.10
) implies that we can assume
\bbbeta
only has positive letters.∎

We show (
5.2
) by first analyzing how the
2
-forms are constructed in finer detail:
Section

5.2
does so for the Deodhar 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}
, and
Section

5.3
for the weave 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
. Both forms are defined inductively, using a “sum of local contributions”  procedure. We then show that partial sums of these local contributions coincide in
Section

5.4
, which implies (
5.2
).

5.2.
The Deodhar 2-form

Let
\bbbeta
be a double braid word. The Deodhar 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}
on
R(\bbbeta)
is constructed by using the grid minors
\Delta_{c,i}
of
\bbbeta
, introduced in
Definition

4.20
. Note that
Lemma

4.22
expresses such grid minors
\Delta_{c,i}
as a monomials in the Deodhar cluster variables
x^{{\operatorname{D}}}
.

For
i,j\in I
, let
a_{ij}=\langle\alpha_{i},\chi_{j}\rangle

denote the entries of the Cartan matrix of
\mathsf{G}
. The Cartan matrix has symmetrizers
d_{i}
, where
d_{i}a_{ij}=d_{j}a_{ji}
. For
i,j\in\pm I
, we set
a_{ij}:=0
if
i,j
have different signs, and
a_{ij}:=a_{|i||j|}

otherwise. We also set
d_{i}:=d_{|i|}
. For
c\in[0,{n+m}]
and
i\in\pm I
, we define the following 1-form on the Deodhar torus
T_{\bbbeta}
:

L_{c,i}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\pm I}a_{ik}\dlog\Delta_{c,k}\in\Omega^{1}(T_{\bbbeta}).

Note that if
i\in I
, then

L_{c,i}=\frac{1}{2}\dlog\left(\prod_{k\in I}\omega_{k}(h^{+}_{c})^{a_{ik}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\dlog\alpha_{i}(h^{+}_{c})

where
\alpha_{i}
is the simple root indexed by
i
. Here we are using the fact that the Cartan matrix gives a change of basis between simple roots and fundamental weights.

For each crossing
c\in[{n+m}]
, we define the following 2-form on
T_{\bbbeta}

\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{\bbbeta,c}:=\operatorname{sign}(i)~2d_{i}~L_{c-1,i}\wedge L_{c,i}\quad\text{where }i:=i_{c}.

Finally, by
[
GLSB23
, Section 2.9]
, the Deodhar 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}=\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{\bbbeta}
associated to the double braid word
\bbbeta
can be given as the sum

(5.3)

\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{\bbbeta}:=\sum_{c\in[m]}\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{\bbbeta,c}=\sum_{c\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}}\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{\bbbeta,c}.

The fact that the second equality holds in
Equation

5.3
, i.e. the 2-forms
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{\bbbeta,c}
vanish if
c\not\in J^{{\operatorname{D}}}_{\bbbeta}
, is
[
GLSB23
, Equation (2.28)]
.

Remark 5.3
.

The convention of
[
GLSB23
]
is that the 2-form of a seed
\Sigma
is
\frac{1}{2}\Omega(\Sigma)
(compare
[
GLSB23
, Equation (1.3)]
with (
5.1
)). Thus
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{\bbbeta,c}
and
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{\bbbeta}
above differ from the 2-form in
[
GLSB23
]
by a factor of 2. However, the exchange matrices obtained in
[
GLSB23
]
and those defined here agree.
∎

5.3.
The weave 2-form

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be a Demazure weave associated with a braid word
\beta
. As in Theorem
4.19
, every edge
\mathtt{e}
of
\mathfrak{w}
is associated with a
u
-variable

(5.4)

u_{\mathtt{e}}=\prod_{c}(x_{c}^{\rm W})^{\nu_{c}(\mathtt{e})},

where the product is over all Lusztig cycles
c
. We now present the weave 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
, as in
[
CGG+25
, Sections 8&9]
, and describe it in terms of these
u
-variables. This description in terms of
u
-variables is a key step in
5.1
, to compare to the Deodhar form. Indeed, once the weave 2-form is expressed in terms of
u
-variables, we can use
Proposition

4.25
, which expresses the torus element
h^{+}
in terms of coroots evaluated at the
u
-variables, to obtain an expression for
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
which is much closer to the Deodhar 2-form as presented in
Section

5.2
.

\ldots
\eta
u_{1}
u_{2}
u_{3}
u_{4}
u_{5}
u_{6}
u_{7}
u_{l}
j_{1}
j_{2}
j_{3}
j_{4}
j_{5}
j_{6}
j_{7}
j_{l}

Figure 8
.
A horizontal slice
\eta
of the weave
\mathfrak{w}

Following
[
CGG+25
, Section 4.1]
, a generic horizontal slice
\eta
of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
is a horizontal cut that intersects the weave
\mathfrak{w}
without passing through any vertices, as indicated by the dashed line in Figure
8
. The edges intersecting
\eta
are colored
j_{1},\ldots,j_{l}
from left to right, and we denote by
u_{1},\ldots,u_{l}
their associated
u
-variables. Let
{\bf j}=s_{j_{1}}\ldots s_{j_{l}}
be the corresponding word in simple reflections (note that
{\bf j}
is not necessarily reduced), and recall the sequence of coroots
\chi_{1}^{\bf j},\ldots,\chi_{l}^{\bf j}
defined in (
4.14
), whose norms are given by the symmetrizer
d_{j_{i}}=||\chi_{j_{i}}||^{2}
. Similarly, define the sequence of roots

(5.5)

\alpha_{k}^{\bf j}:=s_{j_{l}}s_{j_{l-1}}\cdots s_{j_{k+1}}\cdot\alpha_{j_{k}},\qquad\mbox{where }1\leq k\leq l.

For every trivalent vertex
c
of
\mathfrak{w}
with associated Lusztig cycle
\nu_{c}
, we consider the Langlands dual Lusztig cycle, cf.
[
CGG+25
, Section 6.6]
:

(5.6)

\nu^{\vee}_{c}(i)=\nu_{c}(i)d_{j_{i}}d_{c}^{-1},

where
d_{c}:=d_{j}
if
c
is colored by
j\in I
.

Following
[
CGG+25
, Section 6.1.(38)]
, the intersections between Lusztig cycles and their duals at a given slice
\eta
can be defined as

\sharp_{\eta}\left(\nu^{\vee}_{c}\cdot\nu_{d}\right):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,k=1}^{l}{\rm sign}(k-i)\nu_{c}^{\vee}(i)\nu_{d}(k)\cdot(\alpha_{i}^{\bf j},\chi_{k}^{\bf j}).

These intersections determine the weave 2-form
\Omega^{W}_{\eta}
at the given slice
\eta
, by declaring

(5.7)

\Omega_{\eta}^{\rm W}:=\sum_{c,d}d_{c}\cdot\sharp_{\eta}\left(\nu^{\vee}_{c}\cdot\nu_{d}\right)~\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}\wedge\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{d}.

Such weave 2-form
\Omega^{W}_{\eta}
can be expressed in terms of the
u
-variables as follows:

Lemma 5.4
.

In the notation above, the weave 2-form
\Omega^{W}_{\eta}
equals

(5.8)

\Omega_{\eta}^{\rm W}=\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq l}d_{j_{i}}(\alpha_{i}^{\bf j},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})\dlog u_{i}\wedge\dlog u_{k}.

Proof.

By using (
5.4
), we can describe the
u
-variables in the right hand side of (
5.8
) in terms of the
x
-variables. This leads to the following description for the 2-form:

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq l}d_{j_{i}}(\alpha_{i}^{\bf j},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})\dlog u_{i}\wedge\dlog u_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq l}d_{j_{i}}(\alpha_{i}^{\bf j},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})\left(\sum_{c,d}\nu_{c}(i)\nu_{d}(k)\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}\wedge\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{d}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{c,d}d_{c}\left(\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq l}\nu^{\vee}_{c}(i)\nu_{d}(k)(\alpha_{i}^{\bf j},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})\right)\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}\wedge\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{c,d}d_{c}\left(\sum_{i,k=1}^{l}{\rm sign}(k-i)\nu_{c}^{\vee}(i)\nu_{d}(k)(\alpha_{i}^{\bf j},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})\right)\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}\wedge\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Omega_{\eta}^{\rm W},

as required. In the above the second equality uses (
5.6
).
∎

Example 5.5

(Type
G_{2}
)
.

Let
{\bf j}=s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}
, where
\alpha_{1}
is the longer simple root, with symmetrizers
d_{1}=1
and
d_{2}=3
. The sequence of coroots
\chi_{i}^{\bf j}
is

\chi_{2},~~\chi_{1}+\chi_{2},~~3\chi_{1}+2\chi_{2},~~2\chi_{1}+\chi_{2},~~3\chi_{1}+\chi_{2},~~\chi_{1}.

The sequence of roots
\alpha_{i}^{\bf j}
is

\alpha_{2},~~\alpha_{1}+3\alpha_{2},~~\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2},~~2\alpha_{1}+3\alpha_{2},~~\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},~~\alpha_{1},

and note that the pairing between roots and coroots gives

(\alpha_{1},\chi_{1})=2,~(\alpha_{1},\chi_{2})=-3,~(\alpha_{2},\chi_{1})=-1,~(\alpha_{2},\chi_{2})=2.

Thus the
6\times 6
symmetric matrix
M=(m_{ik})
,
m_{ik}:=d_{j_{i}}(\alpha_{i}^{\bf j},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})
, reads

(5.9)

\begin{pmatrix}6&3&3&0&-3&-3\\
3&2&3&1&0&-1\\
3&3&6&3&3&0\\
0&1&3&2&3&1\\
-3&0&3&3&6&3\\
-3&-1&0&1&3&2\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore

\Omega_{\eta}^{\rm W}=\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq 6}m_{ik}\dlog u_{i}\wedge\dlog u_{k},

which in part illustrates
Lemma

5.4
in this example.∎

Equation

5.7
defines the weave 2-form at a given slice
\eta
of a weave
\mathfrak{w}
. The global weave 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
is obtained by scanning the weave downwards, accounting for the local contributions of these sliced 2-forms
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\eta}
as the slice
\eta
moves past the weave vertices. Let us now consider the contributions of the internal vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
to the global weave form
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
: starting with the 3-valent case, then 4-valent and 6-valent, and finally the 8-valent and 12-valent cases.

v

5.3.1.
Local contribution at 3-valents

First, let
v
be a trivalent vertex, as in Figure
9
. Consider the following local contribution at such trivalent vertex
v
:

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}:=\sum_{c,d}d_{c}\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
\nu_{c}^{\vee}(1)&\nu_{c}^{\vee}(2)&\nu_{c}^{\vee}(3)\\
\nu_{d}(1)&\nu_{d}(2)&\nu_{d}(3)\\
\end{matrix}\right|\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}\wedge\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{d}

In line with
Lemma

5.4
, we can express this local contribution in terms of
u
-variables:

Lemma 5.6
.

In the notation above, if the three edges incident to
v
are colored by
i
, then

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}=2d_{i}\left({\dlog u_{1}}\wedge{\dlog u_{2}}+{\dlog u_{2}}\wedge{\dlog u_{3}}+{\dlog u_{3}}\wedge{\dlog u_{1}}\right).

Proof.

Let
e
and
f
be edges of
\mathfrak{w}
with color
i
. Then

\displaystyle 2d_{i}\dlog u_{e}\wedge\dlog u_{f}

\displaystyle=2d_{i}\sum_{c,d}\nu_{c}(e)\nu_{d}(f)\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}\wedge\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{d}

\displaystyle=d_{i}\sum_{c,d}\left(\nu_{c}(e)\nu_{d}(f)-\nu_{c}(f)\nu_{d}(e)\right)\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}\wedge\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{d}

\displaystyle=\sum_{c,d}d_{c}\left|\begin{matrix}\nu_{c}^{\vee}(e)&\nu_{c}^{\vee}(f)\\
\nu_{d}(e)&\nu_{d}(f)\\
\end{matrix}\right|\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}\wedge\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{d}.

The rest of the equality follows from direct computation.
∎

For

a
