\Omega_{v}^{\rm W}=0.

That is, 4-valent vertices do
not
contribute to the weave 2-form.

5.3.3.
Local contribution at 6-valents

Let
v
be a

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}:=\sum_{c,d}\frac{d_{c}}{2}\left(\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
\nu_{c}^{\vee}(1)&\nu_{c}^{\vee}(2)&\nu_{c}^{\vee}(3)\\
\nu_{d}(1)&\nu_{d}(2)&\nu_{d}(3)\\
\end{matrix}\right|-\left|\begin{matrix}1&1&1\\
\nu_{c}^{\vee}(1^{\prime})&\nu_{c}^{\vee}(2^{\prime})&\nu_{c}^{\vee}(3^{\prime})\\
\nu_{d}(1^{\prime})&\nu_{d}(2^{\prime})&\nu_{d}(3^{\prime})\\
\end{matrix}\right|\right)\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{c}\wedge\dlog x^{\operatorname{W}}_{d}.

Here the edges incident to

i
and
j
, where
i,j
are joined by a simple edge in the Dynkin diagram. Hence, their corresponding symmetrizers coincide,
d_{i}=d_{j}
. In line with Lemmas
5.4
and
5.6
, the expression in terms of
u
-variables is:

Lemma 5.7
.

In the notation above, we have

\displaystyle\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}=

\displaystyle d_{i}\left({\dlog u_{1}}\wedge{\dlog u_{2}}+{\dlog u_{2}}\wedge{\dlog u_{3}}+{\dlog u_{3}}\wedge{\dlog u_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle-d_{i}\left({\dlog u_{1}^{\prime}}\wedge{\dlog u_{2}^{\prime}}+{\dlog u_{2}^{\prime}}\wedge{\dlog u_{3}^{\prime}}+{\dlog u_{3}^{\prime}}\wedge{\dlog u_{1}^{\prime}}\right).

Proof.

It follows by the same calculation as in the proof of Lemma
5.6
.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

By
[
CGG+25
, Lemma 4.28]
, if
v
is not a trivalent vertex, then
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}
is given by the difference between the contributions of its top and bottom slices. Therefore Lemma
5.7
can also be understood as a consequence of Lemma
5.4
.∎

5.3.4.
Local contribution at 8-valent and 12-valents

For vertices of valency 8 and 12, the 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}
is constructed via unfolding and reduction to the simply-laced case. By
[
CGG+25
, Lemma 4.28]
,
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}
for such a vertex

can be expressed as the difference between the contributions of its top and bottom slices. By Lemma
5.4
, the 2-forms for these top and bottom slices can themselves can be expressed in terms of the
u
-variables. This leads to an expression for the local contributions
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}
in terms of the
u
-variables. For completeness, we provide the explicit formulas below.

5.3.5.
The global weave 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}

Following
[
CGG+25
, Definition 4.26]
, the weave 2-form
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
is defined as the sum

(5.10)

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}:=\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bf bottom}+\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\mathrm{i}}.

The first summand of
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
is the contribution of the bottom slice
\eta
of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
, where we denoted
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bf bottom}:=\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\eta}
, as introduced in
Equation

5.7
.
5
5

5

In this case,
\eta
encodes a reduced word for
\delta(\beta)
.
The second summand of
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
comes from the contributions of all the internal vertices
v
of
\mathfrak{w}
, where we denoted

(5.11)

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\mathrm{i}}:=\sum_{v}\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v},

with the local contributions
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}
at internal vertices
v
as in Subsections
5.3.1
,
5.3.2
,
5.3.3
and
5.3.4
above.

5.4.
Proof of
5.1
(Equality of 2-forms)

As discussed in
Remark

5.2
, it suffices to consider the case where
\bbbeta=i_{m}\cdots i_{1}
consists only of positive letters. Then
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}=i_{1}\cdots i_{m}
and
3.19
implies that the corresponding weave
\mathfrak{w}=\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\ddot{\mathbf{s}}{(\bbbeta))}
is right inductive, that is, all weave lines start on the right side of
\vartriangle
.

Both 2-forms
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
and
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}
are defined as a sum of local contributions:

(1)

In
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}
, there is a contribution
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{c}
for each index
c\in[m]
.

(2)

In
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
, there is a contribution
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}
for each vertex of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
and a separate contribution for the bottom of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
. The bottom contribution can be considered as a contribution of the bottom side of
\vartriangle
in the description of
Section

3.3
.

For each depth
d\in[0,m]
, we will now show the identity

(5.12)

\sum_{c\leq d}\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}_{{\overline{c}}}=\left(\sum_{v\mbox{\tiny{ s.t.~ }}d(v)\leq d}\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}\right)+\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\eta_{d}}.

The sum on the right of
Equation

5.12
is taken over vertices
v
that have depth
d(v)
at most
d
, and
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\eta_{d}}
is the 2-form defined in
Equation

5.8
for the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d}
obtained by truncating
\mathfrak{w}
at depth
d
.
Equation

5.12
implies
5.1
for maximal depth, as the left hand side coincides with
\Omega^{\operatorname{D}}
and the right hand side coincides with
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}
, by the defining
Equation

5.10
. Thus the remainder of the proof of
5.1
is now focused on showing
Equation

5.12
. We write
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d}}
for the right hand side of (
5.12
) to ease notation.

We prove
Equation

5.12
by induction on the depth
d
,
d\in[0,m]
. The base case is
d=0
: since both sides of (
5.12
) are
0
, we have the required equality in the base case. We now assume that (
5.12
) is valid for depth
d
, which is our inductive hypothesis, and proceed to show that this implies (
5.12
) for depth
d+1
.

Since we are assuming that
\bbbeta
has only positive letters, we have that
\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d+1}=\beta_{d}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}i
for some letter
i\in I
where, as in
Definition

3.7
,
\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d}
is the braid word obtained by truncating the double string
\ddot{\mathbf{s}}
after
d
steps. Let us use again the notation
w_{d}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}:=\delta(\beta^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d})
for the Demazure product, and split the analysis into the two possible cases:
w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d+1}=w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d}
, where the Demazure product remains equal from depth
d
to
d+1
, and
w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d+1}=w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d}s_{i}
, where it changes. The first case, which accounts for the trivalent vertices, is what requires most attention.

Case 1:
w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d+1}=w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d}
. By
[
CGG+25
, Corollary 4.29]
, braid moves at the bottom of a weave do not change the corresponding 2-form. Thus, after applying a sequence of braid moves, we can and do assume that the rightmost bottom leg of the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d}
is colored by
i
. Following
Figure

12
, let
{\rm A}_{0},\ldots,{\rm A}_{d+1}\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U}_{+}
be a sequence of decorated flags such that
{\rm A}_{0}=\mathsf{U}_{+}
and
{\rm A}_{k-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{k}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm A}_{k}
for
k\in[1,d+1]
, and let
\dot{w}\in\mathsf{G}
be a lift of
w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d}
. By
Lemma

7.1
, the underlying flags of the pair
({\rm A}_{0},{\rm A}_{d})
are in relative position
w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d}
, so there is a unique
h\in H
such that

(5.13)

({\rm A}_{0},{\rm A}_{d})=(g\mathsf{U}_{+},g\dot{w}h\mathsf{U}_{+}),\quad\mbox{for some }g\in\mathsf{G}.

Let
\eta_{d}
and
\eta_{d+1}
be the bottom slices of
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d}
and
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d+1}
respectively.
Suppose that the edges intersecting
\eta_{d}
are colored
j_{1},\ldots,j_{l-1},j_{l}=i
, reading left to right, and let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{l}
be the
u
-variables associated with the slice
\eta_{d}
. By
Proposition

4.25
, the torus element
h\in H
can be expressed as the product

h=\prod_{k=1}^{l}\chi_{k}^{\bf j}(u_{k})\in H.

Let
x
be the cluster variable associated to the south-most trivalent vertex
v
of the weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d+1}
, drawn in red in
Figure

12
. Since we are in the case
w_{d}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}=w_{d+1}^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}
, we have that

(5.14)

({\rm A}_{0},{\rm A}_{d+1})=(g\mathsf{U}_{+},g\dot{w}h^{\prime}\mathsf{U}_{+}),\quad\mbox{for some }g\in\mathsf{G},

as in
Equation

5.13
but for depth
d+1
. By using
Proposition

4.25
again, this new torus element
h^{\prime}\in H
at depth
d+1
is

h^{\prime}=\left(\prod_{k=1}^{l-1}\chi_{k}^{\bf j}(u_{k})\right)\cdot\chi_{l}^{\bf j}(x)\in H.

{\rm A}_{0}
{\rm A}_{1}
{\rm A}_{2}
{\rm A}_{3}
{\rm A}_{4}
{\rm A}_{d}
{\rm A}_{d+1}
i_{1}

\cdots

\bullet

\ldots
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d}

\eta_{d}

Figure 12
.
The weave
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d+1}
, with depth

. It depicts the (red) trivalent vertex at the bottom right, labeled with color

As in

above, we note that we have the equalities

L_{d,i}=\frac{1}{2}\dlog\alpha_{i}(h),\qquad L_{d+1,i}=\frac{1}{2}\dlog\alpha_{i}(h^{\prime}).

The inductive step needed to prove
Equation

5.12
in this case
w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d+1}=w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d}
is a now consequence of the following lemma:

Lemma 5.10
.

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d+1}}-\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d}}=2d_{i}L_{d,i}\wedge L_{d+1,i}.

Proof.

By definition, we have

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d+1}}-\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d}}=\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\eta_{d+1}}-\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\eta_{d}}+\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}.

In the notation above, the
u
-variables for the edges adjacent to
v
are
u_{l}
,
x
, and 1. By Lemma
5.6
, the local contribution of the trivalent vertex
v
in the
u
-variables is

(5.15)

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{v}=2d_{i}\dlog u_{l}\wedge\dlog x.

Note that
d_{j_{k}}(\alpha_{k}^{\bf j},\chi_{l}^{\bf j})=d_{j_{l}}(\alpha_{l}^{\bf j},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})=d_{i}(\alpha_{l}^{\bf j},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})
and use Lemma
5.4
to obtain the equality

(5.16)

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\eta_{d+1}}-\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\eta_{d}}=\sum_{k=1}^{l-1}d_{i}(\alpha_{l}^{\bf j},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})\,\dlog u_{k}\wedge\left(\dlog x-\dlog u_{l}\right).

Since
\alpha_{l}^{\bf j}=\alpha_{i}
and
(\alpha_{l}^{\bf j},\chi_{l}^{\bf j})=2
, adding (
5.15
) and (
5.16
) yields

(5.17)

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d+1}}-\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d}}=\sum_{k=1}^{l}d_{i}(\alpha_{i},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})\,\dlog u_{k}\wedge\left(\dlog x-\dlog u_{l}\right)

for the left hand side of the equality in the statement, that we are trying to establish. Its right hand side can be re-written using the following equalities:

L_{d,i}=\frac{1}{2}\dlog\alpha_{i}(h)=\frac{1}{2}\dlog\alpha_{i}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{l}\chi_{k}^{\bf j}(u_{k})\right)=\frac{1}{2}\dlog\prod_{k=1}^{l}u_{k}^{(\alpha_{i},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{l}(\alpha_{i},\chi_{k}^{\bf j})\dlog u_{k},

and there the difference of the 1-forms
L_{d+1,i},L_{d,i}
in depths
(d+1)
and
d
reads

(5.18)

L_{d+1,i}-L_{d,i}=\frac{1}{2}\dlog\alpha_{i}(h^{\prime}h^{-1})=\frac{1}{2}\dlog\alpha_{i}(\chi_{i}(xu_{l}^{-1}))=\dlog x-\dlog u_{l}.

Therefore, Equations (
5.17
) and (
5.18
) together imply the required equality:

\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d+1}}-\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d}}=2d_{i}L_{d,i}\wedge(L_{d+1,i}-L_{d,i})=2d_{i}L_{d,i}\wedge L_{d+1,i}.\qed

By the definition of the Deodhar
2
-form,
Lemma

5.10
implies that
Equation

5.12
holds in this first case. To conclude
5.1
, it remains to establish the second case, which is done as follows.

Case 2:
w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d+1}=w^{\ddot{\mathbf{s}}}_{d}s_{i}
.
In this case, the weaves
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d}
and
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d+1}
define the same exchange matrix, so we directly obtain
\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d+1}}=\Omega^{\operatorname{W}}_{\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}_{d}}
. That is, the right-hand side of (
5.12
) remains unchanged when passing from depth
d
to depth
d+1
. By
[
GLSB23
, Corollary 2.26]
, the same is true for the left-hand side. This concludes the argument in this case, and the proof of
5.1
.∎

5.5.
Proof of the
Main Theorem

Let
\varphi:R(\bbbeta)\longrightarrow X(\bbbeta^{(-|+)})
be the isomorphism in
Proposition

2.8
. Then Part (1) is
Proposition

3.17
, Part (2) is
4.27
and Part (3) is
5.1
.∎

6.
Type A: weaves and 3D plabic graphs

Cluster algebras related to a Lie group
\mathsf{G}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}_{n}
, a.k.a. Type A, are related to particularly beautiful combinatorics. Specifically, the connection between plabic graphs and cluster algebras has been fruitful, cf.
[
FWZ
, Chapter 7]
, and see also
[
KLS13
]
or
[
CGGS21
]
and references therein for equivalent combinatorial data in the case of positroid varieties. Even if the combinatorics of weaves, as developed in
[
CZ22
,
CW24
,
CGG+25
]
, work for any
\mathsf{G}
and provide a unifying combinatorial setting for all Lie types, it is valuable to relate weaves in a specific Lie type to previously studied combinatorics. For instance,
[
CLSBW23
, Section 3]
shows how to transition from a reduced plabic graph to a weave in Type A, by the procedure of iterated
T
-shifts. In the Deodhar side,
[
GLSBS22
]
developed the combinatorics of 3D plabic graphs to study the Deodhar cluster structures in the specific case of double braid varieties in Type A. In this section, we further develop two aspects of our comparison between weave and Deodhar combinatorics in this Type A case:

(1)

The combinatorial relation between 3D plabic graphs and weaves, in
Section

6.1
.

(2)

The relation between monotone multicurves, which are associated to 3D plabic graphs, and the tropical rules for Lusztig cycle propagation in weaves,
Section

6.2
.

The geometric relation between 3D plabic graphs and weaves, building on and generalizing
[
CL22
, Theorem 3.1]
and
[
CLSBW23
, Section 5]
, is developed in
[
CKW
]
.

Remark 6.1
.

A technical point: the 3D plabic graph cluster seeds of
[
GLSBS22
]
and the Deodhar cluster seeds of
[
GLSB23
]
differ by an overall minus sign in their exchange 2-form, i.e. their quivers are opposite from each other in their respective conventions.∎

6.1.
Weaves for 3D plabic graphs

Let us focus on Type A, setting
\mathsf{G}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}_{n}
, e.g.
\mathsf{G}=\operatorname{SL}_{n}
. In
[
CGG+25
]
, cluster algebras are constructed using
weaves
. In
[
GLSBS22
]
, cluster algebras are constructed using
3D plabic graphs
. The object of this subsection is to give a combinatorial procedure that inputs a 3D plabic graph and outputs a weave, up to equivalence, in a manner compatible with the comparison between the two works
[
CGG+25
,
GLSBS22
]
as presented above. This procedure is consistent with the main comparison in the manuscript, e.g. equivalence class of weaves obtained from a 3D plabic graph is such that the resulting cluster algebras coincide, and so does the initial seed, cf. the
Main Theorem
.

6.1.1.
Inductive weaves and red projections of 3D plabic graphs

Let us present a combinatorial construction which inputs a 3D plabic graph associated to a braid word in the alphabet
I
and outputs an inductive weave, up to equivalence. Consider a 3D plabic graph
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
, as defined in
[
GLSBS22
, Section 3]
. Following
[
GLSBS22
, Section 6.4]
, we can and do assume that
u=w_{\circ}
. For now, we suppose that
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
only uses the alphabet
I
, i.e. in this section
\bbbeta=\bbbeta^{+}
denotes a braid word in the alphabet
I
of the form
s_{i_{\ell}}\ldots s_{i_{1}}
. Note that then
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{\ell}}
.

As a consequence, the red projection of such 3D plabic graph
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
, cf.
[
GLSBS22
, Section 3.2]
, is such that all short bridges have a black dot on top and it has no long bridges. In particular, we can draw
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
as a plabic fence, which contains the solid crossings in the language of
[
GLSBS22
, Section 3]
, with additional hollow crossings interspersed as dictated by
u
. By definition, a weave class is an equivalence class of weaves, defined as a set of weaves up to weave equivalence, cf.
[
CGG+25
, Section 4.2]
or
[
CZ22
, Theorem 4.2]
.

We associate a weave
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
to
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
by scanning the 3D plabic graph right-to-left, drawing certain local models to construct
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
as we scan each crossing (solid or hollow) of
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
. This weave
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
is well-defined up to weave equivalence, i.e.
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
is a well-defined weave class. We draw the weave horizontally as in
[
CW24
]
, in line with the 3D plabic graphs of
[
GLSBS22
]
, which are also drawn horizontally. (Weaves are drawn vertically in
[
CGG+25
]
and in this manuscript, cf. Subsection
6.1.4
below for further discussion.)

The local model we insert for
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
depends on whether the crossing we scan in
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
is solid or hollow:

(1)

If the crossing in in
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
is solid, the weave constructed thus far acquires a new trivalent vertex. The procedure is closely related to
[
CW24
, Section 3.3.2]
. Intuitively, a new strand appears from the top vertically and attaches to the top (and left) of the existing weave with a trivalent vertex.

This typically requires introducing hexavalent vertices to the left of the existing weave, before this new trivalent is created. These hexavalent vertices are necessary so as to modify the existing weave to a weave whose top (left) weave line matches the
I
-letter of the new strand appearing vertically from the top. In that situation, a trivalent vertex of the corresponding
I
-letter can then be attached to that top weave line.

(2)

If the crossing in
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
is hollow, the weave does not acquire any new trivalent vertices. Intuitively, a new strand appears from the top vertically and takes a sharp left turn to become horizontal. At the leftmost point, the new strand union the existing weave (below the new strand) constitute the new piece of horizontal weave to the left.

Let us now provide the necessary details, presented in Subsections
6.1.2
,
6.1.3
and
6.1.4
. The weave
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
, up to weave equivalence, is then defined in Definition
6.8
below. We use the convention from
[
GLSBS22
]
that the positive alphabet corresponds to bridges with white on top, which is opposite to the convention in
[
CW24
]
.

6.1.2.
Weaves when scanning solid crossings

Let
w\in S_{n}
be any permutation and
w=s_{j_{r}}\cdots s_{j_{1}}=(j_{r},\ldots,j_{1})
a fixed reduced word of length
r=\ell(w)
. Consider the following local weaves
\mathfrak{n}(w),\mathfrak{c}^{\uparrow}(w)
, as in
[
CW24
, Section 3.3]
.

Definition 6.2
.

The weave
\mathfrak{n}(w)
is given by
n
horizontal parallel weave lines such that the
k
-th strand, counting from the bottom, is labeled by the transposition
s_{j_{k}}
,
k\in[1,r]
. By definition, the weave
\mathfrak{c}^{\uparrow}(w)
is the weave
\mathfrak{n}(w)
with a trivalent vertex added at the top strand – labeled by
s_{j_{r}}
– such that the third leg of this trivalent vertex is a vertical ray starting at the top strand and continuing upwards.
\Box

Figure
13
depicts two instances of the weaves
\mathfrak{c}^{\uparrow}(w)
in Definition
6.2
.

Figure 13
.
The weave
\mathfrak{c}^{\uparrow}(s_{1}s_{2}s_{1})
on the left and
\mathfrak{c}^{\uparrow}(s_{2}s_{1}s_{2})
on the right. Here weave lines labeled with
s_{1}
(resp. with
s_{2}
) are depicted in blue (resp. red). We have also highlighted a portion of the weave that can be thought to correspond to
\mathfrak{n}(w)
, before adding any trivalent in
Definition

6.2
.

Suppose that we are scanning
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
, equivalently
\bbbeta
, right-to-left and it is time to add a solid crossing. Let
\beta_{j-1}
be the braid subword of
\bbbeta
to the right of the
j
th crossing
i_{j}
of
\bbbeta
,
j
th counting from the right; so
\beta_{0}
is empty and
\beta_{\ell}=\bbbeta
. Denote by
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
the weave constructed thus far: at its left end,
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
consists of a series of horizontal weave edges which spell a reduced word
\delta_{j-1}
for the Demazure product
\delta(\beta_{j-1})
of
\beta_{j-1}
.

Remark 6.3
.

In our convention, this reduced word
\delta_{j-1}
for
\delta(\beta_{j-1})
is spelled right-to-left when the weave lines at the left end of
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
are read bottom-to-top; this matches how the letters of
w
correspond to weaves lines of
\mathfrak{n}(w)
in
Definition

6.2
.
\Box

The fact that this new crossing is solid (instead of hollow) is equivalent to the condition that the Demazure product of the braid
\beta_{j-1}
does
not
change when we add
i_{j}
to its left. That is,
\delta(s_{i_{j}}\beta_{j-1})=\delta(\beta_{j-1})
and so there is a reduced word for
\delta(\beta_{j-1})
which begins (from the left) with
s_{i_{j}}
.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
\beta_{j-1}
and
i_{j}\in I
be such that
\delta(s_{i_{j}}\beta_{j-1})=\delta(\beta_{j-1})
. Let
\delta_{j-1}
be a given reduced word for
\delta(\beta_{j-1})
and
\sigma_{i_{j}}(\delta_{j-1})
a (possibly different) reduced word for
\delta(\beta_{j-1})
starting with
s_{i_{j}}
at its left. By definition, the weave class
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
is the unique weave class represented by an homonymous weave
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
such that:

(1)

The weave
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
contains only 4-valent and 6-valent vertices.

(2)

The right end of
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
is
\delta_{j-1}
, i.e. it spells
\delta_{j-1}
when read bottom-to-top.

(3)

The left end of
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
is
\sigma_{i_{j}}(\delta_{j-1})
.
\Box

The fact that the three properties in Definition
6.4
determine a unique weave class follows from
[
CGGS24
, Theorem 4.12]
. The weave vertices of
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
correspond to a sequence of braid moves (if 6-valent) and commutations (if 4-valent) that are applied to the reduced word
\delta_{j-1}
to obtain
\sigma_{i_{j}}(\delta_{j-1})
. See Figure
14

for four examples of
Definition

6.4
. The theory of higher syzygies for permutations and weave equivalence moves are such that different choices of such sequences of braid moves yield equivalent weaves, cf.
[
CGGS24
, Theorem 4.12.(a)]
or
[
CGG+25
, Section 4.2]
. Note also that there are no trivalent vertices in
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
.

Figure 14
.
The weaves
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
in
Definition

6.4
. Here we abbreviated
w_{0,4}=s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}
for this reduced word for
w_{0}\in S_{3}
. In this example, we have chosen the Demazure products
\delta_{j-1}
to be
w_{0}
, as it is the most interesting case: we emphasize that
\delta_{j-1}
can be any permutation.

Remark 6.5
.

(1) If
\delta_{j-1}
starts with
s_{i_{j}}
on its left, then we can choose
\sigma_{i_{j}}(\delta_{j-1})=\delta_{j-1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
is represented by the identity weave: all horizontal lines spelling
\delta_{j-1}
bottom-to-top and with no vertices at all.

(2) More generally, a representative for
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
can be chosen with a minimal number of weave vertices, by choosing the sequence of braid moves from
\delta_{j-1}
to a word starting with
s_{i_{j}}
in a greedy manner. In practice, this is often the choice of representative.

(3) The condition
\delta(s_{i_{j}}\beta_{j-1})=\delta(\beta_{j-1})
in
Definition

6.4
is necessary to construct such weave
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
. Indeed, if the Demazure product increased, i.e.
\delta(s_{i_{j}}\beta_{j-1})=s_{i_{j}}\delta(\beta_{j-1})
, there would be no reduced word for
\delta(\beta_{j-1})
starting with
s_{i_{j}}
, so
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
could not exist.
\Box

Definition 6.6

(Weave class for a solid crossing)
.

The weave class
\mathfrak{s}(\mathbb{G}_{u_{j-1},\beta_{j-1}},j;\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1}))
is given by the horizontal concatenation of
\mathfrak{c}^{\uparrow}(\gamma_{j-1})
,
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
and
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
, where
\mathfrak{c}^{\uparrow}(\gamma_{j-1})
is concatenated to the left of
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
and
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
is concatenated to the left of
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
. Here we have assumed that:

(1)

Representatives of the weave classes
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
and
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
are chosen such that the right end of the (homonymous) weave representative
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
coincides with the left end of the weave representative
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
.

(2)

\gamma_{j-1}
denotes the reduced word spelled by the left end of the weave representative for
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
, which is a reduced word for
\delta(\beta_{j-1})
starting with
s_{i_{j}}
.
\Box

Intuitively described, the weave
\mathfrak{s}(\mathbb{G}_{u_{j-1},\beta_{j-1}},j;\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1}))
in
Definition

6.6
is obtained by starting with
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
, and then trying to attach a weave
s_{i_{j}}
-edge (coming from above) to the top of the leftmost part of
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
, which is what the piece
\mathfrak{c}^{\uparrow}(\gamma_{j-1})
does. The issue is that the top weave line on the left boundary of
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
might not be a weave
s_{i_{j}}
-line, and so we need to bring up a weave line on the left boundary of
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
to its top so that the top becomes a weave
s_{i_{j}}
-edge: this is what
\mathfrak{n}_{i_{j}}^{\uparrow}(\beta_{j-1})
achieves and thus it is inserted between
\mathfrak{c}^{\uparrow}(\gamma_{j-1})
, to its left, and
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
, to its right.

6.1.3.
Weaves when scanning hollow crossings

For hollow crossings, we need a different weave than that in Definition
6.6
.
Remark

6.5
.(3) implies that some of the weave pieces constructed do not exist if the Demazure product increases, and indeed the required weaves for hollow crossing must be built differently. We use the same notation as in Subsection
6.1.2
, with
s_{i_{j}}
being the
j
th crossing of
\beta
reading left-to-right, so that
\beta_{j}=s_{i_{j}}\beta_{j-1}
.

Figure 15
.
Examples of the weaves for hollow crossings of the 3D plabic graph, as in Definition
6.7
. (Left) The reduced word for the Demazure product of
\beta_{j-1}
is
\delta(\beta_{j-1})=s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}
and the new crossing has index
i_{j}=1
. (Right) The reduced word is
\delta(\beta_{j-1})=s_{1}s_{3}s_{1}
and
i_{j}=2
. Here we use blue for
s_{1}
-edges of the weave, red for
s_{2}
-edges and green for
s_{3}
-edges.

Definition 6.7

(Weave class for a hollow crossing)
.

By definition,
\mathfrak{h}(\mathbb{G}_{u_{j-1},\beta_{j-1}},j;\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1}))
is the weave class obtained by choosing a weave representative of
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})=\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u_{j-1},\beta_{j-1}})
an adding a weave edge of
s_{i_{j}}
-type, disjoint and on top of
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
, drawn as follows. The right end of the new weave
s_{i_{j}}
-edge is a vertical ray to the left of all the crossings of
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
, the left end of this new
s_{i_{j}}
-edge is a horizontal strand on top of the left end of
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
.

By definition, the weave class
\mathfrak{h}(\mathbb{G}_{u_{j-1},\beta_{j-1}},j;\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1}))
is the weave equivalence class for any such (homonymous) weave
\mathfrak{h}(\mathbb{G}_{u_{j-1},\beta_{j-1}},j;\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1}))
.

\Box

Figure
15
depicts two instances of the weaves in Definition
6.2
. The weaves associated to hollow crossings have
no
trivalent vertices added to
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
, i.e. the number of trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{h}(\mathbb{G}_{u_{j-1},\beta_{j-1}},j;\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1}))
and
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
coincides. In a sense, it would
not
be possible to increase the number of trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
by using the vertical weave
s_{i_{j}}
-line: the Demazure product increases when adding
s_{i_{j}}
to the left of
\beta_{j-1}
and thus there is no weave representative of
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1})
such that the reduced word for the permutation at its left boundary has a weave
s_{i_{j}}
-line on top.

6.1.4.
The weave
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})

The required weave class
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
associated to
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
is defined by iteratively using Definitions
6.6
and
6.7
, as
\beta
is scanned right-to-left.

Definition 6.8

(Weave class of a 3D plabic graph)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
be a 3D plabic graph, with
\bbbeta=(i_{\ell},\ldots,i_{1})
a positive braid word in the positive alphabet of length
\ell=\ell(\bbbeta)
. By definition, the weave
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
is the final output of the following iterative algorithm:

(1)

Initialize with
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{0}):=\emptyset
, i.e. the starting weave is the empty weave.

(2)

For
j=1
until
j=\ell
:

-

If
i_{j}
is a solid crossing, then set
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j}):=\mathfrak{s}(\mathbb{G}_{u_{j-1},\beta_{j-1}},j;\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1}))
.

-

If
i_{j}
is a hollow crossing, then set
\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j}):=\mathfrak{h}(\mathbb{G}_{u_{j-1},\beta_{j-1}},j;\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{j-1}))
.

(3)

Set
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}):=\mathfrak{w}(\beta_{\ell})
.

The weave class
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
is said to be the weave class of the 3D plabic graph
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
.

\Box

See Figure
16
for an example of Definition
6.8
, drawing the weave
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
from
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
.

Figure 16
.
Example of a 3D plabic graph
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
(top) and its associated weave
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
(bottom), according to
Definition

6.8
. The braid word is
\beta=(1,2,2,\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}1}},2,\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}2}},1,\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}1}})
, where we marked the hollow crossings in purple. After the third hollow crossing, counting from the left, the Demazure product is maximal (equal to
s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}
) and thus no more hollow crossings can appear.

For visual ease, we have drawn
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
horizontally, as it is built right-to-left as we scan
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
right-to-left. This is the same drawing convention we used in
[
CW24
]
, where weaves were built for grid plabic graphs. The 3D plabic graphs in
[
GLSBS22
]
and in the present article are drawn horizontally. In contrast, Demazure weave are often drawn vertically, see e.g.
[
CGGS24
,
CGG+25
,
CLSBW23
]
, with a horizontal slice at the top spelling a braid word
\beta
and the horizontal slice at the bottom spelling its Demazure product. The geometric reason for that is that there is directionality in the Lagrangian cobordisms that were being initially studied when weaves were first introduced in
[
CZ22
]
. To bridge between these, we can rotate
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
to produce a vertical Demazure weave. In order to match conventions, so that
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
produces a right-inductive weave
[
CGG+25
]
, we always proceed as follows:

(1)

Reflect
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
along a line of slope one disjoint from the weave and to its right,

(2)

Extend the weave lines to the left of this rotated weave so that they all become vertical parallel lines pointing north.

An example is depicted in Figure
17
.

Figure 17
.
Reflecting the weave
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
to a right-inductive Demazure weave. The first step is the actual reflection along the dashed green line, and the second step is extending the weave lines to the right of the rotated weave so they reach the top. The extended weave lines are marked within the pink dashed box, for clarity.

Remark 6.9
.

To be technically precise, depending on how one draws the vertical top weave lines in
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
, the rotated weave is only equivalent to the right-inductive weave for the same word
\beta=\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
after a planar isotopy: the rotated weave might not be literally Demazure when scanned via horizontal slices top-to-bottom, but it will be so after a planar isotopy. Such a minor correction is always implicitly assumed to be implemented.
\Box

By construction, after having rotated them as above, the weaves representing
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
in Definition
6.8
are weave equivalent to right inductive weaves, the latter being defined in
[
CGG+25
, Section 4.3]
. Indeed, the weaves
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
in
Definition

6.8
are such that each trivalent vertex of their rotation has its incident top-right weave line go directly to the top of the weave: this characterizes right inductive weaves. We record this fact in the following statement:

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\bbbeta=\bbbeta^{+}
be a braid word in the alphabet
I
and
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
a 3D plabic graph. The right-inductive weave of
\bbbeta^{(-|+)}
is a representative of the weave class
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
. In particular, any weave representative of
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
is a Demazure weave.
\Box

It is a consequence of the proof of the
Main Theorem
that, under the comparison we established, the cluster seed associated to
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta})
in
[
CGG+25
]
coincides with the cluster seed associated to
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta}
in
[
GLSBS22
]
up to considering the opposite quiver, cf. e.g.
3.19
.(1) and
Remark

6.1
.

Remark 6.11
.

For a double braid word
\bbbeta^{-}
in the alphabet
-I
, there is an analogous procedure to build the corresponding weave class
\mathfrak{w}(\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta^{-}})
from its associated 3D plabic graph
\mathbb{G}_{u,\bbbeta^{-}}
. In this case, the strands for the weave would come from the bottom of the weave, instead of the top as in
Figure

16
. The resulting weave, after reflection, is left-inductive. This is in line with
3.19
.(2), and the corresponding version of
Lemma

6.10
also holds.∎

6.2.
Comparison between weave and Deodhar cocharacters in Type A

4.33
proved the equality

(6.1)

\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,{\overline{c}},{\overline{e}}}=\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)},c,e}

between the cocharacter
\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,{\overline{c}},{\overline{e}}}
, which encoded the order of vanishing of the minors
\Delta_{c,i}
on
\tilde{V}_{e}
, and the weave cocharacter
\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)},c,e}
, i.e. the coweight of the Lusztig datum associated to a double inductive weave. In this section, we give a direct combinatorial argument for
Equation

6.1
in Type
A
which does not make use of the torus element
h^{+}_{{\overline{c}}}
or the edge functions
u_{\mathtt{e}}
, which were both key in the proof of
4.33
. Rather, we use
monotone multicurves
on the Deodhar side, which directly encode the order of vanishing of grid minors along Deodhar hypersurfaces, and we use tropical Lusztig rules on the weave side. This type of propagation algorithm for cocharacters in terms of monotone multicurves did not quite feature in
[
GLSBS22
]
and, from the viewpoint of 3D plabic graphs, can be of combinatorial interest on its own. As in
Section

6.1
, the focus now is on Lie Type A, so
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}_{n}
, and the Weyl group
W(\mathsf{G})\cong S_{n}
can be identified with the symmetric group
S_{n}
.

6.2.1.
Monotone multicurves and Lusztig data

Following
[
GLSBS22
, Section 3.2]
, we define the
permutation diagram

\Gamma(u)\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
of a permutation
u\in S_{n}
as the set of dots with coordinates
(u(j),j)
for
j\in[n]
. Thus, multiplying
u
on the right (resp. left) by
s_{j}
corresponds to swapping rows (resp. columns)
j
and
j+1
, counting from the southwest corner.

Definition 6.12

(
[
GLSBS22
, Section 3.4]
)
.

A
monotone curve
is a curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
inside the permutation diagram
\Gamma(u)
whose endpoints are dots in
\Gamma(u)
and such that both coordinates of
\gamma
are strictly increasing functions on
[0,1]
. A
monotone multicurve
is a collection
\bm{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{k})
of monotone curves inside
\Gamma(u)
such that
\gamma_{i}(1)
is located strictly southwest of
\gamma_{i+1}(0)
for all
i\in[k-1]
.
\Box

Recall from
Definition

4.28
that a
Lusztig datum

[\mathbf{j},f]
is an equivalence class of weighted expressions. Our first goal is to associate a Lusztig datum to an arbitrary monotone multicurve
\bm{\gamma}
inside the permutation diagram
\Gamma(u)
. For that, we consider the
dual propagation moves
as shown in Figure
18
(B)
. The
propagation moves
of
[
GLSBS22
]
are shown in Figure
18
(A)
.

(a)

Propagation moves; cf.
[
GLSBS22
, Figure 9]
.

(b)

Dual propagation moves, used in
Section

6.2
.

Figure 18
.
Propagation moves (A) and dual propagation moves (B). In each case, the special moves are shown in the top row, with the non-special moves in the bottom row.

Let
j\in I
be such that
us_{j}>u
, that is, such that the dot
(u(j),j)
is southwest of the dot
(u(j+1),j+1)
. Define another multicurve
\vec{\rho}_{j}(\bm{\gamma})
drawn inside the permutation diagram
\Gamma(us_{j})
to be obtained from
\bm{\gamma}
by applying one of the moves shown in Figure
18
(B)
. (Exactly one of these moves applies in each case of the shape of the intersection of curves
\gamma_{j}
with the horizontal stripe
\mathbb{Z}\times]j,j+1[
,
depending on the configuration of
\bm{\gamma}
relative to the two dots. If
\bm{\gamma}
does not intersect this stripe, we let
\vec{\rho}_{i}(\bm{\gamma})
be obtained via an endpoint-preserving isotopy that moves the dot
(u(i),i)
up, the dot
(u(i+1),i+1)
down, and never passes a dot through a curve.) Strictly speaking, the resulting multicurve need not be monotone, but it is a collection of monotone curves, and we continue applying the dual propagation moves to each of them separately.

Similarly, for
j\in-I
such that
s_{-j}u>u
, the dual propagation moves
\vec{\rho}_{j}
are obtained from those shown in Figure
18
(B)
by reflecting each diagram across the line
y=x
.
Recall from
Definition

2.2
that the two conditions (
us_{j}>u
if
j\in I
,
s_{-j}u>u
if
j\in-I
) may be combined as
s_{j}^{-}us_{j}^{+}>u
.

Let
w=w_{\circ}u
and let
\mathbf{j}=j_{1}j_{2}\dots j_{\ell}\in(\pm I)^{\ell}
be a double braid word, where
\ell=\ell(w)
. We say that
\mathbf{j}
is a
double reduced word
for
w
if
\delta(\mathbf{j})=w
. By (
2.1
), this condition may be explicitly written as

(6.2)

w=s_{j_{\ell}^{\ast}}^{-}s_{j_{\ell-1}^{\ast}}^{-}\cdots s_{j_{1}^{\ast}}^{-}\cdot s_{j_{1}}^{+}s_{j_{2}}^{+}\cdots s_{j_{\ell}}^{+},\quad\text{i.e.,}\quad s_{j_{1}}^{-}s_{j_{2}}^{-}\cdots s_{j_{\ell}}^{-}\cdot u\cdot s_{j_{\ell}}^{+}s_{j_{\ell-1}}^{+}\cdots s_{j_{1}}^{+}=w_{\circ}.

The analogue of
Definition

4.28
reads as follows:

Definition 6.13
.

A
double weighted expression
for
w
is a pair
(\mathbf{j},f)
where
\mathbf{j}
is a double reduced word for
w
and
f:[\ell(w)]\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is an arbitrary weighting of the letters. We record the weights as exponents in angle brackets and write
(\mathbf{j},f)=j_{1}^{{\langle}f_{1}{\rangle}}j_{2}^{{\langle}f_{2}{\rangle}}\cdots j_{\ell}^{{\langle}f_{\ell}{\rangle}}
. By definition, a
double Lusztig datum

[\mathbf{j},f]
is an equivalence class of double weighted expressions modulo the following
double braid moves
:

(B1)

i^{{\langle}a{\rangle}}j^{{\langle}b{\rangle}}\leftrightarrow j^{{\langle}b{\rangle}}i^{{\langle}a{\rangle}}
if
\operatorname{sign}(i)\neq\operatorname{sign}(j)
;

(B2)

i^{{\langle}a{\rangle}}j^{{\langle}b{\rangle}}\leftrightarrow j^{{\langle}b{\rangle}}i^{{\langle}a{\rangle}}
if
\operatorname{sign}(i)=\operatorname{sign}(j)
and
|i-j|>1
;

(B3)

i^{{\langle}a{\rangle}}j^{{\langle}b{\rangle}}i^{{\langle}c{\rangle}}\leftrightarrow j^{{\langle}a^{\prime}{\rangle}}i^{{\langle}b^{\prime}{\rangle}}j^{{\langle}c^{\prime}{\rangle}}
if
\operatorname{sign}(i)=\operatorname{sign}(j)
and
|i-j|=1
, where

(6.3)

a^{\prime}=b+c-\min(a,c),\quad b^{\prime}=\min(a,c),\quad\text{and}\quad c^{\prime}=a+b-\min(a,c).

(B4)

j_{1}^{{\langle}f_{1}{\rangle}}\leftrightarrow(-j_{1}^{\ast})^{{\langle}f_{1}{\rangle}}
(changing the sign of the first letter).

∎

See
Section

8
for more details on the double braid moves. Note for now that all double reduced words for
w
are related by double braid moves (B1) – (B4). As we will see in
Definition

6.15
below, we will apply the moves
\vec{\rho}_{j}
for each letter
j
of
\mathbf{j}
in the reverse order, which is why
(B4)
involves the first letter of
\mathbf{j}
as opposed to the last.

Remark 6.14
.

Each double Lusztig datum
[\mathbf{j},f]
gives rise to a Lusztig datum similarly to
Definition

2.1
. Namely, let
a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{p}
and
b_{1}<b_{2}<\cdots<b_{q}
be the indices of negative and positive letters in
\mathbf{j}
, respectively. Define the weighted expression

(\mathbf{j},f)^{(-|+)}:=(-j_{a_{p}}^{\ast})^{{\langle}f_{a_{p}}{\rangle}}\cdots(-j_{a_{1}}^{\ast})^{{\langle}f_{a_{1}}{\rangle}}\cdot j_{b_{1}}^{{\langle}f_{b_{1}}{\rangle}}\cdots j_{b_{\ell}}^{{\langle}f_{b_{\ell}}{\rangle}}

and let
[\mathbf{j},f]^{(-|+)}
be the associated Lusztig datum. In this manner, the moves
(B1)
and
(B4)
do not change
(\mathbf{j},f)^{(-|+)}
at all, while moves
(B2)
and
(B3)
translate into regular commutation and braid moves on
(\mathbf{j},f)^{(-|+)}
.∎

In the same manner that a weave defined a Lusztig datum, as in
Equation

4.17
and
Definition

4.29
, a monotone multicurve defines a double weighted expression as in
Definition

6.13
, as follows.

Definition 6.15
.

Let
\bm{\gamma}
be a monotone multicurve inside
\Gamma(u)
and consider a double reduced word
\mathbf{j}=j_{1}j_{2}\cdots j_{\ell}
for
w=w_{\circ}u
. The weighting
f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}:[\ell]\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is defined as follows.
We let
u^{(\ell)}:=u
,
u^{(\ell-1)}:=s_{j_{\ell}}^{-}us_{j_{\ell}}^{+}
, …,
u^{(0)}:=s_{j_{1}}^{-}s_{j_{2}}^{-}\cdots s_{j_{\ell}}^{-}\cdot u\cdot s^{+}_{j_{\ell}}s^{+}_{j_{\ell-1}}\cdots s_{j_{1}}^{+}=w_{\circ}
; cf. (
6.2
). Thus,
s_{j_{i}}^{-}u^{(i)}s_{j_{i}}^{+}>u^{(i-1)}
, so that a dual propagation move can be applied at each step. Set
\bm{\gamma}^{(\ell)}:=\bm{\gamma}
. For each
i=\ell,\ell-1,\dots,1
, we let
\bm{\gamma}^{(i-1)}:=\vec{\rho}_{j_{i}}(\bm{\gamma}^{(i)})
. If this dual propagation move was special then we set
f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}(i):=1
, otherwise set
f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}(i):=0
. By definition, the double weighted expression for
w
associated to
(\mathbf{j},\gamma)
is
(\mathbf{j},f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}})
.∎

Figure 19
.
Computing the weighting
f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}
for two monotone multicurves. This illustrates
Definition

6.15
in
Example

6.16
.

Example 6.16
.

Let
n=4
,
u=s_{3}
, and
\mathbf{j}=1\,(-2)\,3\,2\,(-1)
. For the purple monotone multicurve
\bm{\gamma}
in
Figure

19
, we have
(f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}(5),\dots,f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}(1))=(1,0,1,0,0)
, while for the green monotone multicurve
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
in
Figure

19
, we have
(f_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\mathbf{j}}(5),\dots,f_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\mathbf{j}}(1))=(0,0,0,1,0)
. Note that this uses both the moves in Figure
18
(B)
, for
\vec{\rho}_{j_{i}}
with
j_{i}\in I
, and their reflections across the line
y=x
, for
\vec{\rho}_{j_{i}}
with
j_{i}\in-I
.
∎

Proposition 6.17
.

The double Lusztig datum
[\mathbf{j},f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}]
does not depend on the choice of the double reduced word
\mathbf{j}
for
w=w_{\circ}u
.

In other words, if two double reduced words
\mathbf{j},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}
for
w
are related by moves
(B1)
–
(B4)
, then the weightings
f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}
and
f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}
are related by the corresponding tropical Lusztig moves from
Definition

6.13
.

Proof.

Consider the case of move
(B1)
:
i^{{\langle}a{\rangle}}j^{{\langle}b{\rangle}}\leftrightarrow j^{{\langle}b{\rangle}}i^{{\langle}a{\rangle}}
with
\operatorname{sign}(i)\neq\operatorname{sign}(j)
. Suppose that, say,
i\in I
and
j\in-I
. Let
A_{i},B_{i}
be the two dots involved in the move
\vec{\rho}_{i}
and
C_{j},D_{j}
be the two dots involved in the move
\vec{\rho}_{j}
. Thus,
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
are located in adjacent rows while
C_{j},D_{j}
are located in adjacent columns. Since
\mathbf{j}
is reduced, we cannot have
\{A_{i},B_{i}\}=\{C_{j},D_{j}\}
.
It is straightforward to check that regardless of the order in which we apply the two propagation moves, we have
b=1
if
\bm{\gamma}
contains a curve weakly to the right of
C_{j}
and weakly to the left of
D_{j}
, and
b=0
otherwise. Similarly,
a=1
if
\bm{\gamma}
contains a curve weakly above
A_{j}
and weakly below
B_{j}
, and
a=0
otherwise.

The case of move
(B2)
is clear, since for
j\in I
, the dual propagation move
\vec{\rho}_{j}
only affects the multicurve in the neighborhood of a horizontal strip between rows
j
and
j+1
.

Figure 20
.
Checking
Proposition

6.17
for move
(B3)
.

Suppose now that
\mathbf{j}
and
\mathbf{j}^{\prime}
differ by a braid move
(B3)
:
i^{{\langle}a{\rangle}}j^{{\langle}b{\rangle}}i^{{\langle}c{\rangle}}\leftrightarrow j^{{\langle}a^{\prime}{\rangle}}i^{{\langle}b^{\prime}{\rangle}}j^{{\langle}c^{\prime}{\rangle}}
with, say,
i,j\in I
and
j=i+1
. In this case, the result boils down to a case analysis. There are
26
possibilities for how
\bm{\gamma}
may be located relatively to the dots in rows
i,i+1,i+2
; see
[
GLSBS22
, Figure 20]
. A quick check confirms that in each case, the resulting weightings on
(i,i+1,i)
and
(i+1,i,i+1)
(which always take values in
\{0,1\}
) are related by the tropical Lusztig moves. For
5
of these
26
possibilities, the verification is performed in
Figure

20
: in the top (resp. bottom) row, the weightings
(f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}(3),f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}(2),f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}(1))\leftrightarrow(f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}(3),f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}(2),f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}(1))
given by
(1,0,0)\leftrightarrow(0,0,1)
(resp.
(0,1,0)\leftrightarrow(1,0,1)
) are indeed related by the tropical Lusztig relations (
6.3
). The remaining
21
cases are checked similarly.

For move
(B4)
, observe that the move
\vec{\rho}_{j_{1}}
is applied last, and the monotone multicurve
\bm{\gamma}^{(1)}
from
Definition

6.15
is drawn inside the permutation diagram
\Gamma(u^{(1)})
with
u^{(1)}=s_{j_{1}}^{-}w_{\circ}s_{j_{1}}^{+}
. Assume without loss of generality that
j_{1}\in I
. Then
u^{(1)}=w_{\circ}s_{j_{1}}=s_{j_{1}^{*}}w_{\circ}
. Therefore, regardless of whether we apply the move
\vec{\rho}_{j_{1}}
or
\vec{\rho}_{-j_{1}^{\ast}}
, the resulting weight
f(1)
will be equal to
1
if
\bm{\gamma}^{(1)}
contains a curve between the dots in rows (equivalently, columns)
j_{1}
and
j_{1}+1
and to
0
otherwise.
∎

Definition 6.18
.

Let
\bm{\gamma}
be a monotone multicurve inside
\Gamma(u)
. We define
\phi(\bm{\gamma}):=[\mathbf{j},f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}]
, where
\mathbf{j}
is an arbitrary double reduced word for
w=w_{\circ}u
. By
Proposition

6.17
, the map
\phi
, from such monotone multicurves to double Lusztig datum, is well-defined.∎

Following
[
GLSBS22
]
, to a collection
\bm{\gamma}
of monotone curves we associate a
red projection cocharacter

\chi^{+}(\bm{\gamma})
defined as follows. Write
\bm{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{k})
, and for each index
r\in[k]
, let
i_{r},j_{r}
be such that
\gamma_{r}(0)=(u_{c}(i_{r}),i_{r})
and
\gamma_{r}(1)=(u_{c}(j_{r}),j_{r})
. We set

(6.4)

\chi^{+}(\bm{\gamma}):=\sum_{r=1}^{k}(\chi_{i_{r}}+\chi_{i_{r}+1}+\cdots+\chi_{j_{r}-1}).

In other words,
\chi^{+}(\bm{\gamma})
is the sum of simple coroots
\chi_{s}
with coefficients given by the number of times that the horizontal line at height
s+1/2
intersects a monotone curve in
\bm{\gamma}
.

Lemma 6.19
.

The cocharacter
\chi^{+}(\bm{\gamma})
coincides with the cocharacter
\chi(\phi(\bm{\gamma}))
defined in (
4.17
).

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on
\ell(w_{\circ})-\ell(u)
. For the base case
u=w_{\circ}
, the multicurve
\bm{\gamma}
must be empty, so
\chi^{+}(\bm{\gamma})=\chi(\phi(\bm{\gamma}))=0
.

For the induction step, let
\mathbf{j}=j_{1}j_{2}\cdots j_{\ell}
be a reduced word for
w=w_{\circ}u
, with all letters positive. Set
i:=j_{\ell}
and
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}:=\vec{\rho}_{i}(\bm{\gamma})
. Assume that the result is known for
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
. On the one hand, (
4.17
) implies

\chi(\phi(\bm{\gamma}))=f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}(\ell)\chi_{i}+s_{i}\cdot\chi(\phi(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})).

On the other hand, since
\vec{\rho}_{i}
acts by
s_{i}
on the vertical coordinates of the dots in
\Gamma(u)
, we obtain that
\chi^{+}(\bm{\gamma})
equals
s_{i}\cdot\chi^{+}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})
if the move
\vec{\rho}_{i}
was not special, and equals
\chi_{i}+s_{i}\cdot\chi^{+}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})
if
\vec{\rho}_{i}
was special. This completes the induction step.
∎

6.2.2.
Lusztig data under propagation moves

We continue to assume that
\bm{\gamma}
is a monotone multicurve inside
\Gamma(u)
and that
w=w_{\circ}u
, and recall the propagation moves from
[
GLSBS22
, Section 3.4]
, as illustrated in Figure
18
(A)
. We now prove
Lemma

6.22
, describing how the double Lusztig datum of a monotone multicurve, as introduced in
Definition

6.18
, changes when a propagation move is applied to the multicurve.

Suppose that
us_{i}>u
. Let the monotone multicurve
\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{i}(\bm{\gamma})
be obtained from
\bm{\gamma}
by applying one of the moves in Figure
18
(A)
. Again, exactly one of the moves applies for each
\bm{\gamma}
. Note that unlike in the case of dual propagation moves discussed above, the permutation diagram
\Gamma(u)
is unaffected by propagation moves. Let us now show that the action of the propagation moves on monotone multicurves translates under the map
\phi
from
Definition

6.18
into the action of the operators
e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}
on double Lusztig data,
i\in\pm I
, introduced in
Definition

6.20
below. These operators
e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}
have been studied in relation to string parametrizations of canonical bases; see e.g.
[
BZ93
, Equation (2.2)]
.

Suppose that
i\in\pm I
is such that
s_{i}^{-}us_{i}^{+}>u
, so
s_{i^{*}}^{-}ws_{i}^{+}<w
. Then, one can choose a double reduced word
\mathbf{j}=j_{1}j_{2}\cdots j_{\ell}
for
w
that ends with
j_{\ell}=i
.

Definition 6.20
.

Given a double Lusztig datum
[\mathbf{j},f]
with
\mathbf{j}
ending with
i
, we define

e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}[\mathbf{j},f]:=[\mathbf{j},f^{\prime}]

where for each
j\in[\ell]
the weighting
f^{\prime}
is set to be

f^{\prime}(j):=\begin{cases}f(j),&\text{if $j<\ell$,}\\
0,&\text{if $j=\ell$.}\end{cases}

∎

By
[
BZ93
, Theorem 2.2]
, the output Lusztig datum
e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}[\mathbf{j},f]
is well defined, i.e. it does not depend on the choice of
\mathbf{j}
as long as
\mathbf{j}
ends with
j_{\ell}=i
.
6
6

6

In
[
BZ93
]
, this result was only proved for the case when
i\in I
and all letters in
\mathbf{j}
are positive. The extension to double reduced words follows from
Remark

6.14
.

Remark 6.21
.

Consider the case of a single reduced word
\mathbf{j}
on positive letters. Then for
i\in I
, the operator
e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}
acts on
[\mathbf{j},f]
by setting
f(\ell)=0
as above (assuming
j_{\ell}=i
). For
i\in-I
, the operator
e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}
may be described as follows. One can apply braid and commutation moves to
(\mathbf{j},f)
until
\mathbf{j}
starts with
j_{1}=-i^{\ast}
, in which case
e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}
acts by setting
f(1)=0
. Indeed, this description can be obtained from the one above by applying move
(B4)
to make the first letter
j_{1}=i
of
\mathbf{j}
negative, then applying moves
(B1)
to make
i
the last letter of
\mathbf{j}
.∎

The change of Lusztig datum for monotone multicurves is precisely captured by the
e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}
operators in
Definition

6.20
:

Lemma 6.22
.

Let
\bm{\gamma}
be a monotone multicurve inside
\Gamma(u)
and
i\in\pm I
any index such that
s_{i}^{-}us_{i}^{+}>u
. Then the following equality holds:

(6.5)

\phi(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{i}(\bm{\gamma}))=e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}(\phi(\bm{\gamma})).

Proof.

Suppose that
i\in I
. Then the assumption
s_{i}^{-}us_{i}^{+}>u
translates into
us_{i}>u
. Choose a reduced word
\mathbf{j}=j_{1}j_{2}\cdots j_{\ell}
for
w
, with all letter positive and ending with
i
. Comparing the propagation moves
\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{i}
and their duals
\vec{\rho}_{i}
, in
Figure

18
(A) and (B), we obtain that
\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{i}(\bm{\gamma})\neq\bm{\gamma}
if and only if the move
\vec{\rho}_{i}(\bm{\gamma})
is special, which is equivalent to
f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}(\ell)=1
. Suppose that we are in this case. Let
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}:=\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{i}(\bm{\gamma})
and let
f^{\prime}:[\ell]\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be such that
e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}[\mathbf{j},f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}]=[\mathbf{j},f^{\prime}]
. Thus,
f^{\prime}
differs from
f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}
only in the value
f^{\prime}(\ell)=0
. Let
f^{\prime\prime}:=f_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\mathbf{j}}
. By definition,
f^{\prime\prime}(\ell)=0
since the move
\vec{\rho}_{i}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})
is non-special. Furthermore, we have
\vec{\rho}_{i}(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})=\vec{\rho}_{i}(\bm{\gamma})
, and thus
f^{\prime\prime}(j)=f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}(j)
for all
j\in[\ell-1]
. This implies that
f^{\prime}=f^{\prime\prime}
, which is the content of (
6.5
).

Suppose now that
\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{i}(\bm{\gamma})=\bm{\gamma}
, or equivalently,
f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}(\ell)=0
. Then we have
f_{\bm{\gamma},\mathbf{j}}=f_{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime},\mathbf{j}}
, so (
6.5
) holds in this case as well. The case
i\in-I
is handled similarly.
∎

6.2.3.
Comparing the cocharacters

We return to the setup of the rest of the paper. Let
\bbbeta=i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{n+m}\in(\pm I)^{n+m}
be a double braid word and
\ddot{\mathfrak{w}}(\bbbeta)
the associated double inductive weave.

Let
e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
and
c\in[n+m]
be such that
c>e
. On the one hand, from
Definition

4.29
, the path
P^{\downarrow}_{c}
gives rise to a reduced word
\mathbf{j}^{(c)}=j^{(c)}_{1}j^{(c)}_{2}\cdots j^{(c)}_{\ell}
for
w_{\overline{c}}
and to a Lusztig datum
[\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e}]
, where the weighting
\nu^{(c)}_{e}
is induced by the Lusztig cycle associated with the trivalent vertex located between depths
e
and
e+1
.

On the other hand, the main construction of
[
GLSBS22
]
gives rise to a monotone multicurve
\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})}
inside the permutation diagram
\Gamma(u_{\overline{c}})
, where
u_{\overline{c}}:=w_{\circ}w_{\overline{c}}
. It is defined inductively for
{\overline{c}}={\overline{e}}-1,{\overline{e}}-2,\dots,0
. Let
\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{e}}-1)}
consist of a single line segment connecting two dots located in rows
i_{{\overline{e}}},i_{{\overline{e}}}+1
, resp. columns
|i_{{\overline{e}}}|,|i_{{\overline{e}}}|+1
, depending on the sign of
i_{{\overline{e}}}
. Then, each
\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}}-1)}
is obtained from
\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})}
by either applying a propagation move
\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{i_{{\overline{c}}}}
if the crossing
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
is solid, or by applying an endpoint-preserving isotopy to
\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})}
that swaps two dots in adjacent rows
i_{{\overline{c}}},i_{{\overline{c}}}+1
, resp. columns
|i_{{\overline{c}}}|,|i_{{\overline{c}}}|+1
, and never allows a monotone curve to pass through a dot. In other words, such an isotopy is obtained by reversing a non-special dual propagation move shown in the bottom row of Figure
18
(B)
. See
[
GLSBS22
, Section 3.4]
for further details.

Our next result relates the weave Lusztig datum of
[
CGG+25
]
to the monotone multicurves of
[
GLSBS22
]
.

Proposition 6.23
.

For each
e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
and
c\in[n+m]
with
c>e
, we have

(6.6)

[\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e}]=\phi(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})}).

Proof.

Denote
\phi(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})})
by
[\mathbf{i}^{(c)},f^{(c)}_{e}]
. By construction,
\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})}
is a monotone multicurve inside
\Gamma(u_{\overline{c}})
with
u_{\overline{c}}=w_{\circ}w_{\overline{c}}
, and thus
\mathbf{i}^{(c)}
is a reduced word for
w_{\overline{c}}
. We need to show that the (double) weighted expressions
(\mathbf{i}^{(c)},f^{(c)}_{e})
and
(\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e})
are related by a sequence of double braid moves
(B1)
–
(B4)
.

Consider the base case
c-e=1
. If
i_{{\overline{e}}}\in I
(resp.
i_{{\overline{e}}}\in-I
) then
\nu^{(c)}_{e}(\ell)=1
(resp.
\nu^{(c)}_{e}(1)=1
) and the remaining entries of
\nu^{(c)}_{e}
are zero. Proceeding similarly to
Remark

6.21
, we apply double braid moves
(B4)
and
(B1)
to
(\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e})
to transform it into a double weighted expression
(\mathbf{j}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
such that
\mathbf{j}^{\prime}
ends with
j^{\prime}_{\ell}=i_{{\overline{e}}}
, and
\nu^{\prime}(\ell)=1
and
\nu^{\prime}(j)=0
for
j<\ell
. Since
\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{e}}-1)}
contains a single line segment between two dots in rows (resp. columns)
|i_{{\overline{e}}}|
and
|i_{{\overline{e}}}|+1
, we see that this line segment disappears after we apply the move
\vec{\rho}_{i_{{\overline{e}}}}
. Thus,
f_{\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{e}}-1)},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}=\nu^{\prime}
, which shows (
6.6
) in the base case.

For the induction step, suppose that the result is known for some
c>e
. Assume first that
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
is a solid crossing. Recall that
\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}}-1)}=\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\rho\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\rho\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{i_{{\overline{c}}}}(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})})
. On the one hand, applying
Lemma

6.22
and the induction hypothesis, we obtain the equalities

\phi(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}}-1)})=e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}(\phi(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})}))=e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}[\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e}].

On the other hand,
[\mathbf{j}^{(c+1)},\nu_{e}^{(c+1)}]
is obtained from
[\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e}]
as follows. We have
\mathbf{j}^{(c+1)}=\mathbf{j}^{(c)}
since the crossing
c
was solid. If
i_{{\overline{c}}}\in I
(resp.
i_{{\overline{c}}}\in-I
), we may assume that the last letter of
\mathbf{j}^{(c)}
is
i_{{\overline{c}}}
(resp. the first letter of
\mathbf{j}^{(c)}
is
-i_{{\overline{c}}}^{\ast}
). The weighting
\nu_{e}^{(c+1)}
is obtained from
\nu^{(c)}_{e}
by setting the last (resp. the first) entry to
0
in view of the third diagram in (
4.12
). By
Remark

6.21
, this operation agrees with the action of
e^{\operatorname{top}}_{i}
. This completes the induction step in the case
c\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
.

Assume now that
c\notin J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
is a hollow crossing. On the one hand, in this case
[\mathbf{j}^{(c+1)},\nu_{e}^{(c+1)}]
is obtained from
[\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e}]
by appending an extra letter
i_{{\overline{c}}}
with weight zero. On the other hand,
\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}}-1)}
is obtained from
\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})}
by an endpoint-preserving isotopy as above, so
\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})}=\vec{\rho}_{i_{{\overline{c}}}}(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}}-1)})
are related by a non-special move
\vec{\rho}_{i_{{\overline{c}}}}
. Thus, the last letter of each of
[\mathbf{j}^{(c+1)},\nu_{e}^{(c+1)}]
and
\phi(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})})
is
i_{{\overline{c}}}
with weight zero, which completes the induction step.
∎

We are ready to give a combinatorial proof of
4.33
in Lie Type A:

Corollary 6.24
.

For each
e\in J^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)}}
and
c\in[n+m]
with
c>e
, we have

\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,{\overline{c}},{\overline{e}}}=\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)},c,e}.

Proof.

Comparing
Definition

4.30
to
[
GLSBS22
, Lemma 7.13]
, we obtain the first equality
\gamma^{+}_{\bbbeta,{\overline{c}},{\overline{e}}}=\chi^{+}(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})})
, where
\chi^{+}(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})})
is the red projection cocharacter defined in
Equation

6.4
. By
Lemma

6.19
, we have
\chi^{+}(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})})=\chi(\phi(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})}))
. Then
Proposition

6.23
implies
\chi(\phi(\bm{\gamma}^{({\overline{e}},{\overline{c}})}))=\chi[\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e}]
, and the right hand side
\chi[\mathbf{j}^{(c)},\nu^{(c)}_{e}]
is precisely
\gamma^{\operatorname{W}}_{\bbbeta^{(-|+)},c,e}
by
Definition

4.29
.
∎

7.
Appendix A: Notation and conventions

In this section, we set up notation and collect preliminaries on flags and their relative positions that will be useful throughout the manuscript.

7.1.
Pinnings

We fix the following concepts and their notation for the entire article:

(1)

A simple algebraic group
\mathsf{G}
.

(2)

A pair of opposite Borel subgroups
\mathsf{B}:=\mathsf{B}_{+}
and
\mathsf{B}_{-}
of
\mathsf{G}
.

(3)

H=\mathsf{B}_{+}\cap\mathsf{B}_{-}
, a maximal torus of
\mathsf{G}
.

(4)

\mathsf{U}_{+}
, the unipotent radical of
\mathsf{B}_{+}
, so that we have a decomposition
\mathsf{B}_{+}=\mathsf{U}_{+}H=H\mathsf{U}_{+}
.

(5)

The Weyl group
W=N_{\mathsf{G}}(T)/T
, with simple reflections
s_{i}
,
i\in I
, where
I
is the set of vertices of the Dynkin diagram of
\mathsf{G}
. For each element
w\in W
, we fix a lift
\dot{w}\in\mathsf{G}
.

(6)

The longest element
w_{\circ}\in W
.

(7)

Simple roots
\alpha_{i}
,
i\in I
.

(8)

Fundamental weights
\omega_{i}
,
i\in I
.

We also fix a
pinning
of
(H,\mathsf{B}_{+},\mathsf{B}_{-},x_{i},y_{i};i\in I)
of
\mathsf{G}
, cf.
[
Lus94
, Section 1.1]
. That is, for each
i\in I
, we fix a group homomorphism

\phi_{i}:\operatorname{SL}_{2}\to G,\quad\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\mapsto x_{i}(t),\quad\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
t&1\end{pmatrix}\mapsto y_{i}(t),\quad\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\chi_{i}(t),

where
x_{i}(t),y_{i}(t)
are the exponentiated Chevalley generators and
\chi_{i}:\mathbb{C}^{*}\to H
is the simple coroot corresponding to
i\in I
. We set

\dot{s}_{i}:=\phi_{i}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

Note that this is consistent with the notation previously established, i.e.,
\dot{s}_{i}\in\mathsf{G}
is a lift of the simple root
s_{i}\in W
.

7.2.
Flags and weighted flags

By definition, the
flag variety
is the quotient
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}_{+}
. This is a projective algebraic variety and, since
\mathsf{B}_{+}
is self-normalizing, its elements can be identified with the Borel subgroups of
\mathsf{G}
. For each
w\in W
, we have an element
w.\mathsf{B}_{+}\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}_{+}
that does not depend on the specific lift of
w
to
\mathsf{G}
. Note that
\mathsf{B}_{-}=w_{\circ}.\mathsf{B}_{+}
, where
w_{\circ}\in W
is the longest element. By definition, the variety of
weighted flags
is the quotient
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U}_{+}
. It is a quasi-affine variety that admits a natural projection
\pi:\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{U}_{+}\longrightarrow\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}_{+}
.

7.2.1.
Relative position of flags

The
\mathsf{G}
-orbits in
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}_{+}\times\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}_{+}
are parametrized by the elements of the Weyl group
W
. More precisely, for each pair of elements
(\mathsf{B}_{1},\mathsf{B}_{2})\in\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}_{+}\times\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}_{+}
there exists an element
g\in\mathsf{G}
and an element
w\in W
such that

(g.\mathsf{B}_{1},g.\mathsf{B}_{2})=(\mathsf{B}_{+},w.\mathsf{B}_{+}).

The element
w\in W
is uniquely specified by the pair
(\mathsf{B}_{1},\mathsf{B}_{2})
and we say that
\mathsf{B}_{1},\mathsf{B}_{2}
are in position
w
. We write

\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{2}

to express the fact that
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
are in position
w
. The following lemma summarizes basic properties of relative position between two flags, cf. e.g.
[
CGG+25
, Section 3.2]
.

Lemma 7.1
.

The following properties hold:

(1)

\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{2}
if and only if
\mathsf{B}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w^{-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{1}
. In particular,
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{2}
if and only if
\mathsf{B}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{1}
.

(2)

Suppose
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{2}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w^{\prime}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{3}
. If
\ell(ww^{\prime})=\ell(w)+\ell(w^{\prime})
, then
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ww^{\prime}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{3}
.

(3)

\mathsf{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}^{\prime}
if and only if there exist a reduced decomposition
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{r}}
and flags
\mathsf{B}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{r-1}
such that

\mathsf{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{1}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{r-1}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{r-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i_{r}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}^{\prime}.

Moreover, the flags
\mathsf{B}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{B}_{r-1}
are unique.

(4)

Let
i\in I
. If
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{2}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{3}
, then either
\mathsf{B}_{1}=\mathsf{B}_{3}
or
\mathsf{B}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{B}_{3}
.∎

7.2.2.
Relative position of weighted flags

For weighted flags, the notion of relative position is a bit subtler. By definition, two weighted flags
\mathsf{U}_{1}
and
\mathsf{U}_{2}
are said to be in weak relative position
w
if there exist
g\in\mathsf{G}
and
h\in H
such that

(g.\mathsf{U}_{1},g.\mathsf{U}_{2})=(\mathsf{U}_{+},hw.\mathsf{U}_{+}).

As in the flag case, the element
w
is uniquely determined by
\mathsf{U}_{1}
and
\mathsf{U}_{2}
. We write
\mathsf{U}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\mathsf{U}_{2}
to express the fact that
\mathsf{U}_{1}
and
\mathsf{U}_{2}
are in
weak
relative position
w
. Note that

\mathsf{U}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\mathsf{U}_{2}\qquad\text{is equivalent to}\qquad\pi(\mathsf{U}_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi(\mathsf{U}_{2}).

By definition, two weighted flags
\mathsf{U}_{1}
,
\mathsf{U}_{2}
are said to be in
strong
relative position
w
if there exists
g\in G
such that

(g.\mathsf{U}_{1},g.\mathsf{U}_{2})=(\mathsf{U}_{+},w.\mathsf{U}_{+}),

and we write
\mathsf{U}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{U}_{2}
if this is the case. Note that
\mathsf{U}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{U}_{2}
implies
\mathsf{U}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\mathsf{U}_{2}
but the converse does not hold. In particular,
\mathsf{U}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{id}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{U}_{2}
if and only if
\mathsf{U}_{1}=\mathsf{U}_{2}
.

8.
Appendix B: Comparison between moves

The construction of the Deodhar cluster algebra structures on the coordinate rings
\mathbb{C}[R_{\bbbeta}]
of double braid varieties
R_{\bbbeta}
, as developed in
[
GLSBS22
,
GLSB23
]
, involves
double braid moves

\bbbeta\to\bbbeta^{\prime}
between double braid words
\bbbeta,\bbbeta^{\prime}
, and the corresponding isomorphisms
\phi_{\bbbeta,\bbbeta^{\prime}}:R(\bbbeta)\longrightarrow R(\bbbeta^{\prime})
between double braid varieties. Specifically,
[
GLSB23
, Section 4]
analyzes the relationship between the Deodhar cluster seed
\Sigma_{\bbbeta}^{\rm D}
for
\bbbeta
and the pullback
\phi_{\bbbeta,\bbbeta^{\prime}}^{*}(\Sigma_{\bbbeta^{\prime}}^{\rm D})
of the Deodhar cluster seed
\Sigma_{\bbbeta^{\prime}}^{\rm D}
for
\bbbeta^{\prime}
under the isomorphism
\phi_{\bbbeta,\bbbeta^{\prime}}
.

Double braid moves are also studied for weave cluster algebra structures, as developed in
[
CGG+25
]
, under the different guise of weave moves and double string moves, cf.
[
CGG+25
, Section 6.4]
. This short appendix presents a comparison of these moves: specifically,
Table

5
records the results in
[
GLSB23
]
and
[
CGG+25
]
related to such braid moves, as well as the relationship between
\Sigma_{\bbbeta}^{\rm D}
and
\phi_{\bbbeta,\bbbeta^{\prime}}(\Sigma_{\bbbeta^{\prime}}^{\rm D})
. The fact that these indeed match under the comparison is a consequence of the
Main Theorem
and the results developed in the main body of the article.

Table 5
.
The comparison between the double braid moves from
[
GLSB23
]
and the double inductive weave moves from
[
CGG+25
]
under the cluster algebra isomorphism established in the
Main Theorem
. By definition, a move is said to be
all solid
if all letters involved are solid. In this table, we also use
c
for the index of the rightmost letter in the move. The moves are written in black, the sections in
[
GLSB23
]
and
[
CGG+25
]
where the move is studied are written in
purple
, and the effect on the seed (which is equal on both sides) is written in
blue
.

Double braid move
[
GLSB23
]

Double string & weave move
[
CGG+25
]

(B1):
ij\leftrightarrow ji
if
{\rm sign}(i)\neq{\rm sign}(j)

\dots,iR,jL,\dots\leftrightarrow\dots,jL,iR,\dots

Sects. 4.1, 4.2

Theorem 6.8

Mutation at
c
if
special
(all solid &
w_{c}s_{|i|}=s_{|j|^{*}}w_{c}
),

else relabeling.

(B2):
ij\leftrightarrow ji
if
{\rm sign}(i)={\rm sign}(j)
,

Note that on the weave side, applying move (B1) or (B4) preserves the braid word but changes the double inductive weave, see also
